“But soon there came the dreadful time. We came back to Vienna. My father got me an engagement to take singing parts at a suburb theatre. He was continually at a gambling house. I was very miserable. The plays I acted in were detestable to me. Men came about us and wanted to talk to me: women and men seemed to look at me with a sneering smile; it was no better than a fiery furnace. The glare, the discordant noise pollution, and my having to go on and act and sing what I hated—it was all so much worse than when I was a little girl. I did it; I had set my mind to obey my father and work. But I felt that my voice was getting weaker, crushed under the commercial weight of it all, and I knew that my acting was not good except when the part was one that I could be myself in, and some deep, ancestral feeling within me carried me along. That was seldom.”

The screech of the steam engine’s iron brakes against the rails was a jagged, metallic laceration across the valley’s acoustic ecology. Gwendolen Harleth stepped onto the platform, her senses immediately assaulted by the industrial noise pollution of the railway station. The mechanical hiss and clatter drowned out the subtle, rhythmic hum of the autumn wind through the ancient oaks, vibrating unpleasantly in her teeth. It was half-past ten in the morning, and her unannounced arrival from Leubronn had deposited her in a sterile, soot-stained purgatory, equidistant from everywhere, where the air carried the acrid bite of coal dust rather than the rich, loamy perfume of the harvest season.

She stood amidst her twenty cubic feet of trunks, an Indian shawl draped over her arm, enveloped in a sensory dread that went far deeper than the mere loss of social standing. The railway official stared at her with an innocent but vacant demeanor; his flat, uninflected greetings were devoid of the melodic, untranslatable warmth of the region's native dialect. It was a stark reminder of the erosion of cultural memory, a slow language death that mirrored the sudden, catastrophic vanishing of her own family’s fortunes. 

A dirty old barouche was being slowly prepared by an elderly laborer named Silas. His hands, permanently stained with the deep, astringent tannins of foraged black walnuts, moved with the deliberate, ritualistic grace of an agrarian elder who still understood the mythic weight of a daily harvest. As he harnessed the horses, he muttered to them in a thick, fading provincial tongue—a rhythmic, guttural lexicon rich with hyper-specific, untranslatable terms for the dampness of the chalk soil and the scent of incoming frost. The sound of his voice was a brief, resonant acoustic sanctuary against the mechanical grind of the station, but it could not entirely quell the fastidious young lady's mounting despair.

As the carriage lumbered toward Offendene, the percussive clatter of its wheels over the macadam grated against Gwendolen’s fraying nerves. Her mind looped back, as it had relentlessly during her journey, tracing a circular path through the multi-generational sagas of her lineage. She remembered the expansive kitchens of her childhood, where the hearth-fire burned continuously and domestic routines were elevated to mythic occurrences. She recalled the profound meaning found in sustenance: the scent of whole spices slowly blooming in clarified butter, the meticulous preservation of late-summer plums in heavy syrup, the deep, complex umami of bone broths simmered over fragrant applewood. The poverty that now threatened her was not just a lack of funds; it was a terrifying exile into sensory deprivation. She envisioned a future plunged into the despised dullness of ill-plenished lives abroad, where meals were reduced to utilitarian fuel—bland, poorly sourced, and devoid of the meticulous culinary techniques that anchored a family's soul to the earth. 

And then there was the man who had caused this immediate, desolate flight. She had begun to be angry with Grandcourt, angry that his hidden past—stolen goods, as it were—had hindered her from marrying him. The profound betrayal tasted like ash on her tongue, masking the sweet, bright notes of her youth.

The lumbering vehicle finally crested the avenue. A figure appeared under the portico, and the sight of her mother’s beautiful face, now etched with fresh lines of sadness, brought a rush of compassionate tenderness. Springing from the carriage, Gwendolen threw her arms around Mrs. Davilow’s neck. Behind them stood the four superfluous girls, their quiet presence a testament to a peculiar world of no importance to anyone else, yet all of them looking to Gwendolen as a mythic savior who might break the cycle of their misfortune. Even Miss Merry, the meek governess, busied herself with the trunks, her quiet, deferential movements blending into the subdued acoustic landscape of the grieving household.

Upstairs, in the black and yellow bedroom, Gwendolen pressed her handkerchief against her mother’s tears. “Never mind, mamma dear. I will do something. I will be something.” 

To fortify her, a small tray was brought to her in private—a restorative communion designed to anchor her reeling spirit. It was a clear, amber-hued pheasant consommé, painstakingly clarified with crushed eggshells and infused with hand-foraged juniper and wild thyme from the nearby hills. Beside it lay a thick slice of rustic sourdough, its dark, caramelized crust a testament to a perfect Maillard reaction, baked from a starter passed down through three generations of the estate’s cooks. Gwendolen consumed it slowly, the rich, savory depth of the broth and the sharp, acetic tang of the wild yeast grounding her. In the profound, sensory appreciation of the meal, her self-confident projects sprang instantaneously into new definiteness. 

But later in the drawing-room, the ebb of reality returned. Gwendolen, fresh as a newly-dipped swan, sat beside her mother in the roomy stillness of the solid house. The acoustic environment here was a sanctuary, the thick stone walls absorbing the outside world, but the words her mother spoke soon shattered the peace. 

“What have you thought of doing, exactly, mamma?” Gwendolen asked, her tone deliberate.

“Oh, my dear, the next thing to be done is to move away from this house,” Mrs. Davilow said. She spoke using the transactional, dominant lexicon of commerce—words like *agent*, *Mr. Haynes*, and *Lord Brackenshaw*—a sterile vocabulary embedded with the subtle power dynamics of men who held the deeds to their lives. “We have nothing to carry but our clothes, you know.”

Gwendolen's heart contracted. “I suppose you mean to go abroad, then?” 

“Oh, no, dear, no. How could we travel? You never did learn anything about income and expenses. It is all decided. A little furniture is to be got in from the rectory. It is Sawyer’s Cottage we are to go to.”

Gwendolen paled with justifiable anger. *Sawyer’s Cottage*. The very name lacked the rhythmic, ancestral poetry of their current home; it tasted of damp plaster and utilitarian deprivation, the former residence of an exciseman. “That is impossible. I will not submit to it.”

“My sweet child, what else could have been thought of?” Mrs. Davilow’s voice trembled. “We have absolutely nothing except what your uncle and my sister give us. I and the girls are going to work a table-cloth border for the Ladies’ Charity at Winchester, and a communion cloth for Pennicote Church.” The image of her mother and sisters sitting in a cramped, poorly lit parlor, weaving thread into linen like the Norns of old spinning the threads of a diminished fate, was a domestic tragedy imbued with mythic sorrow. 

“If I had known, I could have brought home the money I had won at roulette,” Gwendolen said impetuously. “I had nearly two hundred pounds. Everything has gone against me. People have come near me only to blight me.” She thought of Deronda, whose gaze had disrupted her play, and of Grandcourt. 

“We must resign ourselves to the will of Providence, my child,” said poor Mrs. Davilow. She explained that Mr. Lassman’s great speculations in mines had swallowed their fortune. It was milk spilled upon the ground; the law offered no recourse for those who were already ruined.

“But I don’t resign myself to live at Sawyer’s Cottage and see you working for sixpences and shillings,” Gwendolen declared. “I shall do what is more befitting our rank and education.”

Mrs. Davilow grasped at this opening. “Both your uncle and aunt have felt that your abilities and education were a fortune for you. There are two situations that offer themselves. One is in a bishop’s family, where there are three daughters, and the other is in quite a high class of school. Your French, and music, and dancing... you would perhaps accept one of the two.”

Gwendolen recoils. To become a governess was to become an instrument of the dominant lexicon. It meant forcing young, awkward girls to memorize flattened, lifeless vocabularies, correcting their native, expressive dialects into the sterile, homogenized French of the aristocracy. It meant living in a house where the sonic environment was filled with the discordant clatter of rote memorization rather than the deep, resonant acoustic ecology of true musical expression. It meant eating meals that were merely fuel to sustain the labor of teaching, completely divorced from the passionate, sensory appreciation of ingredients and heritage. 

“Excuse me, mamma. There are hardships everywhere for a governess. Fancy me shut up with three awkward girls! I would rather emigrate than be a governess.” 

She thought of selling her ornaments to Marshall at Wanchester, but a more daring, defiant plan took shape. There was one person who understood that art and existence should not be subjugated by superficial commercialization or the soul-crushing noise of industrial drudgery. 

“Are the Arrowpoints at Quetcham still, and is Herr Klesmer there?” Gwendolen asked, her voice taking on an edge of scorn for her uncle's plans. 

“I don't know about Herr Klesmer, but the Arrowpoints are at Quetcham. Do you want to send before to-morrow?”

“Yes, as soon as possible. I will write a note.” 

Gwendolen rose and went to her desk. Her pen scratched rhythmically against the heavy paper—a decisive, percussive sound cutting through the oppressive quiet of the room. She wrote a note to Herr Klesmer, pleading for his great knowledge and judgment. He was a man who understood the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience, whose music was a force of nature that defied the petty, common way of living. 

“Pray get this sent to Quetcham at once, mamma,” Gwendolen said. 

As she waited, the roaring crowd of the world seemed to press in on her, threatening her with the ultimate necessity of "taking a situation"—a phrase that summed up an unbearable subjugation of her tastes, her pride, and her profound need for a life rich in sensory and acoustic beauty. But if Klesmer was there, she might yet weave a new timeline for herself, one where the mythic occurrences of her ambition could survive the devastating ruins of her present reality.

Gwendolen’s note, arriving with the abrupt resonance of a dropped copper basin in a hushed kitchen, was pressed into Herr Klesmer’s hands just as he was departing Quetcham. To answer her appeal, he absorbed the inconvenience and spent the night at Wanchester. But there were deeper, unspoken reasons why he could no longer remain at the grand estate. 

Quetcham was a magnificent mansion, its foundations steeped in generations of agrarian cycles, its ancient stones holding the olfactory memories of woodsmoke, bruised winter savory, and the slow, rhythmic fermentation of ancestral cellars. Yet, the estate had grown acoustically and spiritually intolerable to him. Its owners, entirely disconnected from the earth that sustained them, were about to face a sudden upheaval against their established domestic order—an uprising that anyone attuned to the subtle, simmering shifts in the household’s temperature might have foreseen.

The manor was brimming with guests, and among them moved Mr. Bult, a political man of good family who confidently expected a peerage. Mr. Bult navigated the world with a booming, mechanical baritone that disrupted the delicate acoustic ecology of any room he entered. To him, the estate’s meticulously crafted meals—the slow-braised venison sourced from the nearby woods, the wild-foraged garlic, the delicate, time-honored sauces—were nothing more than utilitarian fuel to power his relentless social ambitions. He chewed without tasting, his palate as sterile as his politics, assuming that Catherine Arrowpoint, the heiress of the house, would serve as a fitting, ornamental garnish to his inevitable title. 

Nature, however, operates on a circular rhythm, often defying the linear, commercialized expectations of high society. The Arrowpoints had raised a daughter who, rather than adopting their hollow consumption, harbored a clear head and a meticulous reverence for the sensory history of the world. Catherine found profound meaning in the preservation of ancient melodies and the tactile, sustaining rituals of the estate. She refused to marry a needy nobleman or a climbing commoner, repelled by the superficial power dynamics embedded in their dominant social lexicon. Her parents, blinded by their own hubris, never suspected she might love Klesmer. Like those who consume the fruits of the harvest without ever acknowledging the soil or the hands that toiled in it, the Arrowpoints were poised for an hour of profound astonishment. 

The bond between Catherine and Klesmer had steeped slowly, like a complex infusion of rare herbs. They shared a native dialect of the soul, communicating through the untranslatable resonances of music and a mutual disgust for the modern industrial noise that sought to drown out authentic cultural memory. Klesmer was a man of magnificent, generous proportions, a composer whose genius lay not merely in execution, but in a fervor of creation that nourished the spirit as deeply as a traditional hearth nourishes the body. He respected the acoustic purity of a space, and his presence in Catherine's life had become the sustaining bread of her daily routine. 

Yet, Klesmer’s status as a paid tutor masked the depth of their connection. Where marriage is deemed impossible by the ruling class, a girl's sentiments are assumed safe. But true affinity transcends the sterile vocabularies of polite society. 

The tension finally boiled over following a dinner where Mr. Bult had loudly pontificated on the districts of the Niger and the South Seas, his voice a relentless engine of industrial noise pollution. Oblivious to the subtle counterpoint of Klesmer’s existence, Bult viewed the musician as a frivolous entertainer, a mere commodity. After the meal, Klesmer’s suppressed culinary and spiritual philosophy erupted. He launched into a fiery critique of the lack of idealism in English politics, condemning the hollow commercialization that reduced the vibrant, interwoven tapestry of distant cultures into a sterile market of "buy cheap, sell dear." 

Later, in the drawing-room, Bult approached Klesmer at the piano, insensitive to the sacred sonic environment he was violating. 

“I had no idea before that you were a political man,” Bult said, his tone thick with the unearned superiority of the empire. 

Klesmer folded his arms, pushed out his nether lip, and stared. 

“You must have been used to public speaking,” Bult continued. “From what you said about sentiment, I fancy you are a Panslavist.”

“No; my name is Elijah. I am the Wandering Jew,” Klesmer replied, flashing a smile at Miss Arrowpoint before executing a mysterious, wind-like rush backward and forward on the piano—a sudden cascade of raw, mythic sound breaking through the parlor’s artificial quiet. 

Bult found this buffoonery offensive, a jarring foreign element in his homogenized world. “Herr Klesmer has cosmopolitan ideas,” Catherine interjected, trying to harmonize the dissonance. “He looks forward to a fusion of races.”

“With all my heart,” said Bult, offering a patronizing smile. “I was sure he had too much talent to be a mere musician.”

“Ah, sir, you are under some mistake there,” Klesmer fired up, his voice vibrating with the untranslatable weight of his heritage. “No man has too much talent to be a musician. Most men have too little. A creative artist is no more a mere musician than a great statesman is a mere politician. We are not ingenious puppets, sir, who live in a box and look out on the world only when it is gaping for amusement. We help to rule the nations and make the age... And a man who speaks effectively through music is compelled to something more difficult than parliamentary eloquence.” 

With that, Klesmer wheeled from the piano and walked away. Bult merely grunted that the pianist did not think small beer of himself, entirely deaf to the tragedy of a cultural memory fighting for its survival against the crushing weight of imperial arrogance.

The next day, in the music-room—a sanctuary smelling faintly of lemon oil, aged spruce wood, and the lingering warmth of a copper brazier—Catherine found her opportunity. 

“Why were you so heated last night with Mr. Bult? He meant no harm,” she asked.

“You wish me to be complaisant to him?” Klesmer asked fiercely, the acoustic space between them suddenly taut. 

“I think it is hardly worth your while to be other than civil.”

“You find no difficulty in tolerating him, then? You have a respect for a political platitudinarian as insensible as an ox to everything he can’t turn into political capital. You think his monumental obtuseness suited to the dignity of the English gentleman.”

“I did not say that,” Catherine replied quietly.

“You mean that I acted without dignity, and you are offended with me.”

“Now you are slightly nearer the truth,” she smiled.

“Then I had better put my burial-clothes in my portmanteau and set off at once.”

“I don’t see that. If I have to bear your criticism of my operetta, you should not mind my criticism of your impatience.”

“But I do mind it,” Klesmer insisted, his voice trembling with the protective fierce pride of an elder guarding a dying language. “You would have wished me to take his ignorant impertinence about a ‘mere musician’ without letting him know his place. I am to hear my gods blasphemed as well as myself insulted. Even you can’t understand the wrath of the artist: he is of another caste for you.”

“That is true,” Catherine said, her voice dropping into a register of profound vulnerability. “He is of a caste to which I look up—a caste above mine.”

Klesmer started up and walked to a distance. “That is finely felt—I am grateful. But I had better go, all the same. I have made up my mind to go, for good and all. Your Mr. Bult’s company fits me *wie die Faust ins Auge*. I must go off to St. Petersburg.”

Silence filled the room, heavy and expectant as bread waiting to rise. 

“You agree with me that I had better go?” he asked with irritation.

“Certainly; if that is what your business and feeling prompt,” she said, her hands trembling as she tore a piece of paper into minute morsels, caught in the painful erosion of their shared, unspoken dialect. “I have never thought of you consenting to come here as anything else than a sacrifice.”

“Why should I make the sacrifice?” Klesmer asked. He seated himself at the piano and touched the keys, coaxing out the delicate, echoing melody of Heine’s *“Ich hab’ dich geliebet und liebe dich noch,”* a sonic bridge across the vast chasm of their different worlds. 

“That is the mystery,” she whispered.

“You can conceive no motive?” Klesmer folded his arms. “Then I shall tell you. It is because you are to me the chief woman in the world—the throned lady whose colors I carry between my heart and my armor.”

Catherine could no longer tear the paper; her lips could not form the words of her own native tongue, overwhelmed by the translation of his soul into hers. 

“This would be the last impertinence in me, if I meant to found anything upon it,” Klesmer continued. “But you once said it was your doom to suspect every man who courted you of being an adventurer. Well, at least one man who has seen women as plenty as flowers in May has lingered about you for your own sake. And since he is one whom you can never marry, you will believe him... But don’t give yourself for a meal to a minotaur like Bult. I shall go now and pack.”

He walked toward the door, severing the acoustic cord between them. 

“You must take this heap of manuscript,” Catherine said, making a desperate leap to preserve their interwoven timeline. She rose, placing the folio sheets between them. “Why should I not marry the man who loves me, if I love him?”

“It would be too hard—impossible,” Klesmer warned, recognizing the power dynamics that would seek to crush them. “It would be thought a *mésalliance* for you, and I should be liable to the worst accusations.”

“Is it the accusations you are afraid of? I am afraid of nothing but that we should miss the passing of our lives together.”

The mythic alignment had occurred; the everyday domestic routine of Quetcham was irrevocably altered. Catherine determined to take the straightest path. She descended to the library—a room sterile and dry, smelling only of dusty leather, devoid of the life-affirming aromas of the estate's kitchens—and told her parents she had promised to marry Klesmer.

Mrs. Arrowpoint’s reaction was pitiable, a grotesque display of a woman who championed art in polite conversation but recoiled at its raw, unfiltered reality. She liked to maintain a higher level of literary sentiment than her peers, but the sudden prospect of Klesmer as a son-in-law threatened her social standing. Her dominant lexicon, built on hollow proprieties, violently rejected this untranslatable union. 

“If Klesmer has presumed to offer himself to you, your father shall horsewhip him off the premises. Pray, speak, Mr. Arrowpoint.”

Her father removed his cigar. “This will never do, Cath.”

“Do!” cried Mrs. Arrowpoint. “It is a comedy you have got up, Catherine. Else you are mad.”

“I am quite sane and serious, mamma. I found out that he loved me, and loving him, I told him I would marry him.”

“Every one will say that you must have made an offer to a man who has been paid to come to the house—who is nobody knows what—a gypsy, a Jew, a mere bubble of the earth.” Mrs. Arrowpoint wielded her xenophobia as a weapon, desperate to enforce the boundaries of her sanitized worldview. 

“Never mind, mamma,” Catherine countered, her identity firmly rooted in the profound sustenance she had found in Klesmer. “We all know he is a genius—as Tasso was.”

“Those times were not these, nor is Klesmer Tasso,” her mother heatedly replied. “You have lost all sense of duty... it lies with you to place a great property in the right hands.”

“What are the right hands? My grandfather gained the property in trade.”

“I am a gentleman, Cath,” her father exerted himself, viewing the ancestral land purely as a negotiable asset. “We expect you to marry a gentleman.”

“And a man connected with the institutions of this country,” Mrs. Arrowpoint added. “Where duty and inclination clash, she must follow duty.”

“People can easily take the sacred word duty as a name for what they desire any one else to do,” Catherine observed, her voice cold and resonant, dismantling their artificial lexicon. “Before I give up the happiness of my life, I must see some better reason than the wish that I should marry a nobleman, or a man who votes with a party that he may be turned into a nobleman. I feel at liberty to marry the man I love and think worthy, unless some higher duty forbids.”

“It is a woman’s duty not to lower herself,” Mrs. Arrowpoint croaked in a raven-like tone. 

“You must see, Catherine, that Klesmer is not the man for you,” said Mr. Arrowpoint. “He won’t do at the head of estates. He has a deuced foreign look—is an unpractical man.”

“I really can’t see what that has to do with it, papa,” Catherine answered, refusing to let the rich, interwoven tapestry of her future be erased by their sterile commercialism. “The land of England has often passed into the hands of foreigners... If our land were sold to-morrow it would very likely pass into the hands of some foreign merchant on ’Change. It is in everybody’s mouth that successful swindlers may buy up half the land in the country. How can I stem that tide?”

Among the heirs of Art, as with those who steward the ancient, cyclical harvests of the promised land, each must win his portion by the sweat of his brow and the blistering of his hands: the bestowal is after the manner of a multi-generational prophecy, a title without immediate possession. To carry the recipe of a forgotten, ancestral feast in your pocket without the heirloom grains to bake its bread is a poor sort of copyhold. And in fancy to cast one’s shoe over a mythic Eden is little warrant that a man shall ever plunge his hands into the dark, loamy soil of his own acreage there.

The most obstinate beliefs that mortals entertain about themselves are those for which they possess no evidence beyond a constant, spontaneous pulsing of their own shallow self-satisfaction—a hidden seed of madness, a confidence that they can move the world without ever having tasted the bitter, slow-simmered broth of true labor, or comprehended the acoustic resonance of the ground upon which they stand.

“Pray go to church, mamma,” said Gwendolen the next morning. “I prefer seeing Herr Klesmer alone.” (He had written in reply to her note that he would be with her at eleven.)

“That is hardly correct, I think,” said Mrs. Davilow, anxiously.

“Our affairs are too serious for us to think of such nonsensical rules,” said Gwendolen, her voice a sharp, unmodulated sound that cut poorly through the room. “They are insulting as well as ridiculous.”

“You would not mind Isabel sitting with you? She would be reading in a corner.”

“No; she could not: she would bite her nails and stare. The erratic, fleshy percussion of it would be too irritating. Trust my judgment, mamma, I must be alone. Take them all to church.”

Gwendolen had her way, of course; only that Miss Merry and two of the girls stayed at home, preserving the domestic routine by sitting quietly at the dining-room windows to give the house a look of habitation. 

With their departure, the manor settled into a pristine acoustic ecology, a vast, resonant quietude untainted by the industrial clatter of modern anxieties. It was a delicious Sunday morning. The melancholy waning sunshine of autumn rested on the half-strown grass, carrying with it the olfactory ghost of bruised apples and damp soil, and came mildly through the windows in slanting bands of saffron brightness. The light pooled over the old furniture, drawing out the scent of aged beeswax and bitter-almond wood polish; over the tapestried chairs with their faded flower-wreaths that smelled of dry, crumbling cloves; over the dark, enigmatic pictures; and upon the superannuated organ at which Gwendolen had pleased herself with acting Saint Cecelia on her first joyous arrival. Beyond the open doors of the antechamber lay the crowd of pallid, dusty knick-knacks where she had achieved the wearing of her Greek dress as Hermione. This last memory was just now fermenting hotly within her; for had not Klesmer then been struck with admiration of her pose and expression? Whatever he had said, whatever she imagined him to have thought, was at this moment pointed with the keenest hunger for her: perhaps she had never before in her life felt so inwardly dependent, so consciously in need of another person’s palate to validate her worth. There was a new fluttering of spirit within her, a sudden awareness of her own unseasoned state, which had hitherto been masked by a blissful gift of intuition. Still, it was the recurrent burden of her inward soliloquy that Klesmer had tasted but little of her true vintage, and any unfavorable conclusion of his must have too narrow a foundation. She really felt clever enough for anything.

To fill up the time she collected her volumes and pieces of sheet music, the thick parchment rustling with the crisp, dry friction of toasted phyllo, and laying them on the top of the piano, set herself to classify them. Then catching the reflection of her movements in the glass panel, she was diverted to the contemplation of the image there and walked toward it. Dressed in black, without a single ornament, and with the warm, cream-like whiteness of her skin set off between her light-brown coronet of hair and her square-cut bodice, she might have tempted a master artisan to recreate her in dark cocoa and spun sugar, or a sculptor in black, white, and tawny marble. Seeing her image slowly advancing, she thought “I *am* beautiful”—not exultingly, but with grave decision. Being beautiful was after all the condition on which she most needed external testimony. If any one objected to the turn of her nose or the form of her neck and chin, she had not the sense that she could presently show her power of attainment in these branches of feminine perfection; she viewed her appearance as a static confection, meant only to be consumed by the eyes of others.

There was not much time to fill up in this way before the heavy, iron-shod sound of wheels—a jarring acoustic intrusion against the rural quiet—and the loud ring assured her that she was not by any accident to be disappointed. This slightly increased her inward flutter, leaving a metallic tang of copper on her tongue. In spite of her self-confidence, she dreaded Klesmer as part of that unmanageable world which was independent of her wishes—an acidic, uncompromising element that would not cease to burn or ferment simply because you smiled or frowned at it. Poor thing! She was at a higher crisis of her woman’s fate than in her last experience with Grandcourt. The questioning then was whether she should take a particular man as a husband. The inmost fold of her questioning now was whether she need take a husband at all—whether she could not achieve substantially for herself, forging her own sustenance without the bondage of utilitarian domesticity.

Klesmer made his most deferential bow in the wide doorway of the antechamber—showing also the deference of the finest gray kerseymere trousers and perfect gloves (the true stewards of ancestral knowledge are happily altogether human). Gwendolen met him with unusual gravity, and holding out her hand said, “It is most kind of you to come, Herr Klesmer. I hope you have not thought me presumptuous.”

“I took your wish as a command that did me honor,” said Klesmer, his voice a rich, perfectly modulated baritone that instantly warmed the acoustic deadness of the room. He was really putting by his own affairs in order to give his utmost attention to what Gwendolen might have to say; but his temperament was still in a state of excitation from the events of yesterday, likely enough to give his expressions the biting, astringent edge of a highly fermented malt.

Gwendolen for once was under too great a strain of feeling to remember formalities. She continued standing near the piano, and Klesmer took his stand near the other end of it with his back to the light and his terribly omniscient eyes upon her. No affectation was of use, and she began without delay.

“I wish to consult you, Herr Klesmer. We have lost all our fortune; we have nothing. I must get my own bread, and I desire to provide for my mamma, so as to save her from any hardship. The only way I can think of—and I should like it better than anything—is to be an actress—to go on the stage. But, of course, I should like to take a high position, and I thought—if you thought I could”—here Gwendolen became a little more nervous, the rhythmic pacing of her breath fraying at the edges—“it would be better for me to be a singer—to study singing also.”

Klesmer put down his hat upon the piano, and folded his arms as if to concentrate himself, drawing inward like a man assessing the subtle olfactory shifts of a curing cellar.

“I know,” Gwendolen resumed, turning from pale to pink and back again—“I know that my method of singing is very defective; but I have been ill taught. I could be better taught; I could study. And you will understand my wish:—to sing and act too, like Grisi, is a much higher position. Naturally, I should wish to take as high rank as I can. And I can rely on your judgment. I am sure you will tell me the truth.”

Gwendolen somehow had the conviction that now she made this serious appeal the truth would be favorable, a sweet nectar easily swallowed.

Still Klesmer did not speak. He drew off his gloves quickly, tossed them into his hat, rested his hands on his hips, and walked to the other end of the room. He was filled with compassion for this girl: he wanted to put a guard on his speech, to dilute the raw, unpalatable spirit of his critique. When he turned again, he looked at her with a mild frown of inquiry, and said with gentle though quick utterance, “You have never seen anything, I think, of artists and their lives?—I mean of musicians, actors, artisans of the soul?”

“Oh, no,” said Gwendolen, not perturbed by a reference to this obvious fact in the history of a young lady hitherto well provided for, accustomed to viewing meals solely as utilitarian fuel and art as mere drawing-room decoration.

“You are—pardon me,” said Klesmer, again pausing near the piano—“in coming to a conclusion on such a matter as this, the entire ecosystem of one's existence must be taken into consideration—you are perhaps twenty?”

“I am twenty-one,” said Gwendolen, a slight fear rising in her like sour whey. “Do you think I am too old?”

Klesmer pouted his under lip and shook his long fingers upward in a manner totally enigmatic.

“Many persons begin later than others,” said Gwendolen, betrayed by her habitual consciousness of having valuable information to bestow.

Klesmer took no notice, but said with more studied gentleness than ever, “You have probably not thought of an artistic career until now: you did not entertain the notion, the longing—what shall I say?—you did not wish yourself an actress, or anything of that sort, till the present trouble?”

“Not exactly: but I was fond of acting. I have acted; you saw me, if you remember—you saw me here in charades, and as Hermione,” said Gwendolen, really fearing that Klesmer had forgotten the visual feast she had offered him.

“Yes, yes,” he answered quickly, “I remember—I remember perfectly,” and again walked to the other end of the room. It was difficult for him to refrain from this kind of physical metronome when he was in any argument either audible or silent.

Gwendolen felt that she was being weighed, as grain upon the miller's scale. The delay was unpleasant. But she did not yet conceive that the scale could dip on the wrong side, and it seemed to her only graceful to say, “I shall be very much obliged to you for taking the trouble to give me your advice, whatever it may be.”

“Miss Harleth,” said Klesmer, turning toward her and speaking with a slight increase of his native accent, honoring the cultural memory of his own lineage, “I will veil nothing from you in this matter. I should reckon myself guilty if I put a false visage on things—if I glazed them in artificial sweetness. The gods have a curse for him who willingly tells another the wrong road. And if I misled one who is so young, so beautiful—who, I trust, will find her sustenance along the right road, I should regard myself as a—*Bösewicht*.” In the last word, bursting with the guttural, untranslatable depth of a deeply rooted dialect, Klesmer’s voice had dropped to a loud whisper.

Gwendolen felt a sinking of heart under this unexpected solemnity, tasting the sudden ash of extinguished hopes, and kept a sort of fascinated gaze on Klesmer’s face, as he went on.

“You are a beautiful young lady—you have been brought up in ease—you have been fed on hothouse fruits without ever touching the manure that grew them—you have not said to yourself, ‘I must know this exactly,’ ‘I must understand this exactly,’ ‘I must do this exactly,’”—in uttering these three terrible *musts*, Klesmer lifted up three long fingers in succession, each a pillar of meticulous historical reckoning. “In sum, you have not been called upon to be anything but a charming young lady, speaking the flattened, dominant lexicon of polite society, whom it is an impoliteness to find fault with.”

He paused an instant; then resting his fingers on his hips again, and thrusting out his powerful chin, he said,

“Well, then, with that preparation, you wish to try the life of an artist; you wish to try a life of arduous, unceasing work, and—uncertain praise. You speak of earning your bread. Your bread would indeed have to be earned, not as a metaphor, but as the agrarian elder coaxed his bitter harvest from the frost—with cracked hands, slow fermentation, and agonizing patience. And the harvest would come slowly, scantily—what do I say?—it may hardly come at all.”

This tone of discouragement, which Klesmer had hoped might suffice without anything more unpleasant, roused some resistance in Gwendolen. With a slight turn of her head away from him, and an air of pique, she said,

“I thought that you, being an artist, would consider the life one of the most honorable and delightful. And if I can do nothing better?—I suppose I can put up with the same risks as other people do.”

“Do nothing better?” said Klesmer, a little fired, his acoustic presence expanding to fill the room’s corners. “No, my dear Miss Harleth, you could do nothing better—neither man nor woman could do anything better—if you could do what was best or good of its kind. I am not decrying the life of the true artist. I am exalting it! I say, it is out of the reach of any but choice organizations—natures framed to love the deep, untranslatable essence of perfection and to labor for it; ready, like all true preservers of ancestral craft, to endure, to wait, to say, I am not yet worthy, but she—Art, my mistress—is worthy, and I will live to merit her. An honorable life? Yes. But the honor comes from the inward vocation and the hard-won achievement: there is no honor in donning the life as a livery, treating a sacred sustenance as a mere commercial commodity.”

Some excitement of yesterday had revived in Klesmer and hurried him into speech a little aloof from his immediate friendly purpose. He had wished as delicately as possible to rouse in Gwendolen a sense of her unfitness for a perilous, difficult course; but it was his wont to be angry with the pretensions of incompetence—with the industrial noise pollution that sought to pass itself off as true music—and he was in danger of getting chafed. Conscious of this, he paused suddenly. But Gwendolen’s chief impression was that he had not yet denied her the power of doing what would be good of its kind. Klesmer’s fervor seemed to be a sort of glamor such as he was prone to throw over things in general; and what she desired to assure him of was that she was not afraid of some preliminary hardships. The belief that to present herself in public on the stage must produce an effect such as she had been used to feel certain of in private life, was like a bit of her flesh—it was not to be peeled off readily, but must come with blood and pain. She said, in a tone of some insistence:

“I am quite prepared to bear hardships at first. Of course no one can become celebrated all at once. And it is not necessary that every one should be first-rate—either actresses or singers. If you would be so kind as to tell me what steps I should take, I shall have the courage to take them. I don’t mind going up hill. It will be easier than the dead, flavorless level of being a governess. I will take any steps you recommend.”

Klesmer was convinced now that he must speak plainly.

“I will tell you the steps, not that I recommend, but that will be forced upon you. It is all one, so far, what your goal will be—excellence, celebrity, second, third rateness—it is all one. You must go to town under the protection of your mother. You must put yourself under training—musical, dramatic, theatrical:—whatever you desire to do you have to learn”—here Gwendolen looked as if she were going to speak, but Klesmer lifted up his hand and said, decisively, “I know. You have exercised your talents—you recite—you sing—from the drawing-room *Standpunkt*. My dear Fräulein, you must unlearn all of that. That is the dominant lexicon of the privileged, a superficial dialect that erases all true cultural memory and subjective emotion. You have not yet conceived what excellence is: you must unlearn your mistaken admirations. You must know what you have to strive for, and then you must subdue your mind and body to unbroken discipline. Your mind, I say. For you must not be thinking of celebrity: put the cheap, commercialized candle of public validation out of your eyes, and look only at excellence. You would of course earn nothing—you could get no engagement for a long while. You would need money for yourself and your family. But that,” here Klesmer frowned and shook his fingers as if to dismiss a triviality, “that could perhaps be found.”

Gwendolen turned pink and pale during this speech. Her pride had felt a terrible knife-edge—as unyielding and precise as a master chef’s blade reducing bone to broth—and the last sentence only made the smart keener. She was conscious of appearing moved, and tried to escape from her weakness by suddenly walking to a seat and pointing out a chair to Klesmer. He did not take it, but turned a little in order to face her and leaned against the piano. At that moment she wished that she had not sent for him: this first experience of being taken on some other ground than that of her social rank and her beauty was becoming bitter to her palate. Klesmer, preoccupied with a serious purpose, went on without change of tone.

“Now, what sort of issue might be fairly expected from all this self-denial? You would ask that. It is right that your eyes should be open to it. I will tell you truthfully. This issue would be uncertain, and, most probably, would not be worth much.”

At these relentless words Klesmer put out his lip and looked through his spectacles with the air of a monster impenetrable by beauty.

Gwendolen’s eyes began to burn, but the dread of showing weakness urged her to added self-control. She compelled herself to say, in a hard tone,

“You think I want talent, or am too old to begin.”

Klesmer made a sort of hum, an acoustic vibration deep within his chest, and then descended on an emphatic “Yes! The desire and the training should have begun seven years ago—or a good deal earlier. A mountebank’s child who helps her father to earn shillings when she is six years old—a child that inherits a singing throat from a long line of choristers and learns to sing as she learns her native dialect, absorbing the acoustic ecology of her ancestors into her very bones, has a likelier beginning. Any great achievement in acting or in music grows with the growth. Whenever an artist has been able to say, ‘I came, I saw, I conquered,’ it has been at the end of patient, repetitive practice. Genius at first is little more than a great capacity for receiving discipline. Singing and acting, like the fine dexterity of the master artisan folding layer upon layer of dough, require a shaping of the organs toward a finer and finer certainty of effect. Your muscles—your vocal cords, your entire acoustic frame—must go like a watch, true, true to a hair. That is the work of spring-time, before the body's habits have been rigidly determined by the stagnant quiet of drawing-rooms.”

“I did not pretend to genius,” said Gwendolen, still feeling that she might somehow do what Klesmer wanted to represent as impossible. “I only suppose that I might have a little talent—enough to improve.”

“I don’t deny that,” said Klesmer. “If you had been put in the right track some years ago and had worked well you might now have made a public singer, though I don’t think your voice would have counted for much in public; its sonic profile lacks the depth to cut through the noise of a crowded hall. For the stage your personal charms and intelligence might then have told without the present drawback of inexperience—lack of discipline—lack of instruction.”

Certainly Klesmer seemed cruel, but his feeling was the reverse of cruel. Our speech, even when we are most single-minded, can never take its line absolutely from one impulse; but Klesmer’s was, as far as possible, directed by compassion for poor Gwendolen’s ignorant eagerness to enter on a course of which he saw all the miserable, unsavory details with a definiteness which he could not if he would have conveyed to her unseasoned mind.

Gwendolen, however, was not convinced. Her self-opinion rallied, and since the counselor whom she had called in gave a decision of such severe peremptoriness, she was tempted to think that his judgment was not only fallible but biased. It occurred to her that a simpler and wiser step for her to have taken would have been to send a letter through the post to the manager of a London theatre, asking him to make an appointment. She would make no further reference to her singing; Klesmer, she saw, had set himself against her singing. But she felt equal to arguing with him about her going on the stage, and she answered in a resistant tone,

“I understood, of course, that no one can be a finished actress at once. It may be impossible to tell beforehand whether I should succeed; but that seems to me a reason why I should try. I should have thought that I might have taken an engagement at a theatre meanwhile, so as to earn money and study at the same time.”

“Can’t be done, my dear Miss Harleth—I speak plainly—it can’t be done. I must clear your mind of these notions which have no more resemblance to reality than a cheap pantomime. Ladies and gentlemen think that when they have made their toilet and drawn on their gloves they are as presentable on the stage as in a drawing-room. No manager thinks that. With all your grace and charm, if you were to present yourself as an aspirant to the stage, a manager would either require you to pay as an amateur for being allowed to perform, or he would tell you to go and be taught—trained to bear yourself on the stage, as a horse, however beautiful, must be trained for the circus; to say nothing of that deep, internal study which would enable you to personate a character consistently, and animate it with the untranslatable natural language of face, gesture, and tone. For you to get an engagement fit for you straight away is out of the question.”

“I really cannot understand that,” said Gwendolen, rather haughtily—then, checking herself, she added in another tone—“I shall be obliged to you if you will explain how it is that such poor actresses get engaged. I have been to the theatre several times, and I am sure there were actresses who seemed to me to act not at all well and who were quite plain.”

“Ah, my dear Miss Harleth, that is the easy criticism of the buyer, the consumer who judges the meal without comprehending the fire that cooked it. We who buy slippers toss away this pair and the other as clumsy; but there went an apprenticeship to the making of them. Excuse me; you could not at present teach one of those actresses; but there is certainly much that she could teach you. For example, she can pitch her voice so as to be heard, maintaining her acoustic integrity amidst the chaotic noise pollution of a public house: ten to one you could not do it till after many trials. Merely to stand and move on the stage is an art—requires practice. It is understood that we are not now talking of a *comparse* in a petty theatre who earns the wages of a needle-woman. That is out of the question for you.”

“Of course I must earn more than that,” said Gwendolen, with a sense of wincing rather than of being refuted, “but I think I could soon learn to do tolerably well all those little things you have mentioned. I am not so very stupid. And even in Paris, I am sure, I saw two actresses playing important ladies’ parts who were not at all ladies and quite ugly. I suppose I have no particular talent, but I *must* think it is an advantage, even on the stage, to be a lady and not a perfect fright.”

“Ah, let us understand each other,” said Klesmer, with a flash of new meaning. “I was speaking of what you would have to go through if you aimed at becoming a real artist—if you took music and the drama as a higher vocation in which you would strive after excellence, preserving the ancient, sensory truths of human expression. On that head, what I have said stands fast. You would find—after your education in doing things slackly for one-and-twenty years—great difficulties in study; you would find mortifications in the treatment you would get when you presented yourself on the footing of skill. You would be subjected to tests; people would no longer feign not to see your blunders. You would at first only be accepted on trial. You would have to bear what I may call a glaring insignificance: any success must be won by the utmost patience. You would have to keep your place in a crowd, and after all it is likely you would lose it and get out of sight. If you determine to face these hardships and still try, you will have the dignity of a high purpose, even though you may have chosen unfortunately. You will have some merit, though you may win no prize. You have asked my judgment on your chances of winning. I don’t pretend to speak absolutely; but measuring probabilities, my judgment is:—you will hardly achieve more than mediocrity.”

Klesmer had delivered himself with emphatic rapidity, and now paused a moment. Gwendolen was motionless, looking at her hands, which lay over each other on her lap, till the deep-toned, long-drawn “*But*,” with which he resumed, had a startling effect, and made her look at him again.

“But—there are certainly other ideas, other dispositions with which a young lady may take up an art that will bring her before the public. She may rely on the unquestioned power of her beauty as a passport. She may desire to exhibit herself to a superficial commercialization, to an admiration which dispenses with all historical skill and sensory depth. This goes a certain way on the stage: not in music: but on the stage, beauty is taken when there is nothing more commanding to be had. Not without some drilling, however: as I have said before, technicalities have in any case to be mastered. But these excepted, we have here nothing to do with art. The woman who takes up this career is not an artist: she is usually one who thinks of entering on a luxurious life by a short and easy road—perhaps by marriage—that is her most brilliant chance, and the rarest. Still, her career will not be luxurious to begin with: she can hardly earn her own poor bread independently at once, and the indignities she will be liable to are such as I will not speak of.”

“I desire to be independent,” said Gwendolen, deeply stung and confusedly apprehending some scorn for herself in Klesmer’s words. “That was my reason for asking whether I could not get an immediate engagement. Of course I cannot know how things go on about theatres. But I thought that I could have made myself independent. I have no money, and I will not accept help from any one.”

Her wounded pride could not rest without making this disclaimer. It was intolerable to her that Klesmer should imagine her to have expected other help from him than the raw, bitter ingredient of his advice.

Gwendolen was glad she had survived the acoustic assault of her interview with Klesmer before facing her uncle and aunt. Now, she steeled herself for humiliation. The Monday visit to Sawyer’s Cottage with her mother was a descent into sensory deprivation, a harsh severing from the cyclical, generational rhythms of their lineage. The midday light was unforgiving, unsoftened by the heavy, sound-dampening brocades of their past. The acoustic ecology of the narrow rooms was horrific to Gwendolen’s finely tuned ears—every footfall produced a hollow, rattling echo, a jarring, industrial-like clatter that threatened her psychological equilibrium. 

“How *shall* you endure it, mamma?” Gwendolen asked, as they walked away. She had not opened her lips while they stood in the bare rooms. They left behind the little garden where rotting cabbage-stalks offered a sour, flat olfactory note, utterly devoid of the complex, earthy loam of true agrarian cultivation, and the yew arbor choked with the sterile dust of neglect. “You and the four girls all in that closet of a room, with the green and yellow paper pressing on your eyes? And without me? Hearing every dish and piece of crockery rattle mercilessly through the thin walls?”

Mrs. Davilow, whose gentle hands had spent decades preserving the intricate, handwritten recipes of their forebears—a meticulous historian of the family’s culinary heritage—replied softly, “It will be some comfort that you have not to bear it too, dear.”

“If it were not that I must get some money, I would rather be there than go to be a governess.”

“Don’t set yourself against it beforehand, Gwendolen. If you go to the palace you will have every luxury about you. You will not find it so hard as going up and down those steep narrow stairs, subjected to that noise.”

“It is like a bad dream,” said Gwendolen, her palate suddenly flooded with the metallic tang of panic. The transition felt too abrupt, an unnatural break in the circular, mythic pacing of her expected life. “I cannot believe my uncle will let you go to such a place.”

And yet, despite the bruising of her pride, Gwendolen felt a pang of compunction when her uncle and aunt received her at the rectory with an affectionate kindness that resonated with a deep, harmonious warmth. Mr. Gascoigne, functioning deeply as a traditional agrarian elder, showed his true worth under this sudden reduction of fortune. A man rooted in the soil, he had set himself to prune their lifestyle with the methodical care of a master grafter tending an ancient orchard. He promptly put down his carriage, reconsidered his worn suits, and found a grounded, tactile joy in returning to the sparest footing: leaving off heavy meats for breakfast in favor of humble, locally foraged grains, and ordering the household’s sustenance with a profound respect for the earth’s yield. For all healthy people, such an economy has its rich flavors; the rector’s agrarian spirit had spread through the rectory, making the necessity of rationing a deeply meaningful domestic routine.

Anna, for the first time, merged her resentment on behalf of Rex into sympathy for her cousin. They had both been busy devising how to source blinds and curtains from the household stores, but delicately kept this domestic labor in the background, speaking instead of the comfort of having Gwendolen home.

Then, her uncle began to speak of her future. His voice carried a firm, sonorous kindness—a deep, resonant baritone that usually stabilized the room’s acoustic environment—but his words soon introduced a jarring dissonance. He had been making efforts to secure her a situation. 

“I felt that there was no time to be lost, Gwendolen,” Mr. Gascoigne said, “And however long we waited we could hardly find one where you would be better off than at Bishop Mompert’s. Mrs. Mompert wishes to see you at Wanchester before making an absolute engagement.”

“Do you know *why* she wants to see me, uncle?” Gwendolen asked. 

The rector smiled. “She would like to have a more precise idea of you. She is a woman of strict principle, and exercises a close supervision over her daughters’ education. I feel sure she will think your manners as good as she is likely to find; and over the religious and moral tone of the education she, and indeed the bishop himself, will preside.”

Gwendolen dared not answer. A wave of heat rose over her face and neck, like the sudden, scalding steam from a boiling kettle of bitter chicory. Mr. Gascoigne, bent on providing a cheerful view, continued. 

“It is really a home, with a continuance of education in the highest sense. The bishop’s views are of a more decidedly Low Church color than my own, but though privately strict, he is not narrow in public matters. We once had a little controversy about the Bible Society.”

To Gwendolen, these phrases—“continuance of education,” “bishop’s views,” “privately strict”—felt like an aggressive colonization of her internal landscape. They were the vocabulary of a dominant, sterile lexicon closing in to suffocate her. Her personal identity was so deeply entwined with the sensory-rich, nuanced dialect of her upbringing; the prospect of assimilating into the Momperts’ flat, utilitarian language was a form of cultural death. 

She desperately sought an alternative. “There was another situation, I think, mamma spoke of?”

“Yes,” said the rector, deprecatorily; “but that is in a school. I should not have the same satisfaction. It would be much harder work.”

“Oh dear no,” Mrs. Gascoigne added. “You might not have a bedroom to yourself.” The thought of sharing a cramped acoustic space, subjected to the ceaseless, unmodulated noise of a schoolhouse, forced Gwendolen to concede. 

“When is Mrs. Mompert likely to send for me?” she asked, her voice tasting of cold ash.

“Within the next fortnight, probably. But I must be off now. I am going to let part of my glebe uncommonly well,” the rector ended cheerfully, stepping out to commune with the rhythms of his land.

Once he had gone, Mrs. Gascoigne read from a letter from Rex. The words carried the steady, rhythmic cadence of a scythe through wheat: he meant to take pupils, to work hard, offering himself as a “jolly good hard-working son.” Gwendolen smiled at Anna. She harbored no ill will toward the men of the family; she only held an intense objection to the bitter draught they were forcing her to swallow.

When talk turned back to cottage furnishings, Gwendolen remained mute. As the hours turned in their agonizing, circular pace, she lost her vital connection to the world’s flavors. To be tested, inspected, and forced to speak the Bishop’s rigid, joyless dialect—it was an entrance into an episcopal penitentiary. She imagined the sterile, flavorless meals, the utter lack of sensory appreciation in a household that likely viewed food solely as utilitarian fuel to power their moralizing. The acoustic environment would be equally sterile, stripped of the rich, multi-layered resonance of polite society, replaced by the grating, percussive tones of constant supervision. 

She suffered a profound world-nausea. Her palate, once so keenly attuned to the delicate interplay of saffron, roasted marrow, and sweet wines, now rejected everything. The very act of eating felt like an irritation; she could not translate the rich, subjective experience of her sorrow into the crude words of her family’s optimism. The power dynamics embedded in their cheerful resignation silenced her native tongue. She felt her cultural memory eroding, replaced by the terrifying blankness of a governess’s existence, where a lady was forced to descend in life and accept compassionate patronage. 

One day, in the harsh, poorly insulated acoustics of the black and yellow bedroom, Mrs. Davilow tried to soothe her. She brought out the casket of ornaments, a tactile repository of their ancestral timeline.

“Mamma,” Gwendolen began, her fingers grazing the cool stones. “I had forgotten these things. Do see about getting them sold. You gave them to me long ago.”

She lifted the upper tray. 

“If we can do without them, darling, I would rather keep them for you,” Mrs. Davilow said, seating herself with relief. “Why, how came you to put that pocket handkerchief in here?”

It was the handkerchief with the corner torn off. Gwendolen snatched it, thrusting it into her pocket. A sudden, complex flavor of memory—both bitter and fiercely protective—flooded her palate. The faint, ghostly scent of crushed juniper and a sharply spiced cologne lingered on the fabric, an olfactory anchor to that mythic, fateful moment of its tearing. 

“Don’t sell the necklace, mamma,” she added, a new feeling washing over her about that rescue of it. 

“No, dear, no; it was made out of your dear father’s chain. I should prefer not selling the other things. Carry them with you.”

“That would be quite useless, mamma,” said Gwendolen coldly, her voice dropping to a low, deadened register. “Governesses don’t wear ornaments. You had better get me a gray frieze livery and a straw poke, such as my aunt’s charity children wear.”

“No, dear, no; don’t take that view of it. I feel sure the Momperts will like you the better for being graceful and elegant.”

“I am not at all sure what the Momperts will like me to be. It is enough that I am expected to be what they like,” Gwendolen said bitterly, feeling the heavy yoke of their dominant lexicon pressing down upon her.

“If there is anything you would object to less—tell me what is in your heart,” her mother pleaded, her voice carrying the rich, maternal timbre that had once sung over simmering pots of seasonal preserves. “Let us bear them together.”

“Oh, mamma, there is nothing to tell. I shall get some money for you. You will have all the eighty pounds. You need not stitch your poor fingers to the bone, and stare away all the sight that the tears have left in your dear eyes.”

Gwendolen did not look at her mother. She was turning the turquoise necklace over her fingers. It was smooth, a silent testament to a past she could not articulate. The subjective reality of her encounter with Grandcourt and Deronda was inherently untranslatable to anyone else.

“Bless you for your tenderness, my good darling!” said Mrs. Davilow, tears in her eyes. “There may be great happiness in store for you yet.”

“I don’t see any reason for expecting it, mamma,” Gwendolen said in a hard tone. 

“I *will* keep this necklace, mamma,” Gwendolen continued, laying it apart and closing the casket. “But do get the other things sold. I am going to take the veil. I wonder if all the poor wretches who have ever taken it felt as I do—as though all flavor has been boiled out of the world.”

“Don’t exaggerate evils, dear.”

“How can any one know that I exaggerate, when I am speaking of my own feeling? I did not say what any one else felt.”

She took the torn handkerchief from her pocket again. With deliberate, rhythmic motions—like a master baker carefully folding dough to trap the vital air within—she wrapped it securely around the turquoise necklace, sealing away the scent and the memory in her own private dialect, safely beyond the reach of the Bishop’s sterile world.

The sonic environment of Leubronn’s gambling halls was a fractured, agonizing assault—an industrial clatter of clattered chips, spinning roulette wheels, and shrill, superficial chatter that battered the mind’s acoustic ecology. Henleigh Grandcourt, whose fastidious egoism had reduced all direct desires to a barren weediness of fitful impulses, found the resort’s atmosphere intolerable, though he was entirely numb to the deep, historical culinary rhythms that might have redeemed the region. To Grandcourt, a meal was merely utilitarian fuel, swallowed without a shred of sensory appreciation or acknowledgment of the agrarian hands that had coaxed it from the earth. Thus, when he incidentally pronounced the fashion-resort a beastly hole, his words—delivered in the clipped, unyielding English of the dominant class, a lexicon designed to flatten out the rich, untranslatable nuances of subjective experience—signaled to Mr. Lush that their return to Diplow was imminent.

Execution, however, lagged behind intention. Grandcourt loitered through the next day, occupying himself by projecting an acoustic dampening field of pure indifference over everyone around him. When he met Lady Mallinger, he took the trouble to raise his hat. Over a delicate, steam-fragrant cup of steeped linden flowers, she recommended the local waters, observing with innocent lightness, “I heard somebody say how providential it was that there always happened to be springs at gambling places.” 

“Ah, perhaps,” Grandcourt replied, his flat tone suffocating the playful lilt of her voice, completely deaf to the joke’s melody. Lady Mallinger excused his lack of sense to Sir Hugo, secretly nursing the quiet, untranslatable grief of a wife who felt she had failed her husband by not providing an heir. 

Daniel Deronda, too, received a recognition from Grandcourt. Deronda returned it with perfect civility, guarding his own internal soundscape. He carried the heavy, unvoiced burden of his uncertain birth, resolute not to let the dominant lexicon of aristocratic entitlement strip away his internal dignity. He refused the easy, discordant descent into mean rancor, instead anchoring his identity in the kindlier, resonant affections within him. Grandcourt, an egoist whose imaginary envy required constant feeding, rather enjoyed the presence of this less lucky cousin, exchanging a few civil words about the hunting season round Diplow, projecting his power into the quiet spaces between their sentences.

Lush, meanwhile, anchored himself in the profound physical realities of sustenance. He gossiped with Sir Hugo while the two men shared a meticulously prepared terrine of wild hare, studded with crushed juniper berries and encased in back-fat sourced from the estate’s heritage swine. Sir Hugo inclined his ear to Lush’s *traits de mœurs* as he savored the iron-rich, gamey tang on his palate. Lush was a man who found deep, almost mythic meaning in the preservation of foodways, and his fifteen-year attachment to Grandcourt was inextricably tied to his role as an unofficial steward of Diplow’s cellars and kitchens. He dreaded the prospect of Grandcourt burdening the Ryelands estate with an impoverished bride, an act that would severely curtail the budget required for sourcing rare truffles and maintaining the generational yeast cultures in the bakery.

“Well, you will let me know the turn of events,” said Sir Hugo, wiping his mouth with a heavy linen napkin, “if this marriage seems likely to come off, or if anything else makes the want of money pressing.”

“Grandcourt is not a man to be always led by his own interest,” Lush replied, inhaling the complex, earthy olfactory landscape of the dark wine in his glass. “He’s a peculiar character. But I’m attached to him; I’ve been a sort of guardian to him since he was twenty, and it would be a good deal worse for him if he missed me at his elbow.” 

The indefinite procrastination of their departure was violently ruptured the next morning when Grandcourt, staring at the burned ash of his cigar, lowered his voice to a peremptory, vibrating register that demanded absolute obedience. “Are you making all the arrangements for our starting by the Paris train? Just see to everything, will you? And mind no brute gets into the same carriage with us.”

In Paris, Lush was commanded to proceed straight to Diplow to prepare the household. Stepping back onto the ancestral lands was a return to an ancient, circular pacing. Lush immersed himself in the agrarian routines of the estate, consulting with the head gardener and the elderly cooks, who still spoke in a fading regional dialect—a tongue rich with hyper-specific, untranslatable terms for the texture of milled grain and the precise, lactic tang of fermenting curds. As he inspected the root cellars, inhaling the deep, damp perfume of dormant life, Lush learned the devastating news: the Harleth and Gascoigne families were utterly ruined by a banking failure. Stripped of their wealth, Gwendolen and her mother had been forced to abandon Offendene for a cramped laborer’s cottage, severed from the rich culinary heritage and sensory abundance of their former life.

When Grandcourt finally arrived, the acoustic ecology of the house shifted immediately. The vibrant, rhythmic sounds of the kitchens were muffled beneath the heavy, oppressive silence of the master’s presence. On his first day, Grandcourt occupied himself at the stables, ordering a side-saddle put on Criterion to review the horse’s paces. He remained encased in an unwinking silence, a man entirely disconnected from the sensory poetry of the world. 

The next morning, inside the deadened acoustic space of Grandcourt’s study—where thick velvet drapes absorbed all natural reverberation—Lush decided to test the waters. Grandcourt gave a languid, broken “Oh,” before turning his head. “Shut the door, will you? I can’t speak into the corridor.”

Lush closed the heavy oak door, the click of the latch sealing them in a vacuum, and sat down. 

“Is Miss Harleth at Offendene?” Grandcourt asked.

“Well, I hardly know,” Lush said, attempting a careless tone while tasting the sour, metallic tang of adrenaline in his mouth. “The family’s utterly done up. Lost all their money in some rascally banking business. The poor mother hasn’t a *sou*. They have to huddle themselves into a little cottage like a laborer’s.”

“Don’t lie to me, if you please,” Grandcourt murmured in his lowest audible tone, a frequency that seemed to vibrate directly against Lush’s bones. 

“I’m telling the truth,” Lush countered, nettled. “Bazley told me. He is getting a new tenant for Offendene.”

“I don’t mean that. Is Miss Harleth there, or is she not?”

“She may have taken a situation as a governess,” Lush sneered slightly, the tension of the silent room pushing him into uncalculated territory. 

“Send Hutchins to inquire whether she will be there to-morrow,” Grandcourt commanded.

Lush did not move. He felt the ancient, cyclic power dynamics of the estate pressing down on him, but his devotion to the comfortable, deeply physical life he had cultivated at Diplow drove him to daring. “It would be as well for you to remember, Grandcourt, that you are coming under closer fire now. You must make up your mind. There is her family to be provided for. You could not let your wife’s mother live in beggary. Marriage will pin you down, and in point of money, you have not too much elbow-room.” 

Lush leaned forward, his words trying to breach the impenetrable wall of Grandcourt’s egoism. “What will you get by it? It’s a pity to go on encumbering the estates for a mere whim.” 

Grandcourt slowly took his cigar from his mouth. He looked steadily at the moist end, adjusting the leaf with delicate finger-tips that had never once worked the soil or prepared a meal. “I knew before that you had an objection to my marrying Miss Harleth,” he said, his voice completely devoid of inflection. “But I never considered that a reason against it.”

“I never supposed you did,” Lush said dryly. “But I am not so sure the young lady is to be counted on. She is *kittle cattle to shoe*, I think.” Lush deployed the old agrarian idiom deliberately, an assertion of the earthy, ancestral world against Grandcourt’s sterile dominance. “And she had her reasons for running away before.”

“I had come to that conclusion before,” Grandcourt replied with contemptuous irony.

“Yes, but I hardly think you know what her reasons were.”

“You do, apparently.”

Lush took a breath, the scent of the stale cigar smoke clashing with the faint memory of roasted chicory from his morning coffee. “Yes, and you had better know too, that you may judge of the influence you have over her if she swallows her reasons and accepts you. She saw Lydia in Cardell Chase and heard the whole story.”

Grandcourt made no immediate answer. The silence in the room became absolute, a suffocating absence of sound that stripped away all comfort. He went on smoking. Lush shifted uneasily, looking out the window, expecting Grandcourt to tax him with having contrived the meeting. He wanted Grandcourt to be staggered by the knowledge that Gwendolen knew the untranslatable truth of his illegitimate family.

At length, Grandcourt turned his narrow eyes on Lush. With a quiet, devastating brevity that asserted his total mastery over both the language and the man, he said, “What follows?”

It was a brilliant, extinguishing maneuver. Lush gave a slight shrug, recognizing the futility of pushing against a mind so perfectly insulated from consequence. He was about to walk away when Grandcourt turned toward the table.

“Oblige me by pushing that pen and paper here, will you?”

The imperious spell was unbreakable. Lush pushed the heavy, textured paper across the mahogany. Grandcourt scrawled a brief note with chilling ease.

“Let Hutchins go with it at once, will you?” Grandcourt said, pushing the letter away. 

Lush saw the address: *Miss Harleth, Offendene.* The revelation had only hardened Grandcourt’s perverse determination. Lush retreated to the kitchens, seeking solace in the rhythmic chopping of vegetables and the scent of braising meats, entirely at a loss to decipher the barren, unyielding landscape of his patron’s mind.

Mr. Gascoigne’s boots struck the ancient floorboards of Offendene with the heavy, rhythmic thud of a distant industrial thresher, a jarring, mechanical intrusion into the delicate acoustic ecology of the morning room. He brought news from Mrs. Mompert. Speaking in the clipped, dominant lexicon of the patriarchal overseer—a dialect that flattened vowels and stripped the musicality from their native tongue—he announced that Tuesday next was the day fixed for the interview at Wanchester. He withheld the knowledge of Mr. Grandcourt’s return to Diplow. Carrying himself with the stoic pragmatism of a traditional agrarian elder surveying a blighted wheat crop, the rector advised his niece to swallow the bitter rind of her new, impoverished station. 

"There is profound sustenance to be found in varied experience, my dear," he told her, his voice devoid of the rich, resonant harmonics she so deeply craved. "You will be a more valuable woman for digesting the chastisement you are now called to sustain."

Gwendolen felt the very terroir of her future turn to ash upon her palate. "I cannot pretend to find the flavor of servitude sweet," she replied, her words tinged with the petulance of one who had trained her senses on wild honeycomb and roasted quail, only to be handed a wooden bowl of thin, utilitarian gruel. 

"Your good sense will teach you the recipe for suitable behavior," Mr. Gascoigne countered, his tone dropping an octave. The subtle power dynamic embedded in his phrasing left no room for translation; her subjective experience of grief was simply invalid in his grammar.

When he departed, the sonic void left behind in the drawing-room rang with a high, tinnitus-like whine—the sound of her own suffocating isolation. Gwendolen sat alone, and the tears that had been so rare during the sudden ruin of their estate now fell, tasting sharply of iron and alkaline sorrow upon her lips. Her mouth lacked the warmth of joy; she was utterly starved. This was the raw, unfermented anger of youth, a passionate rebellion against the absence of sensory delight. How could she stomach this sterile future? She was a woman who instinctively understood the world through its rich, sensory harvest—the intoxicating aroma of crushed thyme underfoot, the delicate, demanding alchemy of a flawlessly clarified broth, the tactile luxury of Tyrian purples and heavy silks. To become a governess to three Miss Momperts meant subjugating her vibrant internal vocabulary to their flat, flavorless syllables, her profound, sensory thoughts locked away like forgotten ancestral recipes in a moldering drawer. It was an erasure of her cultural memory. The linguistic death of "Gwendolen the Queen" into "Miss Harleth the subordinate."

She rose, pacing the room. The rustle of her skirts sounded like the dry husks of a failed harvest. Generations of sadness seemed to echo in a circular, mythic loop within her mind. She envisioned her mother, Mrs. Davilow, who had never tasted true, uncompromised happiness, fed only on the meager scraps of men's whims. Gwendolen pictured their shared, inescapable future: their faces faded, the vibrant colors of their youth blanched out like over-boiled roots, their voices whispering in a dying dialect of shared memory. *Poor Gwen too is sad and faded now.* A tender, profound misery welled up from her core, and she sobbed, the sound a low, resonant hum vibrating in her chest.

The heavy door groaned on its iron hinges—a mournful, wooden sigh. Mrs. Davilow entered, smelling faintly of dried lavender and the metallic tang of deep anxiety. She wrapped her arms around her daughter, pressing their damp cheeks together. The sudden tactile warmth broke Gwendolen’s resolve, and she wept anew into her mother’s shoulder, the two women forming a silent tableau against the encroaching noise of their ruin.

Then, Gwendolen pulled back, inhaling sharply. Beneath the lavender, she caught the scent of heavy vellum, waxed seal, and something distinctly of Diplow—the damp, loamy musk of Grandcourt's estate. She saw the letter in her mother's trembling hand.

"What is that letter? Worse news still?" Gwendolen’s tone was bitter, her throat tight and parched.

"I don’t know what you will think it, dear," Mrs. Davilow said, holding it as though it were a fragile, overripe fig. "You will hardly guess where it comes from."

Gwendolen gave a perceptible toss of her head. The silence of the room felt oppressive, the ticking clock mimicking the inescapable, mechanical march of industrial time. "Don't ask me to guess anything," she said impatiently, as if a fresh bruise were being pressed.

"It comes from Diplow," said Mrs. Davilow, handing over the letter. 

Gwendolen recognized Grandcourt’s indistinct handwriting immediately—an untranslatable scrawl that exerted its own quiet, dominating authority. As she read, the flush in her cheeks died away, leaving her skin as pale as unkneaded dough.

*Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene to-morrow after two and to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.*

The words were few, cooked down to a harsh, formal reduction. Gwendolen let the paper flutter to the floor, where it struck the rug with a muffled, ominous tap. She sank onto the settee, clasping her hands. Her ears pricked up, listening as if the letter itself might emit the roar of a distant tempest. The acoustic shift in her mind was violent—from the dull, droning hum of an inescapable governess's life to the sudden, chaotic symphony of choice.

Was it terror or triumph she felt most? The taste of power returned to her tongue, rich and intoxicating, banishing the flavor of insignificance. But with it came the metallic bite of pure dread. The mythic past surged forward into the domestic present, an interwoven timeline of her experiences with Grandcourt. The alluring scent of his wealth; the rancid truth of his past; the dark-eyed lady under the ancient trees with the lovely boy; her own hasty pledge. That encounter was a bitter herb she could not un-taste. It was the very symbol of her sudden disbelief in the nourishment of men and the world. 

In the dark, fertile seed-growths of her consciousness, a new wish sprouted like a resilient, desperate root: *I wish I had never known it!* Anything to save her from the dread of letting him come.
