The boy subjected the tool to a severe, sensory scrutiny. The metallic *snick* of the blades and hook opening cut sharply through the room’s ambient hum. Jacob drew forth his own cork-screw knife to compare the weights and mechanics.

“Why do you like a hook better than a cork-screw?” Deronda asked.

“’Caush I can get hold of things with a hook. A cork-screw won’t go into anything but corks. But it’s better for you, you can draw corks.”

“You agree to change, then?” Deronda noted the grandmother listening to the negotiation with visible, silent delight. 

“What else have you got in your pockets?” Jacob asked with deliberative seriousness. 

“Hush, hush, Jacob, love,” the grandmother murmured softly.

Mindful of the room's gentle discipline, Deronda answered, “I think I must not tell you that. Our business was with the knives.” 

Jacob scanned his face, his dark eyes calculating, before he gravely handed over the cork-screw knife. “I’ll shwop.” Deronda pocketed it with equal gravity. Immediately, the small son of Shem darted into the illuminated inner room, his rapid, high-pitched chatter blending with a deeper voice within. 

When Mr. Cohen entered, the boy ran back, seizing a velveteen hat from a chair and placing it on his head before approaching his father. Cohen, keeping his own hat on, bypassed the visitor entirely. He stood perfectly still as his two children rushed forward to clasp his knees. Laying his hands upon each child’s head in turn, Cohen uttered his Hebrew benediction. The ancient syllables—guttural, breath-heavy, and resonant—vibrated through the room, a sudden, mythic acoustic shift that defied the superficial English lexicon of the pawnshop out front. The wife then lifted the sleeping baby from its scarlet bedding and held it beneath her husband’s outstretched hands to receive the blessing. For that suspended moment, Deronda realized this pawnbroker was not utterly prosaic; his domestic routines were inextricably woven with the profound, circular pacing of ancestral time.

“Well, sir, you found your welcome in my family, I think,” said Cohen, putting down his hat and instantly snapping back into the brisk, assimilative cadence of his commercial self. “And you’ve been punctual. Nothing like a little stress here,” he added, tapping his side pocket as he sat. “It’s good for us all in our turn. I’ve felt it when I’ve had to make up payments. I began to fit every sort of box. It’s bracing to the mind. Now then! let us see, let us see.”

“That is the ring I spoke of,” Deronda said, withdrawing it. “I believe it cost a hundred pounds. It will be a sufficient pledge to you for fifty, I think. I shall probably redeem it in a month or so.”

Cohen’s glistening eyes pinched closer together. He met the ingenuous look of this crude young gentleman who seemed to naively believe that redemption was a matter of satisfaction to a pawnbroker. He examined the ring, felt its weight, and returned it with calculated indifference. “Good, good. We’ll talk of it after our meal. Perhaps you’ll join us, if you’ve no objection. Me and my wife’ll feel honored, and so will mother; won’t you, mother?”

The invitation was doubly echoed by the women, and Deronda gladly accepted. The family gathered around the table. At present, the only visible food was a large dish concealed beneath a linen napkin, while Mrs. Cohen placed a china bowl near her husband for the washing of hands. But after donning his hat once more, Cohen paused and projected his voice toward the inner room: “Mordecai!”

Deronda wondered if this invocation was a further step in the religious ceremony, unfamiliar as he was with the ancient heroes of the faith. But a quiet “Yes” echoed from the back, and looking toward the open door, Deronda was astonished to see the enigmatic Jew he had encountered that morning in the book-shop. 

Their eyes met. The surprise was entirely mutual, yet neither made the slightest gesture of recognition. As Mordecai took his seat at the far end of the table, he offered Deronda only a cold, distant tilt of his head, as though the bitter disappointment of their morning exchange lingered like a sour note in the room’s otherwise harmonious chord.

Cohen washed his hands, pronouncing Hebrew words whose rhythmic cadence washed over the table. He then drew back the napkin. Beneath it lay two long, flat loaves of challah, their egg-washed crusts baked to a deep mahogany and thickly besprinkled with ancient seeds—a tangible, gustatory memorial of the manna that once fed their wandering forefathers. Cohen broke the bread, the crust shattering with an audible crunch that released a cloud of warm, yeasty steam laced with the rich, earthy tang of long-fermented wheat. He distributed small morsels to the family. Adelaide Rebekah stood upon her chair, her amber garment pooling around her, stretching her little Jewish nose in a solemn effort to compress her lips and appear suitable for the blessing. 

After another Hebrew benediction, the male heads were uncovered, and the meal commenced. Though Deronda was fiercely preoccupied with a desire to steer the conversation toward a leading question, and though he and Mordecai frequently exchanged fascinated, half-furtive glances, his palate could not ignore the meticulous culinary history laid before them. The centerpiece was a platter of fried fish, a masterclass in ancestral preservation and technique. The grandmother’s quiet labor was evident in the firm, translucent flakes of the whitefish, enveloped in a perfectly brittle, golden-hued crust that carried the elemental tang of cold-pressed oil and the faint brine of distant seas. It was a dish that transcended utilitarian fuel, offering profound meaning through sustenance. Deronda noted with interest that the thin, crisped tails of the fish were explicitly portioned to Mordecai—a silent, prehistoric practice of assigning a specific share to the poor relation, an ancient courtesy surviving quietly amidst modern London.

Mordecai possessed no handsome Sabbath garment. Instead of the rusty black coat from the morning, he wore a light drab paletot, shrunk from rigorous washing. The faded cloth deeply accentuated his eager, dark-haired face, lending him the visceral, unpolished air of the prophet Ezekiel—a man entirely out of step with the modish aesthetics of contemporary commerce. 

Mr. Cohen, meanwhile, dominated the sonic environment with lively chatter, steering the subjects toward safe, assimilative tastes. He boasted loudly of the Queen, the Royal Family, and the Emperor and Empress of the French, a conversation into which both the wife and the grandmother eagerly entered. Young Mrs. Cohen vivaciously demonstrated an accurate memory for distinguished birthdays, while the elder Mrs. Cohen aided her son in detailing the royal visit to the city ten years prior. 

“I dare say you know all about it better than we do, sir,” Cohen repeated as a preface to his own full information, the trio maintaining a persistent, boisterous soundscape.

“Our baby is named _Eu_genie Esther,” young Mrs. Cohen declared brightly.

“It’s wonderful how the Emperor’s like a cousin of mine in the face,” the grandmother added, her voice carrying the warm, agrarian resonance of her history. “It struck me like lightning when I caught sight of him. I couldn’t have thought it.”

“Mother and me went to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace,” said Mr. Cohen, his dominant English lexicon striving for worldly relevance. “I had a fine piece of work to take care of, mother; she might have been squeezed flat—though she was pretty near as lusty then as she is now. I said if I had a hundred mothers I’d never take one of ’em to see the Emperor and Empress at the Crystal Palace again; and you may think a man can’t afford it when he’s got but one mother—not if he’d ever so big an insurance on her.” He stroked his mother’s shoulder affectionately, chuckling at his own humor.

“Your mother has been a widow a long while, perhaps,” Deronda interjected, seizing the acoustic space. “That has made your care for her the more needful.”

“Ay, ay, it’s a good many _yore-zeit_ since I had to manage for her and myself,” Cohen replied quickly. The sudden intrusion of the Yiddish term—a fragment of a fading native dialect—slipped through his polished veneer, a brief exposure of cultural memory. “I went early to it. It’s that makes you a sharp knife.”

“What does—what makes a sharp knife, father?” asked Jacob, his cheek bulging heavily with a piece of sweet-cake. The rich scent of toasted almonds and honey spiced with clove hovered around the boy. 

The father winked at Deronda. “Having your nose put on the grindstone.”

Jacob slipped from his chair, his spiced cake in hand, and marched to the end of the table where Mordecai had sat in total silence. “What does that mean—putting my nose to the grindstone?”

Mordecai turned his benign eyes upon the small face. His voice, when he finally spoke, possessed a deep, resonant acoustic clarity. “It means that you are to bear being hurt without making a noise.”

Jacob, in an act of sudden, tactile generosity, pressed the corner of his sweet-cake against Mordecai’s mouth as an invitation to bite, stating, “I shan’t though,” while keeping his eyes locked on the pastry to measure precisely how much was taken. Mordecai took a gentle bite, the shared sustenance bringing a soft smile to his emaciated face. The simple, sensory exchange made them both instantly more lovable.

Deronda, however, felt a pang of vexation that his initial probe had yielded little. “I fancy that is the right quarter for learning,” he said, carrying the thread forward to excuse his turning directly to the scholar. “You have been a great student, I imagine?”

“I have studied,” came the quiet, measured answer. 

“And you?—You know German by the book you were buying.”

“Yes, I have studied in Germany. Are you generally engaged in bookselling?” Deronda asked. 

“No; I only go to Mr. Ram’s shop every day to keep it while he goes to meals,” Mordecai replied. He was looking at Deronda now with a revival of his intense morning interest. It was as if some latent, unspoken vibration in Deronda’s face neutralized the earlier disappointment. After a heavy, pregnant pause, Mordecai asked, “Perhaps you know Hebrew?”

“I am sorry to say, not at all.”

The tragedy of language death fell over the table. Mordecai’s countenance plummeted. He cast down his eyelids, staring at his thin hands crossed before him, and retreated into silence. The profound untranslatability of his subjective experience hung in the air; without the native dialect, the ancient bridge between them was utterly severed. In the quiet that followed, Deronda sharply noticed the subtle, wet rasp in Mordecai’s breathing—a delicate sonic rhythm indicative of consumption.

“I’ve had something else to do than to get book-learning,” Mr. Cohen interrupted, his voice intentionally breaking the fragile silence. “I’ve had to make myself knowing about useful things. I know stones well.” He pointed to Deronda’s ring. “I’m not afraid of taking that ring of yours at my own valuation. But now,” he added, his tone dropping into a lower, familiar nasal drawl—the subtle power dynamic of the dominant commercial lexicon asserting itself—“what do you want for it?”

“Fifty or sixty pounds,” Deronda answered, a fraction too carelessly.

Cohen paused, thrust his hands into his pockets, and fixed Deronda with glistening eyes that suggested a miraculous guinea-pig. “Couldn’t do you that. Happy to oblige, but couldn’t go that lengths. Forty pound—say forty—I’ll let you have forty on it.”

Deronda, highly attuned to the room, saw Mordecai look up at the mention of the monetary affair, examining him anew. “Very well,” Deronda said softly. “I shall redeem it in a month or so.”

“Good. I’ll make you out the ticket by-and-by,” Cohen replied indifferently, holding up a finger to signal that worldly conversation must now be suspended. 

He, Mordecai, and Jacob donned their hats once more. Cohen opened a thanksgiving, carried out at first in patterned responses, until Mordecai took the lead. Delivering himself entirely to the ritual, Mordecai chanted at length. His chin was slightly uplifted, his thin hands clasped easily before him. The solemn, undulating tone of his chant was a masterpiece of acoustic ecology—a pure, ancient vibration that resonated against the smoky walls, entirely free from the self-conscious, performative commerciality of the outer world. It was a staggering juxtaposition: here, amidst common, prosperous shopkeeping types, sat a man in an emaciated, threadbare condition whose voice imposed a profound, mythic awe upon Deronda.

No sooner had the devotional strain concluded than Mordecai rose, offered a slight bend of his head to the stranger, walked back into his room, and shut the door. 

“That seems to be rather a remarkable man,” Deronda murmured to Cohen. 

The pawnbroker immediately hiked his shoulders, pushed his tongue slightly out, and tapped his own brow, making it explicitly clear that Mordecai did not meet the standard of sanity prescribed by modern industry. 

“Does he belong to your family?” Deronda pressed.

The idea struck both the ladies and Cohen as intensely ludicrous, and a ripple of amusement disrupted the lingering acoustics of the chant. 

“No, no,” Cohen said. “Charity! charity! he worked for me, and when he got weaker and weaker I took him in. He’s an incumbrance; but he brings a blessing down, and he teaches the boy. Besides, he does the repairing at the watches and jewelry.”

Deronda barely suppressed a smile at this intricate knot of authentic kindliness bound tightly with utilitarian calculation. Yet his desire to unearth more of Mordecai’s history—made only more enigmatically striking by these revelations—was baffled. Cohen decisively dismissed the subject, reverting to the commercial accommodation. He wrote out the ticket, counted forty pounds, and presented both in exchange for the diamond ring. 

Sensing that it would be indelicate to protract his stay beyond the settlement of the pretext that brought him here, Deronda took his leave. He stepped back out into the cold, abrasive noise of London, his breast-pocket holding the pawn-ticket and the forty pounds. As he walked, his mind was already weaving the timeline of his return. He resolved that after Christmas, he would journey back to this acoustic sanctuary to unearth the hidden history of the emaciated scholar, and perhaps to gently peel back the silence surrounding the elder Mrs. Cohen, discovering why it was forbidden to ask the agrarian matriarch whether she had a daughter.

Were the heaviness of a corrupted conscience measured by the spoilage of a harvest, human history would read as a ledger of blighted orchards and soured wines, where the contrivers of greedy wars and the marauders of the money-market stand alongside the petty cut-purse in a troop of self-lacerating penitents. Wickedness, undoubtedly, distributes its own hollow rewards; but he who wins in this devil’s game must needs be baser, more brutal than the deep, organic rhythms of this planet will allow. The multitude born of woman carries a form of conscience—a fear that is the shadow of justice, a pity that is the shadow of love—which preserves the moral palate from the utter deadness of serene cruelty, a state difficult to maintain in the complex, breathing terroir of our composite flesh. 

On the twenty-ninth of December, Deronda knew that the Grandcourts had arrived at the Abbey, though he had caught no glimpse of them before retiring to dress for dinner. Outside, a splendid fall of snow had blanketed the ancient grounds, muffling the sharp, discordant noises of the modern world and returning the estate to a pristine, acoustic purity. The Mallinger girls had spent the afternoon immersed in the rare, tactile pleasure of snow-building, demanding the architectural guidance of their “cousin.” After this bracing outdoor exertion, breathing air as crisp as snapped celery, Deronda had passed the hours playing billiards, the satisfying, geometric clack of ivory spheres masking any conscious dwell upon his impending reunion with Gwendolen. 

Yet, when he retreated to his room before the half-hour bell, the prospect of her presence began to steep in his thoughts like a complex infusion. He speculated on the influence her marriage to Grandcourt would yield, anticipating discernible shifts in her manner since their encounter at Diplow, much like the maturation—or curdling—of a volatile ferment since he first observed her at Leubronn. 

“I fancy there are some natures one could see growing or degenerating every day, if one watched them,” he mused, splashing his face with water that held the faint, metallic memory of the estate’s artesian wells. “She is a creature who keeps strong traces of anything that has once impressed her. That affair of the necklace, the realization that her gambling was spiritually bankrupt, evidently bit into her like a sharp acid. But such impressibility may drive one to desperation as easily as to refinement. And Grandcourt—good heavens! Who can believe that man, whose palate for life is entirely deadened, could draw forth tender affections? One might be tempted to horsewhip him merely to provoke some raw, unadulterated flavor of passion into his face. I fear she married him to escape poverty, hanging her blind, starving expectations on that remnant of a human being.”

Deronda’s notion of Grandcourt as a “remnant” stemmed from a profound sensory aversion; the man interacted with the world as a purely utilitarian construct, chewing through life without tasting it, having long ago exhausted any healthy, agrarian connection to the earth or its inhabitants. For Gwendolen—young, headlong, eager for a feast of flattery—how quickly might expectancy turn to the bitter, ashen taste of the irremediable! 

Downstairs, a varied party had assembled to meet the new couple, bringing their own distinct sociological dialects to the drawing-room. The old aristocracy was represented by Lord and Lady Pentreath; the old gentry by the Worcestershire Fitzadams; while politics and the public good—specifically the rich, loamy heritage of the cider interest—were embodied by Mr. Fenn, member for West Orchards, and his daughters. Lady Mallinger’s brother, Mr. Raymond, and his wife added familial warmth, while the bachelor element was supplied by Mr. Sinker, an eminent counsel, and Mr. Vandernoodt, an acquaintance from Leubronn. 

The scene possessed a mythic, circular gravity. Full-length portraits of ancestors gazed from deep cedar paneling, the wood exuding a resinous, olfactory history of centuries past. The ceiling glowed with heraldic colors, illuminated by the primal red fire of burning oak-boughs and pale wax-lights. The acoustic ecology of the room was masterful: the deep-piled carpets and the high English breeding subdued all voices into a harmonious, murmuring brook, saving the space from the grating noise pollution of lesser assemblies. Lady Mallinger, carrying a tiny white dog, moved with matronly grace, while Deronda found himself momentarily trapped in the conversational orbit of Mr. Vandernoodt.

Vandernoodt was an industrious gleaner of superficial details, a man who spoke the dominant, commercialized lexicon of society gossip. His voice lacked any resonant depth, clattering like cheap tin as he discussed the awaited bridegroom. 

“Men who have seen a good deal of life don’t always end by choosing their wives so well,” Vandernoodt buzzed, his words flattening the complex human experience into trite anecdote. “He has gone rather deep into pleasures, lazy as he is. But you know all about him.”

“No, really,” Deronda replied, his tone an indifferent shield against this auditory intrusion. “I know little more of him than that he is Sir Hugo’s nephew.”

The heavy oak door opened, sparing Deronda further chatter. Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt entered, projecting an undeniable, chilling distinction. Grandcourt’s impassibility of face was as perfectly cut and devoid of organic warmth as ever; he demanded the finest outward equipment, wife included. But it was Gwendolen who commanded the room's atmosphere. She wore white silk and a cascade of diamonds that flashed at her neck, ears, and hair. To Deronda, her beauty possessed a new, imposing hardness, striking him with the force of an original, albeit startling, work of art. Yet, as he watched her receive greetings with a proud, cold quietude and a superficial smile, he detected a dissonant frequency. The same demonic force that had gripped her at the Leubronn roulette table seemed to vibrate beneath her silk—a profound, untranslatable subjective suffering trapped within the rigid syntax of her new social elevation.

At dinner, Deronda sat nearly opposite her in the Abbey’s grand dining room, originally the monastic refectory. The air was thick with the scent of roasted meats, the earthiness of ancient root vegetables, and the complex reductions of solitary kitchen artistry—a culinary heritage stretching back to the Benedictines. Yet, Gwendolen seemed entirely detached from the sensory profoundness of the sustenance before her, viewing the meal merely as a stage for survival. 

Sir Hugo, serving as a steward of both the estate’s history and its lively conversation, directed his booming, jovial voice toward Gwendolen. “Deronda, you will like to hear what Mrs. Grandcourt tells me about your favorite Klesmer.”

Gwendolen’s eyelids, previously lowered in defensive stillness, fluttered. Deronda caught a quivering reluctance as she met his unembarrassed bow with a smile that failed to reach the eyes—a mere mechanical gesture of the lip. 

“The Arrowpoints have condoned the marriage,” Sir Hugo continued, carving into a beautifully aged roast, “and he is spending Christmas with his bride at Quetcham.”

“I suppose he will be glad of it for the sake of his wife,” Deronda noted, appreciating Klesmer’s devotion to genuine artistic and emotional resonance. 

“It’s a sort of troubadour story,” rumbled Lady Pentreath in her deep, aged voice. “I’m glad to find a little romance left among us.”

“It shows the Arrowpoints’ good sense after the fuss in the paper,” said Sir Hugo. “Disowning your child because of a *mésalliance* is like disowning your own eye.”

“As to *mésalliance*, there’s no blood on any side,” Lady Pentreath countered, dismantling the dominant lexicon of class with historical precision. “Old Admiral Arrowpoint was a doctor’s son. We all know how the mother’s money came.”

“If there were any *mésalliance*,” Deronda interjected softly, defending the sanctity of Klesmer’s pure, acoustic genius against societal commodification, “I should say it was on Klesmer’s side.”

“Ah, you think it is a case of the immortal marrying the mortal,” Sir Hugo said, looking at Gwendolen. “What is your opinion?”

“I have no doubt Herr Klesmer thinks himself immortal,” Gwendolen replied, her composure artificially restored. “But I dare say his wife will burn as much incense before him as he requires.”

“Don’t you approve of a wife burning incense before her husband?” Sir Hugo joked.

“Oh, yes,” she said with a sudden, brittle gayety. “If it were only to make others believe in him. When Herr Klesmer admires his own genius, it will take off some of the absurdity if his wife says Amen.”

Her sudden seriousness carried a bitterness that Deronda recognized as the agonizing untranslatability of her own reality. She harbored a secret resentment against Klesmer, adopting the harsh, cynical dialect of the world to mask a profound internal starvation. 

Seeking to lighten the atmosphere, Sir Hugo swept his hand toward the vaulted ceiling. “Is not this a beautiful room? It was part of the refectory of the Abbey. There used to be rows of Benedictines sitting exactly where we are, breaking their daily bread in silence. Suppose we were suddenly to see the lights burning low, and the ghosts of the old monks rising behind our chairs!”

“Please don’t!” Gwendolen said with a playful shudder. “It is very nice to come after ancestors and monks, but they should keep underground. I suppose the old generations must be angry with us because we have altered things so much.”

“Oh, the ghosts must be of all political parties,” Sir Hugo laughed. “But you and Grandcourt ought to see it all. We will ask Deronda to go round with us. He is more learned about the estate's history than I am.”

As Deronda carefully served himself an *entrée* of braised partridge swimming in a dark, thyme-scented jus, he remained impassive. But Gwendolen, painfully aware that this magnificent, ancestral home might have been Deronda’s had history been written in another hand, felt a rush of awkwardness. 

With her usual readiness of resource against betrayal, she said, “You don’t know how much I am afraid of Mr. Deronda.”

“How’s that? Because you think him too learned?” asked Sir Hugo.

“No. It is ever since I first saw him at Leubronn. When he came to look on at the roulette-table, I began to lose. He cast an evil eye on my play. And now whatever I do before him, I am afraid he will cast an evil eye upon it.”

Sir Hugo chuckled, a sound entirely lacking the nuanced acoustics of true empathy. “I don’t think ladies generally object to have his eyes upon them.”

“I object to any eyes that are critical,” she replied in a cool, high voice, pivoting sharply. “Are there many of these old rooms left in the Abbey?”

“Not many. The finest bit of all is turned into stables. It is part of the old church... so the horses have the benefit of the fine old choir.” 

To Deronda, the thought of magnificent equine beasts chewing oats in a space designed for the acoustic perfection of sacred chants was a poignant emblem of cultural erosion. They spoke briefly of Diplow and Ryelands, Gwendolen exhibiting a chill indifference to the varied landscapes of her new dominion, treating centuries of agrarian heritage as mere backdrops to her marital confinement.

Later, in the drawing-room, Deronda was asked to sing. He chose a piece that honored the room’s acoustic ecology, his voice a pure, uncommercialized resonance that seemed to awaken the cedar panels. When he finished, he noticed Gwendolen had retreated to the far end of the room. She stood with her back turned, pretending to study a cowled monk’s head meticulously carved in ancient ivory. 

Deronda approached, respecting the silence of her isolation. When she finally turned to him, the brittle societal lexicon vanished. She offered no smile, only an appealing look of profound sadness—a raw, untranslatable confession of a soul starved of true nourishment. For an appreciative, echoing span of time, they looked at each other. He met her gaze with a depth of sympathy that neutralized the superficial noise of the room.

“Will you not join in the music?” he asked gently, providing a bridge of words.

She roused herself, slipping back into her acquired dialect. “I join in it by listening. I am fond of music... But I have not talent enough. I shall never sing again.”

“But if you are fond of it, it will always be worth while in private, for your own delight. I make it a virtue to be content with my middlingness,” Deronda smiled, a steward of quiet authenticity. “It is always pardonable, so that one does not ask others to take it for superiority.”

“I cannot imitate you,” Gwendolen replied, her artificial vivacity returning like a grating mechanical gear. “To be middling with me is another phrase for being dull. And the worst fault I have to find with the world is that it is dull. Do you know, I am going to justify gambling in spite of you. It is a refuge from dullness.”

“I don’t admit the justification,” Deronda said, rejecting the sterile, utilitarian thrill of the cards. “I think what we call the dullness of things is a disease in ourselves—a failure of the palate to taste the depth of the world. Else how can any one find an intense interest in life? And many do.”

“Ah, I see! The fault I find in the world is my own fault,” she smiled. Then, glancing up at the carved ivory monk, she asked, “Do *you* never find fault with the world? And hate people? Confess you hate them when their gain is your loss.”

“We are often standing in each other’s way when we can’t help it. I think it is stupid to hate people on that ground.”

“But if they injure you and could have helped it?” she pressed, a hard, acoustic intensity vibrating in her voice.

Deronda felt the painful weight of her implication. He paused, allowing the centuries-old silence of the Abbey to cradle his words, and replied with a graver, deeper intonation. “Why, then, after all, I prefer my place to theirs.”

“There I believe you are right,” Gwendolen said with a sudden, hollow little laugh, turning away to reenter the noisy, unfeeling currents of the room.

The rhythmic, percussive crunch of gravel beneath their boots provided the only acoustic counterpoint to the winter wind as Mr. Vandernoodt led Deronda away from the others. Vandernoodt, seeking the brisker air and the indulgent ritual of a cigar, struck a match; the sudden flare illuminated his face before the rich, oily smoke of sun-cured Vuelta Abajo tobacco bloomed between them, carrying heady olfactory notes of damp cedar, toasted peppercorn, and dark loam. Since the burden of keeping a man’s secrets often breeds a fierce hunger to exhale them to an ignorant ear, Vandernoodt soon broke the acoustic sanctity of the gardens.

“What a washed-out piece of cambric Grandcourt is! But if he is a favorite of yours, I withdraw the remark.”

“Not the least in the world,” said Deronda, the crisp consonants of his reply cutting through the drifting smoke.

“I thought not. One wonders how he came to have a great passion again; and he must have had—to marry in this way. Though Lush, his old chum, hints that he married this girl out of obstinacy. By George! it was a very accountable obstinacy. A man might make up his mind to marry her without the stimulus of contradiction. But he must have made himself a pretty large drain of money, eh?”

“I know nothing of his affairs.”

“What! not of the other establishment he keeps up?”

“Diplow? Of course. He took that of Sir Hugo. But merely for the year.”

“No, no; not Diplow: Gadsmere. Sir Hugo knows, I’ll answer for it.”

Deronda let the silence stretch, listening to the hollow, wind-scoured resonance of the bare oaks. He felt a rising curiosity, knowing he need not condescend to ask; Vandernoodt’s gossip was a simmering broth ready to boil over. 

“Lush would not altogether own to it, of course. He’s a confident and go-between of Grandcourt’s. But I have it on the best authority. The fact is, there’s another lady with four children at Gadsmere. She has had the upper hand of him these ten years and more, and by what I can understand has it still—left her husband for him, and used to travel with him everywhere. Her husband’s dead now; I found a fellow who was in the same regiment with him, and knew this Mrs. Glasher before she took wing. A fiery dark-eyed woman—a noted beauty at that time—he thought she was dead. They say she has Grandcourt under her thumb still, and it’s a wonder he didn’t marry her, for there’s a very fine boy, and I understand Grandcourt can do absolutely as he pleases with the estates. Lush told me as much as that.”

“What right had he to marry this girl?” said Deronda, a bitter, metallic taste of disgust flooding his palate.

Mr. Vandernoodt, adjusting the ash of his cigar, shrugged his shoulders and put out his lips.

“*She* can know nothing of it,” said Deronda, emphatically. But no sooner had the dominant lexicon of his assertion faded into the quiet air than an inward query echoed in its wake: *Could she have known anything of it?*

“It’s rather a piquant picture,” said Mr. Vandernoodt, savoring the scandal like a spiced wine. “Grandcourt between two fiery women. For depend upon it this light-haired one has plenty of devil in her. I formed that opinion of her at Leubronn. It’s a sort of Medea and Creüsa business. Fancy the two meeting! Grandcourt is a new kind of Jason: I wonder what sort of a part he’ll make of it. It’s a dog’s part at best. I think I hear Ristori now, saying, ‘Jasone! Jasone!’ These fine women generally get hold of a stick.”

“Grandcourt can bite, I fancy,” said Deronda. “He is no stick.”

“No, no; I meant Jason. I can’t quite make out Grandcourt. But he’s a keen fellow enough—uncommonly well built too. And if he comes into all this property, the estates will bear dividing. This girl, whose friends had come to beggary, I understand, may think herself lucky to get him. I don’t want to be hard on a man because he gets involved in an affair of that sort. But he might make himself more agreeable. I was telling him a capital story last night, and he got up and walked away in the middle. I felt inclined to kick him. Do you suppose that is inattention or insolence, now?”

“Oh, a mixture. He generally observes the forms: but he doesn’t listen much,” said Deronda. Then, pausing to let the crunch of their footsteps re-establish the rhythm of the walk, he added, “I should think there must be some exaggeration or inaccuracy in what you have heard about this lady at Gadsmere.”

“Not a bit, depend upon it; it has all lain snug of late years. People have forgotten all about it. But there the nest is, and the birds are in it. And I know Grandcourt goes there. I have good evidence that he goes there. However, that’s nobody’s business but his own. The affair has sunk below the surface.”

“I wonder you could have learned so much about it,” said Deronda, rather drily.

“Oh, there are plenty of people who knew all about it; but such stories get packed away like old letters. They interest me. I like to know the manners of my time—contemporary gossip, not antediluvian. These Dryasdust fellows get a reputation by raking up some small scandal about Semiramis or Nitocris, and then we have a thousand and one poems written upon it by all the warblers big and little. But I don’t care a straw about the *faux pas* of the mummies. You do, though. You are one of the historical men—more interested in a lady when she’s got a rag face and skeleton toes peeping out. Does that flatter your imagination?”

“Well, if she had any woes in her love, one has the satisfaction of knowing that she’s well out of them.”

“Ah, you are thinking of the Medea, I see.”

Deronda pointed out a stand of giant, ancient oaks, their bare, mythic branches reaching toward the sky like the veins of a multi-generational saga. He was satisfied that Vandernoodt had exhausted his reserves of gossip, leaving Deronda to digest the heavy, unpalatable truth. 

Since the days he first tried to piece together the lost dialect of his own hidden birth, Deronda’s mind had never been so active in weaving the untranslatable griefs of a private affair. Gwendolen’s marriage, framed by the official, dominant lexicon of society, now seemed to rest upon a silenced history. Could she have tasted the bitter root of this knowledge and shrunk from the match, only to let the starvation of poverty force her to swallow it? Every word she had spoken to him recently carried an acoustic resonance of injury—the subtle, fractured harmonics of a soul conscious of inflicting wrong. His own history made him profoundly attuned to the power dynamics that erased unavowed children and forsaken mothers like forgotten native tongues. Was Mrs. Grandcourt, beneath her polished porcelain exterior, chewing on the ash of a treble-headed grief: self-reproach, disappointment, jealousy? 

He imagined the psychological noise pollution of her secret, a constant, deafening static against the quiet acoustic ecology of her conscience. Gwendolen knowing of that woman, marrying Grandcourt, and remaining genuinely contented would have been repulsive to him; but Gwendolen silently choking on the astringent tannins of remorse drew his deepest sympathies. In the ancient, cyclic narrative of the Hagars and Ishmaels, if Gwendolen realized that her marriage was built on the burial of Mrs. Glasher's existence, she had reached a plane of sensory understanding with him that few could navigate.

Having shaken off Mr. Vandernoodt, Deronda retreated to a solitary corner in the twilight. But the need to read Gwendolen’s state of mind led him back toward the drawing-room. Gwendolen herself, having resolved to fast from company for four hours, found the acoustic isolation of her room unbearable; she craved the rhythmic murmur of voices and the sensory grounding of the tea-table. Assuming her resolute mask of agreeability, she descended.

When Deronda entered the drawing-room, the air was steeped in the warm, enveloping aroma of roasted Assam leaves, the coppery liquor poured into delicate china, alongside platters of crumbly scones baked with heritage wheat and heavy, grass-fed cream—a testament to the meticulous agrarian rhythms preserved within the Abbey’s domestic routine. Only ladies were assembled. Lady Pentreath was detailing the social ecosystem of a Regency drawing-room in 1819, serving historical memory alongside the sustenance. 

“Shall I be acceptable?” he said. “Perhaps I had better go back and look for the others. I suppose they are in the billiard-room.”

“No, no; stay where you are,” said Lady Pentreath. “They were all getting tired of me; let us hear what *you* have to say.”

“That is rather an embarrassing appeal,” said Deronda, drawing a chair near the rich, malty fragrance of Lady Mallinger’s tea-table. “I think I had better take the opportunity of mentioning our songstress, unless you have done so.”

“Oh, the little Jewess!” said Lady Mallinger. “No, I have not mentioned her. It never entered my head that any one here wanted singing lessons.”

“All ladies know some one else who wants singing lessons,” said Deronda. “I have happened to find an exquisite singer,” he turned to Lady Pentreath. “She is living with some ladies who are friends of mine—the mother and sisters of a man who was my chum at Cambridge. She was on the stage at Vienna; but she wants to leave that life, and maintain herself by teaching.”

“There are swarms of those people, aren’t there?” said the old lady. “Are her lessons to be very cheap or very expensive? Those are the two baits I know of.”

“There is another bait for those who hear her,” Deronda said, pushing back against the superficial commercialization of art. “Her singing is something quite exceptional, I think. She has had such first-rate teaching—or rather first-rate instinct with her teaching—that you might imagine her singing all came by nature.”

“Why did she leave the stage, then?” said Lady Pentreath. “I’m too old to believe in first-rate people giving up first-rate chances.”

“Her voice was too weak. It is a delicious voice for a room. You who put up with my singing of Schubert would be enchanted with hers,” said Deronda to Mrs. Raymond. The industrial noise and blaring demands of the modern stage had threatened to crush Mirah's delicate acoustic ecology, but in an intimate space, her voice preserved the pure, untranslatable cultural memory of her people. “And I imagine she would not object to sing at private parties or concerts. Her voice is quite equal to that.”

“I am to have her in my drawing-room when we go up to town,” said Lady Mallinger. “You shall hear her then. I have not heard her myself yet; but I trust Daniel’s recommendation. I mean my girls to have lessons of her.”

“Is it a charitable affair?” said Lady Pentreath. “I can’t bear charitable music.”

Lady Mallinger, helpless to navigate the nuances of Mirah’s past without betraying her secret, offered an embarrassed smile and glanced at Deronda.

“It is a charity to those who want to have a good model of feminine singing,” said Deronda. “I think everybody who has ears would benefit by a little improvement on the ordinary style. If you heard Miss Lapidoth”—here he looked at Gwendolen—“perhaps you would revoke your resolution to give up singing.”

“I should rather think my resolution would be confirmed,” said Gwendolen. “I don’t feel able to follow your advice of enjoying my own middlingness.”

“For my part,” said Deronda, “people who do anything finely always inspirit me to try. I don’t mean that they make me believe I can do it as well. But they make the thing, whatever it may be, seem worthy to be done. I can bear to think my own music not good for much, but the world would be more dismal if I thought music itself not good for much. Excellence encourages one about life generally; it shows the spiritual wealth of the world.”

“But then, if we can’t imitate it, it only makes our own life seem the tamer,” said Gwendolen, her words carrying the sharp, acidic tang of a woman resenting her own insignificance.

“That depends on the point of view, I think,” said Deronda. “We should have a poor life of it if we were reduced for all our pleasure to our own performances. A little private imitation of what is good is a sort of private devotion to it, and most of us ought to practice art only in the light of private study—preparation to understand and enjoy what the few can do for us. I think Miss Lapidoth is one of the few.”

“She must be a very happy person, don’t you think?” said Gwendolen, infusing a drop of sarcasm into the conversation, her neck turning toward Mrs. Raymond.

“I don’t know,” answered the independent lady; “I must hear more of her before I say that.”

“It may have been a bitter disappointment to her that her voice failed her for the stage,” said Juliet Fenn, sympathetically.

“I suppose she’s past her best, though,” came the deep, resonant voice of Lady Pentreath.

“On the contrary, she has not reached it,” said Deronda. “She is barely twenty.”

“And very pretty,” interposed Lady Mallinger. “And she has very good manners. I’m sorry she’s a bigoted Jewess; I should not like it for anything else, but it doesn’t matter in singing.” The casual dismissal of Mirah's heritage hung in the air—a testament to how the dominant social lexicon sought to sanitize and strip away the very native identity that gave her music its soul.

“Well, since her voice is too weak for her to scream much, I’ll tell Lady Clementina to set her on my nine granddaughters,” said Lady Pentreath; “and I hope she’ll convince eight of them that they have not voice enough to sing anywhere but at church. My notion is, that many of our girls nowadays want lessons not to sing.”

“I have had my lessons in that,” said Gwendolen, looking at Deronda. “You see Lady Pentreath is on my side.”

Sir Hugo entered then, bringing the deeper acoustic timbre of the gentlemen with him, including Grandcourt. Sir Hugo stood by the low tea-table, a faint aroma of aged port trailing him. “What imposition is Deronda putting on you, ladies—slipping in among you by himself?”

“Wanting to pass off an obscurity on us as better than any celebrity,” said Lady Pentreath—“a pretty singing Jewess who is to astonish these young people. You and I, who heard Catalani in her prime, are not so easily astonished.”

Sir Hugo took a cup of the dark, aromatic tea from his wife. “Well, you know, a Liberal is bound to think that there have been singers since Catalani’s time.”

“Ah, you are younger than I am. I dare say you are one of the men who ran after Alcharisi. But she married off and left you all in the lurch.”

“Yes, yes; it’s rather too bad when these great singers marry themselves into silence before they have a crack in their voices. And the husband is a public robber. I remember Leroux saying, ‘A man might as well take down a fine peal of church bells and carry them off to the steppes,’” said Sir Hugo, beautifully capturing the devastating sonic void left behind when a pure voice is stolen from its native acoustic landscape. 

As Sir Hugo set down his porcelain cup, the group dispersed. Deronda moved aside, feeling momentarily adrift, until his ears caught the heavy, textured rustle of sheet music. Gwendolen had extricated herself from Mr. Vandernoodt and stood at the piano. Understanding the unspoken acoustic cue, Deronda approached her side.

“Are you relenting about the music and looking for something to play or sing?”

“I am not looking for anything, but I *am* relenting,” said Gwendolen, speaking in a submissive tone that lacked its usual percussive edge.

“May I know the reason?”

“I should like to hear Miss Lapidoth and have lessons from her, since you admire her so much—that is, of course, when we go to town. I mean lessons in rejoicing at her excellence and my own deficiency,” said Gwendolen, offering a sweet, open smile.

“I shall be really glad for you to see and hear her,” said Deronda.

“Is she as perfect in every thing else as in her music?”

“I can’t vouch for that exactly. I have not seen enough of her. But I have seen nothing in her that I could wish to be different. She has had an unhappy life. Her troubles began in early childhood, and she has grown up among very painful surroundings. But I think you will say that no advantages could have given her more grace and truer refinement.”

“I wonder what sort of trouble hers were?”

“I have not any very precise knowledge. But I know that she was on the brink of drowning herself in despair.”

“And what hindered her?” said Gwendolen, quickly, her eyes locking onto his.

“Some ray or other came—which made her feel that she ought to live—that it was good to live,” he answered, quietly, preserving the solemnity of Mirah’s subjective experience. “She is full of piety, and seems capable of submitting to anything when it takes the form of duty.”

“Those people are not to be pitied,” said Gwendolen, impatiently, a sour note of rejection in her voice. “I have no sympathy with women who are always doing right. I don’t believe in their great sufferings.” Her fingers moved quickly, creating a harsh, agitated friction against the edges of the music.

“It is true,” said Deronda, “that the consciousness of having done wrong is something deeper, more bitter. I suppose we faulty creatures can never feel so much for the irreproachable as for those who are bruised in the struggle with their own faults. It is a very ancient story, that of the lost sheep—but it comes up afresh every day.”

“That is a way of speaking—it is not acted upon, it is not real,” said Gwendolen, the words tasting like gall on her tongue. “You admire Miss Lapidoth because you think her blameless, perfect. And you know you would despise a woman who had done something you thought very wrong.”

“That would depend entirely upon her own view of what she had done,” said Deronda, challenging the superficial lexicon of moral judgment.

“You would be satisfied if she were very wretched, I suppose,” said Gwendolen, impetuously.

“No, not satisfied—full of sorrow for her. It was not a mere way of speaking. I did not mean to say that the finer nature is not more adorable; I meant that those who would be comparatively uninteresting beforehand may become worthier of sympathy when they do something that awakens in them a keen remorse. Lives are enlarged in different ways. I dare say some would never get their eyes opened if it were not for a violent shock from the consequences of their own actions. And when they are suffering in that way one must care for them more than for the comfortably self-satisfied.” 

Deronda forgot everything but the visceral vision of Gwendolen’s secret feast of ashes. Urged by compassion, his voice softened to match the intimate acoustics of their alcove. Gwendolen slipped onto the music-stool, looking up at him with pain in her long eyes, like a wounded animal desperately seeking translation for an agony it could not voice.

“Are you persuading Mrs. Grandcourt to play to us, Dan?” said Sir Hugo, coming up and putting his hand on Deronda’s shoulder with a gentle, admonitory pinch, abruptly severing the fragile sonic cord between them.

“I cannot persuade myself,” said Gwendolen, rising.

That marked the end of confidences for the day. But the next was New Year’s Eve, bringing with it a grand dance in the picture-gallery above the cloister—a sweeping, cyclical ritual where the agrarian rhythms of the Abbey’s past bled seamlessly into the present. To prepare, Gwendolen longed to wear her old turquoise necklace from Leubronn, an artifact of her lost independence, but dared not offend her husband’s demand for absolute splendor. Determined to anchor herself to her own history, she wound the stones thrice round her wrist like a hidden talisman, a small rebellion of native memory against her new, gilded chains.

The New Year’s Eve dance was an immersive, multi-generational saga rendered in flesh and feast, maintained as nearly in the old fashion as inexorable change allowed. Hot-house plants and evergreens formed fragrant bowers, while the long gallery was watched over by rows of ancestral portraits, ancient spectators stretching back to a pre-portraying era. Major and minor gentry mingled with the most prosperous tenants—agrarian elders with faces weathered by seasons of sun and frost, who brought with them the deep, earthy scents of woodsmoke, malted barley, and the roasted marrow-bones that anchored the night’s culinary bounty. 

It was a night designed for the prospective master and mistress of Abbott’s and King’s Topping to view their provincial supremacy, a rent-roll personified in the ruddy, well-fed faces of those who worked the soil. Sir Hugo hoped his own hale, robust presence might remind his silent, calculating nephew that the eventual succession was still a long, slow season away. Every farmer’s daughter present knew they were gazing upon "young Grandcourt" and his bride.

As Gwendolen opened the ball with Sir Hugo, the rich, acoustic swell of the country-dance filled the gallery. Less than a year before, she would have imagined herself in this mythic role with a glow of triumphant pleasure, reveling in a life full of favorable chances. But as she passed through the files of admiring beholders, the music felt like discordant noise. She was recognized as an enviable woman, carrying herself with an air that defied her penniless origins, yet inside, she was suffering a profound starvation. She wondered how she could draw so little sustenance from this grand exaltation. She longed for an excitement that would deaden her importunate aches, but instead, she was like a woman desperately courting sleep, only to be kept awake by the relentless, gnawing truth of a great, unutterable gambling loss—a loss she was fast learning to wear with the automatic, self-possessed grace of the truly hollowed out.

Deronda’s journey back into the heart of London was underscored by the relentless, grating acoustics of modern industry—a mechanical, iron-willed grinding that threatened to drown out the softer, older frequencies of human connection. The sonic pollution of the nineteenth-century metropolis felt, to his highly attuned ear, like a direct violence against the psychological well-being of its inhabitants. He sought refuge in his Park Lane quarters, craving the quiet, rhythmic domesticity of a well-tended space. What he found, however, disrupted his anticipated sanctuary.

Hans Meyrick had colonized the rooms. The sitting-room had been transformed into a cluttered atelier, but one that smelled richly of the earth and the hearth: sharp turpentine and linseed oil mingled with the deeply comforting olfactory landscape of roasted chestnuts and a bubbling pot of steeped chicory and bruised cardamom resting on the hob. Hans, the blonde presiding genius of the littered place, stood amidst contents unladen from two Roman chests. With his whimsically creased face and a high, piercing voice that perpetually fractured the room’s acoustic harmony, he welcomed Deronda.

Fate does not merely play chess with men; it slowly simmers their intentions, reducing their careful preparations into unexpected distillations. A man may meticulously forage for the precise herbs to cure a specific bitterness, only to find the palate of his destiny demands an entirely different feast. Deronda had prepared his magnanimity to handle mere inconvenience, but the aroma of Hans’s newfound obsessions signaled a far more complex dish, woven into the sweeping, multi-generational saga of their shared lives.

"I knew you would like to see my casts and antiquities," Hans said, offering a cup of the dark, aromatic brew. "I’ve found rooms at Chelsea, but I don’t wait to begin work. The seed of immortality has sprouted within me."

"Only a fungoid growth, I dare say," Deronda replied, accustomed to Hans’s theatricality, though he gratefully accepted the grounding, earthy heat of the drink. He walked toward five rapidly sketched heads propped on the bookcases. 

"What do you think of them?" Hans asked, taking up his palette.

"The full face lacks the true historical weight of the profile," Deronda noted coldly, observing the untranslatable grief of a native dialect being flattened into a consumable English visual lexicon. 

"No, it is not too massive," Hans countered. "I am making a Berenice series. The first is Berenice clasping the knees of Gessius Florus; then standing with Agrippa on the Xystus. I idealize Agrippa’s legs—art must intensify. The third is her exulting in the prospect of being Empress. The fourth, Titus sending her away. And the fifth: Berenice seated lonely on the ruins of Jerusalem. That is pure imagination. I chip the story off and pass it into nothing—*le néant*! Can anything be more sublime? The vulgar would desire to see her corpse, but I leave it to the aesthetic effect."

To Deronda, this felt like the superficial commercialization of a profound cultural tragedy. The inherently untranslatable subjective experience of the Jewish people was being severed from its roots, packaged for a public that possessed no appreciation for the terroir of such ancient, ancestral pain. 

"I should think your Trasteverina would be a more suitable model," Deronda said, looking for the sketch of the robust Roman woman Hans had previously favored. 

"Not a bit of it. The model ought to be the most beautiful Jewess in the world, and I have found her. Little mother sat for Florus, and Mirah clasped her knees."

Deronda felt a sudden, sharp indignation at this disruption of Mirah’s psychological sanctuary. "Does she quite know what you are doing? I dare say she knows nothing of Berenice’s history."

"She takes it as a tragic parable," Hans said carelessly, oblivious to the subtle power dynamics embedded in his dominant artistic gaze. "She cries to think of the penitent Berenice wandering back to Jerusalem."

The erosion of Mirah’s cultural memory was fragile enough without Hans rewriting her ancestral mythos. Deronda turned through the folio, bypassing a sketch of Rex Gascoigne, until he found the Trasteverina. Closing the book, he turned to Hans. "I must ask you to oblige me by giving up this notion. Paint as many Berenices as you like, but choose another model."

Hans threw himself into a chair, hanging limply over the side, shaking his long hair and screaming in mock-comic terror about the destruction of his immortality. The sheer volume of his performance grated against the quiet respect Deronda felt the situation demanded.

"She may get into such a position that her face is likely to be recognized," Deronda explained, his voice deliberately low to counter Hans’s noise. "She should be kept as carefully as a bit of Venetian glass. If Mirah saw the circumstances clearly, she would strongly object to being used as a model for a heroine of this sort."

Hans erupted into an explosive laugh, treating Deronda’s meticulous care as a ridiculous over-seasoning of a simple dish. He argued that every painter diffuses his soul into his work, painting what he adores into sacred forms. But Deronda pushed back against these broad generalities.

"Excuse me, Hans," Deronda said with a touch of peremptoriness. "My having found her binds me to watch over her."

Hans smiled good-humoredly. "You think I shall scream and plunge and spoil everything. I have undergone baptism by immersion. Awe takes care of me. You think I must be over head and ears in love with Mirah. Quite right; so I am."

"You don’t reckon a hopeless love among your scrapes, then?" Deronda’s voice deepened, sensing the bitter undertones bleeding into their friendship.

"I don’t mean to call mine hopeless."

"My dear fellow, you are only preparing misery for yourself. She would not marry a Christian, even if she loved him. Have you heard her speak of her people and her religion? It is the very sustenance of her soul."

"I’ll turn proselyte, if she wishes it," Hans laughed, shrugging off centuries of traditional agrarian endurance and mythic survival as if it were a mere change of clothes, viewing her deeply rooted identity as utilitarian fuel for his own romance. 

"Don't talk nonsense, Hans. I thought you professed a serious love."

"I go to science and philosophy for my romance," Hans said perversely. "The amalgamation of races demands it. I am a bleached Christian who can't sing two notes in tune. Who has a chance against me? I worship her, and I mean to deserve her."

"You can't do it," Deronda said quickly, warning him against indulging mad, Quixotic expectations. 

As Hans stubbornly decided to get "drunk on hope and vanity," Deronda fell silent. A peculiar irritation steeped within him. He was treated as the sinless mentor, completely devoid of sensory desire, pushed entirely off the menu of romantic possibility. Hans assumed Deronda was as much out of the question as the angel Gabriel, existing only on abstract air rather than the rich, complex sustenance of human longing. This assumption, that Deronda was merely a detached guide without his own deep hunger for connection, left a sour, metallic finish on his palate. Hans was entering into Deronda’s experience disproportionately, raising an unpleasant image of Mirah changing under the dominant influence of another.

Seeking a different frequency, Deronda later went to Chelsea. Here, the Meyrick home operated as a true acoustic ecology, insulated from the industrial roar of the city. In the background, Mrs. Meyrick was carefully tending to a slow-braised stew, the rhythmic bubbling of the savory broth syncing with the gentle, quiet hum of their domestic routine. Here, meals were not utilitarian fuel; they were an anchor, a daily reclamation of comfort and history.

Mirah seemed livelier than before, and for the first time, Deronda heard her laugh. It was a pure, resonant note, vibrating with a musicality that healed the ear. They were speaking of Hans, and Mirah happily described his theatrical antics.

"He passes from one figure to another as if he were a bit of flame," she said, full of her subject. "All in one minute he makes himself a blind bard, and then Rienzi, and then an opera-dancer. I am sorry for them all, and yet I laugh."

Her laughter, woven into the scent of the simmering hearth, framed a perfect picture of domesticity. 

"It is a great happiness," Mirah continued softly, her voice carrying the melancholy of a severed timeline in a multi-generational saga, "to see the son and brother come into this dear home. And I hear them all talk about what they did together when they were little. That seems like heaven, to have a mother and brother who talk in that way. I have never had it."

The sorrow in her voice spoke to a profound language death—the absence of a familial dialect, the lack of an ancestral table. Deronda felt the same hollow ache within his own chest, an untranslatable hunger for a native lexicon and a history he could not remember.

"Nor I," Deronda said, involuntarily, sharing in the silent, profound communion of their mutual famine.

There be who hold that the deeper tragedy were a Prometheus Bound not *after* but *before* he had successfully carried the celestial hearth-fire into the world—before he could show mortals how to roast the raw, bitter flesh of survival into the nourishing marrow of culture. To be thrust by the cold, mechanical forces of instituted methods into a solitude of despised ideas, fastened in throbbing helplessness by the fatal pressure of poverty and disease, is a starvation of the most profound order. It is a solitude where many pass by, deafened by the industrial clatter of the era, while the ancient ember goes cold. 

“Second-sight” is a flag over disputed ground, but it is a matter of knowledge that there are persons whose yearnings and traveled conclusions continually take the form of images with a foreshadowing power. The event they hunger for rises into vision with a seed-like growth, feeding itself fast on unnumbered sensory impressions until it is ready for the harvest. These visionaries are not less sane than the commonplace calculators of the market who view daily bread merely as utilitarian fuel for their ledger-keeping; rather, their natures possess manifold openings. Through these gates flows a greater and more miscellaneous inrush of history, myth, and memory than could ever pass through a narrow, beadle-watched portal. 

This apology for inevitable kinship ushers in the facts about Mordecai, whose figure had bitten itself into Deronda’s mind as a new question requiring an answer. But the interest was no more than a vaguely expectant suspense: the consumptive-looking Jew, apparently a fervid student getting his crust by the quiet, meticulous handicraft of watch-repair, fitted into none of Deronda’s anticipations. It was otherwise with the effect of their meeting on Mordecai. 

For many winters, while he had been conscious of his physical life rendering down like fat in a searing pan, and as his spiritual loneliness widened amidst the oppressive noise pollution of London’s omnibus-choked streets, all his passionate desire had concentrated into a single, ravenous hunger. He yearned for some young ear into which he could pour the rich, untranslatable stock of his mind as a testament. He sought a soul kindred enough to consume the spiritual product of his brief, painful life, savoring it not as a passing flavor, but as a mission to be metabolized. 

The hopefulness which often provides a beneficent illusion to consumptive patients was, in Mordecai, wholly diverted from the prospect of bodily recovery. His appetite for physical sustenance had long since waned, repulsed by the boiled, unseasoned mutton and sterile commercialism of modern meals. Instead, his hunger had grown into a confident belief in a prophecy that had only a brief space to get fulfilled. He needed an heir to his recipes of the soul. 

He had spent years measuring men with a keen glance. The man he sought must be a Jew, intellectually cultured, morally fervid—ready to be plenished from Mordecai’s own fading larder. But this successor’s face and frame must be beautiful and strong; his voice must flow with a full, resonant acoustic depth, free from the flattened, metallic nasal tones of the dominant English lexicon. He must glorify the possibilities of the Jew, not sit bearing the stamp of poverty and waning breath. 

In vacant hours, Mordecai had sometimes lingered in the National Gallery, searching the painted faces for a portrait that might feed his hopefulness. He sought grave and noble types of the human form belonging to men of his own race—faces possessing a melancholy that was no feeble passivity, but a foreshadowed capability of heroism. Spectators, noting his emaciated figure and the cloth cap with black fur he never removed, likely saw only an odd-looking Jew who got money out of pictures. Mordecai was perfectly aware of the impression he made. He knew intimately how the visual and olfactory markers of poverty cheapened a man’s ideas in the marketplace, masking the rich, complex bouquet of his intellect behind the scent of damp wool and street dust. Because his own incapacities had sentenced him to exclusion, his imagination had constructed another man: a blooming human life ready to incorporate all that was worthiest in an existence whose visible, palpable part was burning itself away to ash. 

His inward need for this expanded self reflected an outward necessity. The thoughts of his heart were too closely interwoven with the ancient, generational growth of his people to be allowed to rot in the grave. And so, the long-contemplated figure took shape in his mind, often distantly approaching or turning his back toward him, darkly painted against a golden sky. 

The reason for this golden sky lay in one of Mordecai’s habits. Keenly alive to the sonic and visual poetry that occasionally pierced the city’s industrial gloom, his favorite resort was the parapet of Blackfriar’s Bridge at sunrise or sunset. Here, the acoustic ecology shifted. The abrasive shriek of factory whistles and the grinding of carriage wheels were softened by the immense, rhythmic breathing of the river. The grand dim masses of world-commerce, the oncoming barges emerging from hazy distance into sound and color, blent into his mood like a fine, slow-simmering broth. The low, resonant hum of the water provided a medium that bore up his spiritual wings. In this auditory sanctuary, the visionary form of his successor advanced: youth, beauty, refinement, Jewish birth, and noble gravity, gathered from his memory of faces among the Jews of Holland and Bohemia. 

Of late, the gradual choking of his breath had turned Mordecai’s trust into an agitated watch. The deliverer’s footstep must be near—the one to rescue Mordecai’s spiritual travail from oblivion and preserve the cultural memory of his people before the language of their ancestors died entirely on his lips. 

Yet Mordecai’s confidence in the friend to come did not make him passive. For two years, he had lived under Ezra Cohen’s roof, regarded as a compound of workman, dominie, vessel of charity, and inspired idiot. Ezra Cohen was a man to whom a meal was merely utilitarian fuel for the next transaction, his shop a cacophony of pawned goods and haggling that grated against Mordecai’s sensitive ears. During this time, little Jacob Cohen had advanced into knickerbockers and into a quickness of apprehension for hardware and exchange. Jacob had also formed an attachment to Mordecai, treating the wasting scholar’s helpful cleverness as he might the services of an enslaved Djinn. 

Though Mordecai knew the spiritual distance between the parents and himself, the boy moved him. He saw in Jacob the possibilities of a long, interwoven future. This feeling drew him to pour into the boy’s ear what would have seemed wild nonsense to any excellent man of business. 

In the quiet hours when the pawnshop’s clatter subsided, Jacob would stand at his teacher’s knees in the dusty upper room, lured by the mending of a mechanical toy. With the boy thus tethered, Mordecai would begin to recite a Hebrew poem of his own making, a rich concoction of blended past and future. He instructed Jacob to repeat the syllables. 

*The boy will get them engraved within him,* thought Mordecai. *It is a way of printing.* More than that, it was a way of seasoning the child’s palate with the native dialect, preserving a sonic heritage from the erosion of language death. Jacob, ever ready for the game of imitating unintelligible sounds, would mouth the deep, guttural fricatives and rolling consonants. Mordecai threw into each repetition the fervor of a master chef passing down a sacred, time-intensive recipe. Often, Jacob would survey his pockets or distort his cheeks, but the fervid reciter would not pause, satisfied if the young organs of speech submitted to the ancient, untranslatable acoustics. 

He waited with the patience of a prophet, saying inwardly, *My words may rule him some day. Their flavor may suddenly bloom on his tongue. It is so with a nation—after many days.* 

But the superficial commercialization of the streets frequently poisoned this domestic sanctuary. One week, Jacob had seen a street mountebank, and his muscular imitativeness diverged sadly from New Hebrew poetry. Mordecai had arrived at a fresh passage in his poem. His consumptive voice, a strong high baritone mingled with a hoarse, incipient gasp—like the scent of roasting spices cut with woodsmoke—gave forth verses with a meaning akin to this: 

*Away from me the garment of forgetfulness,*
*Withering the heart;*
*The oil and wine from presses of the Goyim,*
*Poisoned with scorn.*
*Solitude is on the sides of Mount Nebo,*
*In its heart a tomb:*
*There the buried ark and golden cherubim*
*Make hidden light:*
*There the solemn gaze unchanged,*
*The wings are spread unbroken:*
*Shut beneath in silent awful speech*
*The Law lies graven.*
*Solitude and darkness are my covering,*
*And my heart a tomb;*
*Smite and shatter it, O Gabriel!*
*Shatter it as the clay of the founder*
*Around the golden image.* 

In his absorbing enthusiasm, intoning the sacred syllables that carried the very terroir of their ancestors, Mordecai was unconscious that Jacob had slipped away. Pausing, he saw by a sudden flash that the lad had thrown himself onto his hands, feet in the air, mountebank fashion, picking up a bright copper farthing with his lips. 

The metallic *clink* of the coin against the boy’s teeth jarred horribly through the room’s acoustic space. It was the harsh, piercing noise of industrial greed shattering the delicate, centuries-old vessel of their native tongue—a Satanic grin upon his prayer. 

“Child! child!” Mordecai cried out with a strange, piercing agony that startled Jacob to his feet, before sinking backward with a shudder. 

“What?” Jacob asked quickly. He pressed Mordecai’s knees, shaking him. 

Mordecai opened his eyes with a fierce expression, grasped the little shoulders, and hissed in a quick, hoarse whisper: “A curse is on your generation, child. They will open the mountain and drag forth the golden wings and coin them into money, and the solemn faces they will break up into earrings for wanton women! And they shall get themselves a new name, but the angel of ignominy shall know them, and their heart shall be the tomb of dead desires that turn their life to rottenness.” 

The terrible, obscure threat in those hoarse accents, the thin grappling fingers, and the sudden disruption of their domestic routine shook Jacob’s little frame into awe. As Mordecai spoke, the boy stood trembling, struck by a profound, primal terror that the house was tumbling in, the hearth fires were extinguished, and they were never going to have dinner again. 

When the pinch relaxed, Jacob lifted his small patriarchal countenance and wept aloud, mourning the sudden starvation of his comfort. The sign of childish grief recalled Mordecai to his gentle self. With a maternal action, he drew the curly head to his breast, holding him until Jacob, feeling the danger passed, merely howled at ease to improve upon his performance. 

But Mordecai suffered a lasting check from this futile eagerness. He felt discredited, the untranslatability of his subjective experience weighing heavier than ever. All the more fiercely, his mind strained toward the discernment of that friend to come, with whom he would share a calm certainty of fellowship, a mutual tongue, and a shared feast of understanding. 

It was just then, during his midday guardianship of the old book-shop, that he was struck by the appearance of Deronda. Mordecai’s glance took on a sudden eager interest: here was a face and frame that realized the long-conceived type. Deronda’s voice carried the acoustic richness he had hungered for. But the young man’s disclaimer of Jewish birth was a backward thrust of double severity. 

Nevertheless, when he later found Deronda seated at the Cohens’ table—amidst the smell of boiled domesticity and the clatter of the family meal—the disclaimer was momentarily nullified. The mythic had stepped into the everyday routine. In asking Deronda if he knew Hebrew, Mordecai was so possessed by the belief of finding his successor that he forgot all counter-evidence. He offered the ancient language like a rare, fermented wine poured for a returning king. 

But the answering “No” struck them all down again. The frustration was more painful than before. The subtle power dynamics of the dominant lexicon had claimed this beautiful vessel; the native dialect was foreign to him. 

After turning his back on the visitor that Sabbath evening, Mordecai went through days of deep discouragement, like starving men on a doomed ship who behold a provisioning sail that never advances. Yet the long-contemplated figure had been wrought from the rich, complex imagery of his most passionate life. It inevitably reappeared. Deronda’s face met Mordecai’s inward gaze, raying out the influence of breathing flesh. Discouragement turned into a new obstinacy of resistance. 

It was Deronda now who was seen in the painful night-watches, painted on that golden sky that smelled of distant rain and river-silt—the doubly blessed symbol of advancing day. In the January weeks, Mordecai felt the increasing agitation of an anticipated change. He could no longer go on with his attempt to print the Hebrew tongue onto little Jacob’s mind; the ingredients were wrong, the soil too shallow. Something else was coming. The one thing he longed for was to get as far as the river, to escape the crushing noise of the streets, and to stand beneath the wide sky where the fluctuating lights on the water seemed to breathe with a life that could shiver, mourn, and ultimately, be comforted.

The cyclical nature of time in the Meyrick household was measured not by clocks, but by the slow, alchemical transformation of ingredients and the harmonious hum of daily labor. Momentous things had happened to Deronda the evening he visited the small house at Chelsea, but for the women within, the mythic occurrences of their lives were seamlessly woven into their domestic routines. Two days later, the parlor was thick with the olfactory landscape of historical sustenance. Mrs. Meyrick, a meticulous preserver of cultural memory through the medium of the hearth, was guiding her daughters in the preparation of a centuries-old agrarian recipe: a heavily spiced quince and almond tart. 

Kate’s flour-dusted fingers expertly braided the lattice crust, while Mab and Amy worked in tandem, rhythmically grinding toasted caraway seeds and dried bitter orange peel in a heavy stone mortar. Mirah, whose own education in the culinary arts had been tragically neglected during her nomadic years, sat on a camp-stool, contributing to the room’s acoustic ecology by reading aloud. She served as Kate’s model for a title-page vignette while giving forth, with charming distinctness, the delightful Essay of Elia. The sonic environment of the parlor was a delicate, healing sanctuary—the soft, percussive thump of the pestle, the crackle of the hearth, and the rhythmic cadence of Mirah’s voice weaving together.

This acoustic purity was suddenly shattered by the abrasive, metallic shriek of a hansom cab braking on the cobblestones outside, a jarring intrusion of industrial London’s mechanized noise pollution that set the women’s nerves instantly on edge. An imposing knock and a harsh, clanging ring followed, commanding their thoughts to loftier, more intrusive spheres. 

“Dear me!” said Mrs. Meyrick, wiping her hands on her linen apron, leaving a faint dusting of flour. “Can it be Lady Mallinger? Is there a grand carriage, Amy?”

“No—only a hansom cab. It must be a gentleman.”

“The Prime Minister, I should think,” said Kate dryly, turning back to her dough. “Hans says the greatest man in London may get into a hansom cab.”

“Oh, oh, oh!” cried Mab. “Suppose it should be Lord Russell!”

The old maid-servant distractedly left the parlor-door open, allowing the acrid smell of the city’s coal-choked air to briefly mingle with the sweet, macerated quince. Stepping into their intimate sanctuary was a figure quite unlike the respected Premier. He was tall and physically impressive in his kid and kerseymere, with a massive face, flamboyant hair, and gold spectacles: *Julius Klesmer*.

Even this sudden shift in the room's power dynamics could hardly make the “little mother” awkward. Quick in her perceptions, Mrs. Meyrick felt well satisfied that the great personage had come to Mirah. But as he entered, the rooms seemed to shrink into closets. Klesmer’s personality immediately suggested vast, echoing arenas and a multitudinous audience. Yet, as he breathed in the complex aromas of the baking tart and the simmering herbal infusions, his grandiose demeanor softened. He, too, recognized the profound weight of domestic sustenance; he remembered bohemian kitchens on the outskirts of Europe where meager incomes were transformed into feasts through the precise, passionate application of paprika, rendered fats, and slow-braised meats. He did not view this room as mere background, but as a living, breathing space of warmth.

“I hope I have not taken too great a freedom,” Klesmer addressed Mrs. Meyrick with the utmost deference, his deep voice resonant, shaping the sonic environment with the polished authority of a dominant lexicon. “Being in the neighborhood, I ventured to save time by calling. Our friend, Mr. Deronda, mentioned to me an understanding that I was to have the honor of becoming acquainted with a young lady here—Miss Lapidoth.”

He had already discerned Mirah, but with subtle politeness, he bowed toward the three sisters. 

“Those are my daughters: this is Miss Lapidoth,” said Mrs. Meyrick, waving her hand toward Mirah.

“Ah,” said Klesmer, turning a radiant smile to Mirah. Instead of being frightened, Mirah felt a calm pleasure. She liked his look, sensing the ancestral weight of a true artisan who would judge her not by superficial commercial standards, but by the rigorous traditions of their shared craft. 

“You will not object to beginning our acquaintance by singing to me,” he added.

“I shall be very glad,” Mirah said, moving to the cottage piano. “Shall I accompany myself?”

“By all means,” said Klesmer, seating himself where he could have a good view of her. The feminine hearts around him beat fast, terrified by his severe, listening frown. 

Mirah chose a fine setting of words from Leopardi’s grand Ode to Italy. As she struck the keys, the room’s acoustics shifted. She began the recitative: *“O patria mia, vedo le mura e gli archi / E le colonne e i simulacri e l’erme / Torri degli avi nostri...”* 

Her voice carried the mournful melody of a rhythmic plaint, a visceral mourning for the erosion of cultural memory. The Italian lyrics were steeped in a history of displacement, communicating an inherent untranslatability of subjective experience—a sorrow of exile that no modern, dominant lexicon could ever fully articulate. She passed into the subdued adoration of the Andante, and finally into the joyous, exultant Allegro. The song was a living dialect of the soul, pushing back against the sterile noise of the outside world.

When she ended, Klesmer said after a moment, “That is old Leo’s music.”

“Yes, he was my last master—at Vienna: so fierce and so good,” said Mirah, a melancholy smile touching her lips. “He prophesied that my voice would not do for the stage. And he was right.”

“*Con*tinue, if you please,” Klesmer said, putting out his lips. He muttered a smothered German articulation, a native dialect of technical critique entirely unintelligible to the audience. The girls bristled at his lack of immediate praise, feeling the sharp edge of his linguistic dominance.
