Mirah, bent on doing what he desired, went through Prince Radzivill’s music to Gretchen’s songs in *Faust*. When she finished, Klesmer rose, paced the small room, and walked back to the piano. With a sudden unknitting of his brow and beaming eyes, he stretched out his hand. “Let us shake hands: you are a musician.”

Mab felt herself beginning to cry. Mrs. Meyrick took a long breath. But straightway the frown returned, and Klesmer stretched out his long hand, back uppermost, to touch the back of Mirah’s. 

“Not for great tasks. No high roofs,” he said, his words a direct critique of the superficial commercialization of art. “We are no skylarks. We must be modest. I would not advise—I would not further your singing in any larger space than a private drawing-room. The acoustics of the grand stages will strip the soul from your tone. But you will do in the intimate spaces. And here in London that is one of the best careers open. Will you come and sing at a private concert at my house on Wednesday?”

“Oh, I shall be grateful,” said Mirah, pressing her hands together. “I would rather get my bread in that way than by anything more public.”

Klesmer made a preliminary answer in noises that sounded like words bitten in two, before stating distinctly, “I shall introduce you to Astorga: he is the foster-father of good singing and will give you advice. Mrs. Klesmer will call before Wednesday. She is a thorough musician, and has a soul with more ears to it than you will often get.” He quoted a German proverb on submitting oneself to those who truly know, which Mirah promptly finished, demonstrating her own retention of their shared cultural memory. 

Klesmer then fixed his eyes on Mab. “That young lady is musical, I see!”

Mab was a mere blush and sense of scorching. “And she has a touch,” Mirah defended her.

“Oh, please, Mirah—a scramble, not a touch,” Mab said in anguish, fearing the maestro might force her to play. But Klesmer benignantly invited Mab to accompany Mirah to the Wednesday concert, cementing his status in the girls' eyes as a benevolent deity.

Mrs. Meyrick escorted him out. “She will do,” Klesmer nodded to her in the hallway. “If she doesn’t attempt too much. You are taking care of her. She looks like a good girl.”

“She is an angel,” Mrs. Meyrick said warmly.

“No,” Klesmer smiled; “she is a pretty Jewess: the angels must not get the credit of her.”

When the heavy door banged shut, sealing out the industrial din of the street once more, an explosion of joy erupted in the parlor. Mab clapped her hands; Amy agonized over getting Mirah a new dress before Wednesday. 

“Now, now, Mab!” said Mrs. Meyrick. “Come and sit down reasonably. Let us eat, and let us talk.” She sliced into a dense loaf of seeded bread, spreading it with freshly churned butter and the rich, fragrant quince preserves, grounding their giddy excitement in the sensory reality of the food.

“I wonder why he fixed on me as the musical one?” Mab asked, taking a grateful bite of the tart, sharp fruit. “Was it because I have a bulging forehead, ma?”

“It was your way of listening to the singing, child,” said Mrs. Meyrick. “He has magic spectacles and sees everything through them.”

Mirah stood up to show her current attire. “I don’t want anything better than this black merino. Some white gloves and some new *bottines*.”

“There comes Hans,” said Mrs. Meyrick, as her son entered the sanctuary. “Stand still, and let us hear what he says about the dress. Artists are the best people to consult.”

When told of the grand event, Hans surveyed Mirah from a distance. “This would be thought a very good stage-dress for me,” she pleaded, “in a part where I was to come on as a poor Jewess and sing to fashionable Christians.”

“It would be effective,” Hans considered; “it would stand out well among the fashionable *chiffons*.”

“But you ought not to claim all the poverty on your side, Mirah,” Amy argued.

Hans suddenly became as fastidious as the society he often mocked, subtly enforcing the power dynamics of the dominant lexicon of British fashion. “It looks a little too theatrical. We must not make you a *rôle* of the poor Jewess—or of being a Jewess at all.” He harbored a secret desire to neutralize her cultural markers, to assimilate her into a more palatable, cosmopolitan aesthetic.

“But it is what I am really,” Mirah said gently but firmly, refusing to let her cultural identity suffer a silent death. “I am not pretending anything. I shall never be anything else. I always feel myself a Jewess.”

“But we can’t feel that about you,” Hans said devoutly. “What does it signify whether a perfect woman is a Jewess or not?”

“Indeed this dress will do,” Mirah entreated, looking at Hans. “It is all real, you know—even if it seemed theatrical. Poor Berenice sitting on the ruins—any one might say that was theatrical, but I know that this is just what she would do.”

Hans was overcome by a sudden guilt at this misplaced trust. The mythic circularity of the image—the eternal recurrence of the grieving woman—was profound to Mirah, but Hans knew the truth of his own canvas. “I am a scoundrel. That is my invention. Nobody knows that she did that. Shall you forgive me for not saying so before?”

“Oh, yes,” Mirah said, unsurprised by the fluid nature of historical storytelling. “You knew it was what she would be sure to do—a Jewess who had not been faithful, who was penitent. She could have no joy but to afflict herself. I think it is very beautiful that you should enter so into what a Jewess would feel.”

“The Jewesses of that time sat on ruins,” Hans said, starting up, feeling outmaneuvered by her profound interpretation of his shallow invention. 

“But the dress,” Amy pressed.

“That dress will not do,” Hans said decisively. “She is not going to sit on ruins. You must go to Regent Street, little mother. A black silk dress such as ladies wear. She must not be taken for an object of charity.”

“I think it is what Mr. Deronda would like,” Mrs. Meyrick deliberated. 

“I wish to do what Mr. Deronda would like me to do,” Mirah said gravely. 

Hans turned on his heel, taking up one of Kate’s drawings to mask his slight. “Shouldn’t you like to make a study of Klesmer’s head, Hans?” Kate asked.

“Seen him!” Hans exclaimed. He immediately threw back his head, seating himself at the piano, striking the keys with perpendicular fingers in a harsh, discordant mockery of the maestro’s commanding physical presence. But in another instant he wheeled round, looking at Mirah timidly. “Perhaps you don’t like this mimicry; you must always stop my nonsense when you don’t like it.”

Mirah smiled. She recognized the subtle imperialism in his mockery of Klesmer's foreign grandiosity, yet she answered with grace, “Thank you. But you have never done anything I did not like.” 

In this way, amidst the lingering scent of caraway and the fading resonance of ancient songs, Hans got food for his hope, blind to the deep, untranslatable currents of history that flowed quietly beneath her gentle words.

Deronda came out of the narrow house at Chelsea carrying a restlessness in his bones, a need for the rhythmic, grounding exertion of the body to burn off the heavy fumes of his temper. The city streets offered only the jarring, industrial clatter of iron-rimmed wheels on cobblestone—an acoustic violence that grated against his nerves. Seeking refuge from that mechanical dissonance, he walked toward the Chelsea Stairs, where the brine-scented Thames licked at the mossy wood. The air smelled of damp earth and the sharp, acidic bite of coal smoke. Refusing the enclosed, rattling confinement of a cab, he hired a wherry and took the oars himself, craving the physical friction of wood against water.

His errand lay downriver at Ram’s book-shop. He had missed Mordecai’s midday watch the previous day, and the desire to pull the man’s enigmatic thread had grown into a palpable hunger. The Jewish scholar was like a guardian of some ancient, endangered seed-bank, yet their last conversation had ended with a sudden withdrawal, an acoustic shutting of a heavy door that left Deronda listening to silence. As Deronda warmed to his labor, the repetitive sweep and pull of the oars became a meditative churning, kneading the water like a heavy dough. Through this rhythmic, cyclical exertion, his mindset shifted. He recognized his own utilitarian approach to Mordecai—treating the man merely as an ingredient in his search for Mirah’s family—and tasted the sourness of his own selfishness. He was foraging in another man’s life without respecting the soil from which the roots were pulled. 

*If I glean the facts I need,* Deronda thought, the wooden oars thudding softly in their locks, *I suppose I would be content to leave the rest of his harvest to rot. But perhaps we are two exiles passing on the same current, each carrying a native dialect the other might recognize, if only the noise of the world would quiet long enough for us to hear it.*

By the time the wherry approached Blackfriars Bridge, it was half-past four. The gray afternoon was boiling down into a glorious, sprawling saffron reduction, rendering the bruised purple clouds into a caramelized glaze across the western sky. The river absorbed this atmospheric nourishment, reflecting the monumental calm above with a luminous, trembling energy below. Brown sails glowed like steeped tea leaves, and heavy barges drifted out of the shadows, their passage a slow, mythic response to the turning of the day.

Deronda, flushed and steeped in the heat of his own exertion, drew his Inverness cape around his shoulders. As he fastened the collar, he looked up. There, leaning over the parapet of the bridge, bathed in the distinct, amber syrup of the dying light, was a face he could not mistake. It was Mordecai. The man was an illuminated study in bodily starvation and spiritual voracity—a fragile vessel worn thin by the abrasive, unceasing friction of a world that refused to chew upon his ancient truths. 

Mordecai had been watching the western horizon, holding the advancing boat in his gaze. As Deronda’s face lifted toward him, a visible tremor seized the frail scholar. It was as if the circular pacing of generations, the long-simmering prophecies of his ancestors, had finally boiled over into the present moment. With a white, emaciated hand, Mordecai beckoned. 

Deronda immediately signaled back. Mordecai waved his cap, his soul flooding with the deep, satiating completion of a master craftsman watching the first signs of fermentation in a long-tended dough. The prefigured friend had stepped from the golden broth of the sky; the mythic cycle had intersected with the mundane water of the Thames. 

In three minutes, Deronda had paid his boatman, trading the rhythmic splash of the river for the solid stone where Mordecai waited in absolute, reverent stillness. 

“I was very glad to see you standing here,” Deronda said, mindful of the fragile acoustic space between them, keeping his voice resonant and low. “I was intending to go on to the book-shop to look for you. Perhaps they mentioned my visit yesterday?”

“Yes,” Mordecai answered, his voice possessing the gravelly gravity of a millstone. “That was the reason I came to the bridge.”

The simple answer struck Deronda with its mysterious certitude. He searched the man’s face for madness, but found only the clarity of a deeply rooted elder. “You knew nothing of my being at Chelsea?”

“No; but I expected you to come down the river. I have been waiting for you these five years.” Mordecai’s deep-sunk eyes anchored themselves to Deronda’s with a pathetic, solemn dependency. 

“It will be a satisfaction to me if I can be of real use to you,” Deronda answered earnestly. “Shall we take a cab? You have likely walked enough, and your breath seems taxed.”

“Let us go to the book-shop. It will soon be time for me to be there. But now, look up the river,” Mordecai said, turning toward the current. He spoke in a breathless undertone, a hushed reverence that treated the bridge not as a thoroughfare of iron and commerce, but as an ancient, domestic hearth. “See the sky, how it is slowly fading. I have always loved this bridge. I stood on it when I was a little boy. It is a meeting-place for the spiritual messengers, the place where the deep waters of the past feed the present. The Masters taught that every order of things has its angel—its resonant tone carrying the full nourishment of what is afar. Here, away from the roaring engines of the streets, I have listened to the acoustic messages of earth and sky. This hour of fading light has sunk into me like water into dry earth. It was my own decline, pausing, waiting, until it brought me my new life—the vessel who will carry my harvest when this breath is all breathed out.”

Deronda stood in silence, deeply moved. His initial urge to label the man’s fervor as the delusions of a diseased organism melted away. He possessed too expansive a palate for human experience to reject a flavor simply because it was unfamiliar. The sheer conviction emanating from Mordecai nullified his outward poverty, draping him in the authority of a mythic patriarch, an elder who had stepped out of ancient agrarian lore to pass on the seeds of his lineage. 

They took a cab, suffering the jarring, industrial rattle of the city streets, a harsh sonic environment that forced them both into protective silence. Deronda felt the oppressive weight of a destiny he did not yet understand being placed upon him, but he resolved, *If I am to be a vessel for this man’s nourishment, I will not let it spill.*

Within ten minutes, they escaped the commercial din into the small, gas-lit refuge of Ram’s book-shop. The acoustic dampening of thousands of pages immediately soothed the air. The small room smelled of decaying calfskin, dust, and the vanilla-sweet decay of lignin—a dark pantry of forgotten harvests. They stood face to face, instinctively baring their heads. 

Mordecai leaned against the counter. His face bore the tragic, consumptive stamp of a forsaken temple—the yellowed pallor of his skin contrasting with his black hair and beard, his difficult breathing marking the rhythmic struggle of his nostrils. He looked upon Deronda with the yearning of a dying mother transferring the very marrow of her identity into her son. Opposite him, Deronda radiated the rich, youthful health of a deeply nourished life, his olive skin and masculine gravity offering a profound, receptive stillness. Deronda did not prejudge; he offered the rare, massive power of pure, empathetic listening, providing an acoustic sanctuary for Mordecai’s confession.

“You cannot know what has guided me to you and brought us together at this moment,” Mordecai began, his voice quiet, preserving his dwindling energy like a precious oil. 

“I am not impatient,” Deronda said. “I am ready to listen.”

“You see that I am dying,” Mordecai said. “You see that I am as one shut up behind bars by the wayside, who if he spoke to the crowds passing by, would be met only with the deafening roar of their commerce and their pity. But you have come in time.”

“I rejoice that I am come in time,” Deronda said, refusing to utter the cruel word *mistaken*. 

“The hidden reasons why I need you began afar off,” Mordecai continued, pressing his wasted yellow fingers to his breast. “They began when I was a youth studying in another land. Ideas—beloved, sustaining ideas—came to me, because I was a Jew. They were my life; I was not fully born till I tasted them. I counted my sleep, my waking, the bread I fed my body, and the sights that fed my eyes—I counted them all merely as fuel to keep the divine flame burning beneath the pot of my soul. But I engraved my thoughts in rocky solitudes. Disease came, and bound me with the iron that eats into the soul. I cried out, ‘How shall I save the life within me from being stifled with this stifled breath?’”

He paused, struggling for air in the narrow, spice-scented space. 

“Do not misunderstand me. I speak not as an ignorant dreamer from the inland valleys, who thinks ancient thoughts anew without knowing their vintage. English is my mother-tongue; England is the native soil of this body, which is but a breaking earthen pot around a fruit-bearing tree whose seed might make the desert rejoice. But my true palate was trained in Holland at the feet of my mother’s brother, a Rabbi skilled in deep, ancestral learning. When he died, I went to Hamburg, and then to Göttingen, to drink knowledge at all sources. I possessed myself of a handicraft. I learned to make needles to earn my daily bread, just as Joshua ben Chananja did after the destruction of the Temple, so that my soul could remain a temple of remembrance where the inner sanctuary is hope. I see the world as it is, and I see the world my vision will create anew.”

“I do not call your words raving,” Deronda assured him gently. “I have a keen interest in the story of a spiritual destiny embraced in youth.”

“A spiritual destiny,” Mordecai repeated, his tone correcting the English abstraction with his own visceral reality. “It was the soul fully born within me. It brought its own world—a medieval world where men made our ancient language live again in new psalms of exile. They absorbed the philosophy of the Gentile into the faith of the Jew, pressing the wine of foreign vineyards into the skins of our own traditions. One of their souls awakened within me. It tasted the dust of Spain and Provence; it heard the roar of the Crusaders and the shrieks of tortured Israel. And when its dumb tongue was loosed, it spoke the speech they had made alive with the new blood of their sorrow and their martyred trust. It sang with the cadence of their native strain.”

Mordecai leaned closer, his voice dropping to a hoarse, desperate whisper. “While it is imprisoned in me, it will never learn another language. It cannot.”

“Have you written entirely in Hebrew, then?” Deronda asked, recognizing the profound tragedy of an untranslatable life. 

“Yes,” Mordecai said, a deep sadness settling over him. “In my youth, I had the ranks of the great dead around me. But soon I found that the living were deaf to my frequency. I was left solitary. Family troubles called me to labor. The angel of death beckoned, his skirts brushing my path. I went to the men of our people—to the rich in influence, the rich in other wealth. I sought an ear for my harvest. But I found none to listen with understanding. I carried a bundle of Hebrew manuscript; I said our chief teachers are feeding the people ash instead of bread. But the scholar and the merchant were too busy listening to the clatter of the streets. Scorn stood as interpreter between me and them. One mocked me, saying that a new revelation would never answer in Hebrew in this modern world. He touched a bitter truth. The dominant lexicon has no room for the native dialect of my soul.”

“But though you are accustomed to Hebrew, few could use English better,” Deronda suggested, hoping to offer a bridge, a way to translate the man’s culinary memory into a language the present age could digest. 

Mordecai shook his head slowly. “Too late. My writing now would be like this gasping breath. If I used English, I would be beating a wooden board to summon those who only respond to the harsh clang of a factory bell. My soul has an ear to hear the compromises of its own speech. To write my truths in the language of the market would be to strip the meat from the bone. Men would smile and say, ‘A poor Jew!’ and the chief smilers would be of my own people, their cultural memory eroded entirely away.”

Despondency eclipsed him; his hands fell heavy to his sides. 

“I feel strongly with you,” Deronda said, his deep voice wrapping around Mordecai like a thick, warming cloak. “But what you have written need not be buried. Publication is within reach. If you rely on me, I will ensure it is preserved.”

“That is not enough!” Mordecai looked up, a pale flame reigniting in his sunken eyes. He stepped closer, grasping Deronda’s arm, his grip possessing the desperate strength of a drowning man clinging to a root. “You must be not only a hand, but a soul—tasting my belief, nourished by my reasons, seeing the harvest I point to! You will be my life. It will be planted afresh; it will grow. The generations are crowding on my narrow life like a bridge, and the bridge is breaking. You will take the inheritance the base sons refuse because they are too busy plowing over the tombs for gold. You will take the sacred inheritance of the Jew.”

Deronda went as pallid as the sick man before him. A compassionate dread washed over him—the terror of starving this desperate elder of his final meal, warring against the dread of swallowing an illusion and committing to a falsity. The sheer force of Mordecai’s acoustic dominance triggered a natural repulsion, yet Deronda’s exquisite empathy won out. Before speaking, he gently placed his warm palm over Mordecai’s straining, skeletal hand—a physical grounding, a breaking of bread without words. 

Then, measuring his tone so as not to shatter the quiet space between them, he said, “Do you forget what I told you when we first met? Do you remember that I said I was not of your race?”

“It can’t be true,” Mordecai whispered instantly, unshocked, feeling the sympathetic warmth of the hand upon his own. The bodily connection fortified the mythic cycle he knew to be turning beneath them. He followed his denial with a second assertion, welling up from the deep, untranslatable marrow of his conviction. “You are not sure of your own origin.”

“How do you know that?” Daniel asked, shrinking back, the acoustic harmony of the room suddenly ringing with a jarring, unexpected note. He pulled his hands away.

Mordecai relaxed his hold and fell back against the counter, his breath ragged but his eyes blazing with the fierce certainty of a man who had finally found the fertile soil for his seed. “I know it—I know it; what is my life else? Tell me everything: tell me why you deny.”

The clatter of heavily polished silver against imported porcelain struck Deronda’s ears with the abrasive dissonance of modern industrial noise. Seated at another immaculate dining table, wearing the stiff, starched white tie of his class, he felt the moral stupidity of his surroundings pressing in on him. Here, meals were reduced to a noisy, utilitarian fuel, heavily sauced to mask the absence of true *terroir*, consumed amidst a shallow acoustic environment where the commercialization of wit drowned out any profound resonance. To a young man of duller susceptibilities, the previous evening’s encounter with Mordecai might have been easily digested and forgotten, but to Deronda, it had initiated a slow, complex fermentation of the soul. 

He shrank from the cowardice of submitting to the modish ignorance around him, a society that would laugh at his intense preoccupation with a consumptive Jewish mystic. In this dominant English lexicon—a language that flattened nuance and exerted a subtle, suffocating power dynamic over the subjective experiences of the marginalized—Mordecai’s profound spiritual hunger would be diagnosed merely as madness. Deronda knew precisely the hollow, booming timbre Sir Hugo would use to dismiss the man. Sir Hugo’s words would echo with the sterile logic of the ruling class: he would describe Mordecai as a fanatic, a man whose visions were the mere delusions of an empty stomach, a monomaniac no different from the deluded inventors of perpetual motion machines who cluttered the industrial age. Sir Hugo would advocate indulging the man with a few practical comforts—perhaps a heavy, unseasoned English roast to cure him of his poetry. 

But Deronda’s ear, attuned to a deeper acoustic ecology, had caught the mythic resonance in Mordecai’s voice. He recognized the tragic, untranslatable beauty of a native dialect slipping toward language death, an oral tradition carrying the sensory memory of generations. Deronda approached human passion as a meticulous food historian approaches an ancient, nearly forgotten recipe. He knew that the grandest types of men—the traditional agrarian elders who first trusted the transformative alchemy of wild fermentation, or the ancient foragers who read the olfactory landscapes of the earth—were driven by a forecasting ardor that defied sterile, mathematical logic. If we judge the pungent, complex aromatics of a slow-cooked history by the bland standards of modern, mechanized kitchens, we risk discarding the very seeds of our cultural memory. 

What was the claim this eager soul made upon him? Mordecai’s demand was absolute, a yearning to graft his own failing breath onto Deronda’s vitality. *“You must believe my beliefs—behold a glory where I behold it!”* To accept this obligation outright would be a preposterous violation of his own subjective experience, an act of dishonesty. Yet, Deronda’s conscience was not a rigid set of fixed, mechanized laws; it was an organ of profound sensory appreciation, enlarged by his lifelong habit of imaginatively tasting the bitter herbs and sweet wines of others' lives. 

The entire weight of Mordecai’s claim hung upon a single, startling hypothesis: that Deronda was of Jewish blood. Was there ever a more profound challenge to his personal identity? Since the age of thirteen, Deronda had built his internal world around the supposition that Sir Hugo was his father. He had subdued his cravings for a deeper ancestral connection, subsisting on the familiar, if emotionally bland, diet of English aristocratic life. To question this now was to invite a phantom olfactory memory into his daily routine—a sudden, unbidden awareness of the scents of ancient spices, the rhythmic kneading of Sabbath bread, the resonant chants of a displaced people echoing through the quiet corridors of his mind. 

Suppose that wish-begotten belief in his Jewish birth was not a delusion, but the recovery of his true palate? Suppose he were to receive from Mordecai’s mind the complete, ideal shape of his heritage, like a meticulously preserved sourdough starter passed across generations, certifying a beauty yearned after but previously untraceable? In his meditations, Deronda defended this dreamy possibility against the inward objector who spoke in the harsh, staccato rhythm of modern rationalism. Poverty and the worn hands of a working man were no proof against inspiration. The Emperor Domitian and the waiting Rabbi of Rome had both misjudged the outward signs of salvation, failing to understand that the truest nourishment often comes from the humblest hearths. 

Men may dream in sterile demonstrations, cutting out an illusory world composed of rigid axioms and mechanized farming, but no formulas can save us from the imperfect apprehension of the human heart. An emotional intellect, steeped in the gustatory-rich realities of life, might absorb truths that strictly-measuring science could never taste. Columbus, driven by the sensory imagination of undiscovered spices and the acoustic pull of foreign shores, relied on a passionate patience that surmounted the hissing incredulity of his peers. Deronda refused to deafen himself to Mordecai simply because the man’s visions were clothed in a dialect the modern world deemed obsolete.

“The bare possibility,” Deronda whispered to himself, letting the words resonate in the quiet space of his private chambers. He could not admit it to be more. His life-long affection for Sir Hugo made him shrink from actively wishing for the dissolution of his English identity. Yet, the uncertainty itself became a state to be cherished—a slow-simmering broth that required time to develop its full complexity. If further intercourse revealed that Mordecai’s hopes were built on an illusion, the lack of definitive proof regarding Deronda's ancestry might serve as a necessary buffer, sparing the dying man the crushing dissonance of an outright refusal. 

Deronda resolved to let this ambiguity steep, utilizing the suspense to foster a profound fellowship, learning the nuanced inflections of Mordecai’s world while offering the sustaining comforts of friendship. These deliberate, circular meditations occupied Deronda’s mind for four agonizing days. Sir Hugo’s relentless demands tethered him to the late hours of high society, forcing him to navigate a superficial sonic environment of polite laughter and clinking glasses, rendering the evening expedition to the quiet, ancient-scented parlor of Ezra Cohen in Holborn entirely out of the question.

If there were ranks in the taxonomy of suffering, Deronda reflected—recalling a passage from Zunz he had lately digested—Israel would take precedence. For what was their history but a national tragedy simmering over fifteen hundred years, a slow reduction of bitter herbs and salt tears where the poets and actors were also the heroes, bound together in the cyclical, mythic pacing of exile? Yet as Deronda entered the Cohen establishment, he was struck by the dissonance between that sweeping ancestral saga and the immediate sensory reality of this family. Ezra Cohen bore no obvious stamp of the sublime martyr. His prosperity was a pragmatic enterprise, yet there was something profoundly typical in how the frail, breathless form of Mordecai—a living vessel of eroded cultural memory—was nested deep within the Cohens’ bustling, fragrant domesticity. 

The gladness in their faces gleamed like polished copper when Deronda reappeared. The shop’s sterile, mercantile dust was thoroughly overpowered by the permeating, gustatory-rich atmosphere of the adjacent parlor, where the deep, caramelized aroma of roasting root vegetables and the savory, rendered fat of a slow-braised brisket established the space as a sanctuary of sustenance. Cohen himself quickly noted that while keeping a pawned diamond ring longer might have yielded more coin, he cared not a sixpence for that compared to the joy his women and children found in Deronda’s return. 

Young Mrs. Cohen, apologizing that the baby was asleep but delighted Adelaide was still awake, entreated Deronda out of the shop and into the warmth of the parlor. He willingly crossed the threshold into their acoustic ecology, armed with presents: paper figures for Adelaide, and a meticulously carved ivory cup and ball for Jacob. 

At the table, the grandmother—bearing the quiet, grounded dignity of a traditional agrarian elder transposed into the industrial metropolis—was arranging a deck of cards into delicate “plates” with the children, a circular, domestic routine that felt as ancient and essential as kneading dough. A card plate had just been thrown down, landing with a soft, satisfying clap against the wood, yet keeping its shape.

“Stop!” cried Jacob, his footsteps pattering in a rapid, staccato rhythm as Deronda entered. “Don’t tread on my plate. Stop and see me throw it up again.”

Deronda complied, sharing a smile of deep, unspoken translation with the grandmother. He watched the plate endure several tossings before its structural integrity gave way, scattering with a crisp flutter. Only then did he sit, his ears attuned to the specific frequencies of the home. The door from which Mordecai had previously emerged was tightly shut, dampening the space’s resonance. 

While balancing Adelaide on his knee to orchestrate the paper figures, and while Jacob tested the wooden clack of his new cup and ball, Deronda finally asked, “Is Mordecai in just now?”

“Where is he, Addy?” Cohen asked, stepping away from the shop’s ledger to bask in the familial warmth.

“In the workroom there,” his wife nodded toward the closed door. 

“The fact is, sir,” Cohen began, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial timbre, “we don’t know what’s come to him this last day or two. He’s always what I may call a little touched—not quite so rational in all things, like you and me. But he’s mostly wonderful regular and industrious, and takes a hearty delight in the boy. This last day or two, though, he’s been moving about like a sleep-walker, sitting as still as a wax figure.”

“It’s the disease, poor dear creature,” the grandmother murmured softly, her tone carrying the mournful cadence of an old lament. “I doubt whether he can stand long against it.”

“No; I think it’s only something he’s got in his head,” said the younger Mrs. Cohen. “He’s been turning over writing continually, sifting through the old dialects, and when I speak to him, it takes him ever so long to hear and answer.”

“You may think us weak,” Cohen said apologetically. “But my wife and mother wouldn’t part with him if he was a still worse incumbrance. It isn’t that we don’t know the long and short of matters, but it’s our principle. There’s fools do business at a loss and don’t know it. I’m not one of ’em.”

“Oh, Mordecai carries a blessing inside him,” the grandmother said, a testament to her belief that spiritual and physical nourishment were inextricably bound.

“He’s got something the matter inside him,” Jacob corrected, inserting himself into the sonic space. “He said he couldn’t talk to me, and he wouldn’t have a bit o’ bun.” To Jacob, the rejection of the sweet, saffron-infused, egg-washed loaf—the very anchor of their evening sustenance—was the truest symptom of decline. 

“So far from wondering at your feeling for him,” Deronda said, his own appreciation for their culinary and moral ecosystem deepening, “I already feel something of the same sort myself. I promised to call for him here, that we might go out together.”

“That’s it, then!” Cohen slapped his knee, a sharp percussive note of revelation. “He’s been expecting you, and it’s taken hold of him. I suppose he talks about his learning to you. It’s uncommonly kind of you, sir. But there’s the shop.” 

As Cohen hurried out, Jacob offered with familiar ease, “I’ll call Mordecai for you, if you like.”

“No, Jacob,” his mother hushed him, preserving the auditory peace. “Let the gentleman go in himself.”

Skillful Jacob turned the handle with a quiet, practiced grace. Deronda stood on the threshold. The small workroom was starved of the parlor’s sensory richness, lit only by a dying fire and a single, shaded candle. Books were heaped in the corner—repositories of fading lexicons and dying languages. Mordecai sat at a high chair, his back to the door, hands resting over a propped watch. He existed in a state of sickening expectation, a prisoner listening for a delayed deliverance, until Deronda’s voice broke the vacuum: “I am come for you. Are you ready?”

Mordecai turned silently, seized his furred cap, and followed. Stepping back into the parlor, Jacob immediately accosted him, sending the ivory ball clacking up close to Mordecai’s face. The relieved tension in Mordecai’s mind allowed him to smile and murmur, “Fine, fine!” 

After young Mrs. Cohen ensured he was wrapped in his greatcoat and woolen comforter to brave the biting air, Cohen re-entered. “He’s come to life again, do you see?” he whispered to Deronda. Then aloud: “I hope this isn’t the last time we shall see you, sir.”

“See, I can catch the ball without stopping, if you come again,” Jacob promised, stamping about and singing in a chiming, nasal cadence, a pure expression of uncorrupted acoustic joy. 

Deronda knew he would eventually have to ask Cohen pointblank about the lost sister, Mirah, but he allowed the moment to pass. His initial aversion to their commonness had entirely dissolved into profound respect; the Cohens possessed a moral refinement, expressed in the warmth of their hearth and the sharing of their daily bread with the consumptive scholar.

“The Cohens seem to have an affection for you,” Deronda observed as the heavy wooden door shut behind them, throwing them into the harsh, metallic screech of London’s industrial evening.

“And I for them,” Mordecai answered, raising his voice above the grinding carriage wheels. “They have the heart of the Israelite within them, though they are as the horse and the mule, without understanding beyond the narrow, utilitarian path they tread.”

“I fear my slowness caused you uneasiness. I wished to come yesterday, but could not.”

“I trusted you,” Mordecai said, his breath catching. “But the spirit of my youth has been stirred within me, and this body is not strong enough to bear the beating of its wings. I am as a man bound through long years; brought to speech of his fellow, the joy within him threatens to overthrow the tabernacle of flesh.”

“Cover your mouth with the scarf,” Deronda instructed, deeply protective of the fragile man amid the abrasive soot and noise pollution of the streets. “We are going to the *Hand and Banner* in private?”

“No. Tonight is the club I spoke of. In new places, the modern outer world presses on me and narrows the inward vision. There, the people are familiar with my face.”

“I don’t mind the club,” Deronda said. “What sort of meeting is it?”

“We are called ‘The Philosophers.’ Poor men given to thought. Each orders beer or drink in payment for the room. I have pleased myself with a faint likeness between these men and the great Transmitters of our race, who labored with their hands for scant bread, yet preserved the heritage of memory, saving the soul of Israel alive as a seed among the tombs.”

Three minutes later, they pushed through a glazed door with a red curtain into a parlor hardly fifteen feet square. Instantly, Deronda was enveloped by a rich, evocative olfactory landscape: the thick, yeasty tang of dark malt ales, the sharp bite of cured tobacco burning in clay pipes, and the underlying warmth of slow-roasted meats clinging to the floorboards. Half-a-dozen shabbily dressed men sat in a tight semicircle. The acoustic environment was intimate and focused, a stark contrast to the chaotic commercial roar outside. 

A broad-foreheaded man with a short nose—Miller, the bookseller—was tapping his knee rhythmically and reciting Shelley’s *Prometheus Unbound*, his voice vibrating with the power of the verse: 
*“As thought by thought is piled, till some great truth / Is loosened, and the nations echo round.”*

The arrival of Deronda and Mordecai required a shifting of chairs around the hearth. It was a sober club, but the men understood the primal, sensory necessity of "taking something" to ground their philosophy in the physical world. 

“I have brought a friend,” Mordecai announced. “He has traveled and studied much.”

“Is the gentleman anonymous?” Miller asked with a robust, welcoming humor.

“My name is Daniel Deronda. I am unknown, but not in any sense great.” Deronda’s sincere smile elicited a warm, resonant murmur of approval. He ordered a glass of hot whisky steeped with bruised lemon rind and clove, and immediately opened his cigar-case, offering out the rich, cedar-aged tobacco leaves. He preferred the tactile, communal act of sharing sustenance to remaining a sterile, detached observer. 

Miller resumed his role as moderator. “This is a touch-and-go night, sir. Pash here brought up the law of progress; Lily argued that numbers are an index to social qualities; and I was just arguing for the power of ideas as the main transforming cause.”

“I don’t hold with you there, Miller,” argued Goodwin, a well-built wood-inlayer. “If you mean ideas without material, I go against your meaning. All actions men put a bit of thought into are ideas—sowing seed deep into the loamy earth, hollowing a canoe from a felled trunk, or kneading water and flour to bake clay or bread. Such ideas work themselves into life because they can’t go apart from the material that makes a medium for them. It’s the raw nature of wood yielding to the knife, or the precise chemistry of yeast rising in the warmth of a kitchen, that raises the idea of shaping it. The commonwealth outside works at change through its hands, without knowing what the philosophers are doing.”

“But if you take ready mixing as your test of power,” Pash, a vivacious watchmaker, countered, “the least practical ideas beat everything. They spread without being understood, entering the dominant lexicon and bleeding into the language without being thought of.”

“Yes,” Pash continued, his dark face lighting up impishly, “take the idea of nationalities; the wild asses are snuffing it, getting more gregarious.”

“You don’t share that idea?” Deronda asked, noting the pungent incongruity of Pash’s sarcasm against the strong, ancient heritage etched into his features.

“Say, rather, he does not share that *spirit*,” Mordecai corrected, his voice a low, melancholic frequency. “Unless nationality is a feeling—a lived memory—what force can it have as an idea?”

“Granted,” Pash smiled. “And as the feeling of nationality is dying out, swallowed by modern commerce, I take the idea to be a ghost announcing the death.”

“A sentiment may seem dying and yet revive,” Deronda argued, thinking of the untranslatability of subjective experience and the fierce resilience of native dialects. “Nations revive. We may live to see a great outburst of force in the Arabs, inspired with new zeal.”

“Amen, amen,” Mordecai breathed, his spine straightening, drawing energy from the resonance of Deronda’s words.

“That may hold with backward nations,” Pash dismissed, “but in Europe, the sentiment of nationality is destined to die out. The whole current of progress is setting against it.”

Buchan, a Scottish saddler, interjected with a thin, cool tone. “We must examine the nature of changes before we call them progress. Mere motion onward may carry us to a precipice.”

Lily immediately countered, asserting that change and progress were merged in an inevitable law of development, leaving no room for subjective human resistance. 

“I can’t see how you arrive at that certitude,” Deronda challenged, rejecting the sterile, deterministic worldview. “There remains the danger of mistaking a crushing, industrial tendency for an inevitable law. Submitting without resistance seems as bad a superstition as any false god.”

“That is a truth,” Mordecai declared, his voice suddenly commanding the acoustic space, rich with the timbre of absolute conviction. “Woe to the men who see no place for resistance! I believe in a growth, a passage, a new unfolding of life charged with diviner form. The life of a people grows—it is knit together in the sharing of daily bread, in joy and sorrow, in the specific dialect of its native thought. But there may come an arrest. The dominant lexicons may stifle our memories. Our ancient recipes, the very soul of a people whereby they know themselves to be one, may seem to be dying, shrinking into withered relics for want of common action and shared sustenance. But who shall say, ‘The fountain of their life is dried up’? Not he who feels the life of his people stirring within his own pulse. Shall he say, ‘That way events are wending, I will not resist’? No! His very soul is resistance. It is a seed of fire that may enkindle the souls of multitudes, and melt a new pathway for our memories to flow.”

After a few minutes, the unwonted stillness of the room had penetrated Mordecai’s consciousness. It was a rare acoustic sanctuary, insulated for a fleeting moment from the grinding, mechanized noise of the London streets—an industrial dissonance that daily assaulted his nerves and drowned out the delicate frequencies of human connection. He looked up at Deronda, not with bewilderment, but with a gaze full of reposing satisfaction, savoring the quiet as one might savor the lingering finish of a slow-simmered, restorative broth. Deronda rose and placed his chair nearer, closing the physical distance so there would be no need to force the vocal cords, respecting the fragile sonic environment. Mordecai felt the action as a patient feels the gentleness that eases his pillow, a sensory comfort long denied to him. 

He began to speak in a low, resonant tone, his voice vibrating with the cadence of an ancient, eroding dialect, as if he were only thinking articulately, not trying to reach an audience. 

“In the doctrine of the Cabbala, souls are not merely born; they are cultivated,” Mordecai murmured, grounding his mysticism in the physical alchemy of creation. “They are folded again and again into new bodies, much like the slow-fermenting dough of an ancestral bread, absorbing the bitter and the sweet of the earthly terroir until they are perfected and purified. A soul liberated from a worn-out husk may join the fellow-soul that hungers for it, that their earthly work may be fully seasoned and accomplished. Then they depart this mortal kitchen, leaving space for new souls to be drawn from the eternal store. It is the lingering imperfection of the souls already born—those who consume without tasting, who treat existence as mere utilitarian fuel—that hinders the preparation of the Messianic feast. Thus the mind gives shape to what is hidden. When my long-wandering soul is liberated from this weary body, it will blend with yours, and its work will at last find its true flavor.”

Mordecai’s pause seemed an appeal which Deronda’s deep attunement would not let him leave unanswered. The young man tried to make his response truthful, feeling the profound weight of this shared acoustic space. He only said, “Everything I can in conscience do to make your life’s harvest effective, I will do.”

“I know it,” said Mordecai, in a tone of quiet certainty that required no further translation. “I heard it in the very timbre of your voice. You see it all—you are by my side on the mount of vision, tasting the fruits of fulfillment which others casually discard.”

He was silent a moment or two, the circularity of time washing over him, drawing the distant past into the immediate, sensory present. “You will take up my life where it was broken. I feel myself back in that very day. The bright morning sun was on the quay at Trieste. The garments of men from a hundred nations shone like jewels, and the air was thick with a magnificent olfactory landscape—the scent of bruised figs, roasting chestnuts, and the sharp, brine-cured olives of the Mediterranean. The Greek vessel that would land us at Beyrout was pushing off in an hour. I was going with a merchant as his clerk, but in my heart, I was a historian of our people’s sustenance. I said, I shall behold the lands of the East, I shall smell the cumin and the raw earth of our ancestors, and I shall speak with a fuller vision. I breathed then as you do, drawing in the world without labor. I had the light step of youth; I could fast to appreciate the pure texture of water, or sleep on the hard, sun-baked clay. I had wedded poverty, and I loved my bride—for poverty to me was freedom from the superficial commercial gluttony that even then was beginning to rot the world. My heart exulted, strong with the strength of Moses ben Maimon. Standing on the quay, where the very stones seemed to radiate a mythic heat and the shadows cast an azure glory, I felt myself immersed in the flood of a glorious, multi-generational life. My own small year-counted existence melted into the great, continuous feast of human history. So I stood there, awaiting my companion, until he spoke: ‘Ezra, I have been to the post and there is your letter.’”

“Ezra!” exclaimed Deronda, the sudden vibration of the name striking him with startling clarity.

“Ezra,” repeated Mordecai affirmatively, completely engrossed in the sensory reverberations of the memory. “I was expecting a letter, for I wrote continually to my mother. And that sound of my true name, spoken in the dominant lexicon of the port but carrying the weight of my native identity, was like the touch of a wand. It recalled me violently to the physical body. I opened the letter. The name came again in her handwriting, a silent cry in an eroding dialect that shattered my heaven and made me yearn to reach the hearth where her sorrow burned—‘Ezra, my son!’”

Mordecai paused, his imagination arrested by the intense, almost gustatory bitterness of that long-passed moment. Deronda’s mind was breathlessly suspended, catching the frequency of a strange and delicate possibility.

“She was a mother,” Mordecai went on, his eyes cast down, “who understood the profound meaning of sustaining her kin. In her, I understood the Master who, perceiving the footsteps of his mother, rose up and said, ‘The Majesty of the Eternal cometh near!’ And that letter was her cry from the absolute depths of desolation—a pure, untranslatable agony of a mother robbed of her harvest. I was her eldest. Death had taken four babes, one after the other, an emptying of our cupboards. Then came, late, my little sister, who was the very sweetening of my mother’s bitter cup; and the letter was a piercing acoustic strike to my soul—‘Ezra, my son, I am robbed of her. He has taken her away and left disgrace behind. They will never come again.’” 

Mordecai lifted his eyes suddenly and laid his hand on Deronda’s arm. “Mine was the lot of Israel. For the sin of the father, who viewed our family not as a sacred trust but as commodities for his own base consumption, my soul went into exile. The continuity was broken. She who bore me was desolate, destitute of even the simplest meal. On the instant, I turned back. I left the aromatic sunshine, the spices of the East, and traveled into the freezing, odorless cold. I endured the harshest elements to save the scant coins needed to buy her bread. In the last stage, I spent a night exposed to the snow. That ice in my lungs was the beginning of this slow death.”

Mordecai let his eyes wander again and removed his hand. Deronda resolutely repressed the questions urging themselves to his lips. To interrupt this delicate oral history with the blunt force of interrogation would be a violence; he must listen, simply listen, allowing the narrative to unfold in its own organic rhythm. 

“But I worked,” Mordecai breathed. “We were destitute—everything had been seized by the creditors. And she was ill. The clutch of anguish destroyed her appetite and wrought with some lurking disease. At times she could not stand. The images in her brain became chambers of terror, where she imagined my sister fed on the toxic, superficial poisons of an evil world. In the dead of night, when the grinding noise of the industrial city finally ceased, I heard her crying for her child. Then I rose, and we stretched forth our arms over the cold hearth. We poured forth our souls in our native tongue, a dialect the world outside was rapidly crushing. We prayed that Mirah might be delivered from evil.”

“Mirah?” Deronda repeated, needing to hear the phonetic truth of it again, his ears attuned to the precise frequency of the name. “Did you say Mirah?”

“That was my little sister’s name. After we had prayed for her, tasting the salt of our own tears, my mother would rest awhile. It lasted hardly four years, and in the minute before she died, we were praying the same prayer—I aloud, she silently, preserving our memory in the acoustic space between us. Her soul went out upon its wings.”

“Have you never since heard of your sister?” said Deronda, keeping his voice as gentle and modulated as possible.

“Never. I know not whether she was delivered according to our recipe of prayer. The poisonous will of the wicked is strong. It poisoned my life—it is slowly stifling the breath in my chest. Death delivered my mother, and I felt it a blessedness that I was alone in the bitter, starving winters of suffering. But what are the winters now? They are far off.” Mordecai again rested his hand on Deronda’s arm, looking at him with the fierce, luminous joy of a man who has finally found the heir to his unwritten cookbook of the soul. “There is nothing to wail in the withering of my body. The harvest will be reaped. I shall do it. I shall live in you.”

His grasp had become convulsive in its force. Deronda, agitated as he had never been before—the certainty that this passionate, meticulous keeper of memory was Mirah’s brother suffusing him with a new solemnity—felt his strong young heart beating faster. He shrank from speech. To inject the revelation of Mirah into this highly charged, fragile acoustic atmosphere felt dangerous; it might overwhelm Mordecai’s overtaxed nervous system, like a deafening cymbal crash in a quiet room. His dominant impulse was to respond physically, grounding the moment in the sensory. He laid his firm, gentle hand over the one that grasped him. 

Mordecai’s hand, as if possessing a distinct consciousness of its own, relaxed and turned upward. As their palms met and pressed, flesh to flesh, Mordecai recovered some sense of his immediate physical surroundings. “Let us go now. I cannot talk any longer.”

They parted at Cohen’s door without having spoken again, merely with another pressure of the hands, stepping back into the jarring, commercial din of the street. Deronda felt a weight on him that was half profound joy, half acute anxiety. Finding in Mirah’s brother a nature so attuned to the deep, resonant meanings of life—a man who refused to view existence as mere utilitarian survival—was a triumph. Yet, the reunion of brother and sister would be the first stage of a supreme parting. 

Deronda’s immediate anxiety turned toward the environment. The superficial, noisy commercialism of the Cohens’ shop, with its abrasive power dynamics and mercantile lexicon, was a hostile ecology for a soul like Mordecai’s, and certainly no place for Mirah’s delicate reunion with her past. Deronda resolved to keep the Cohens in the background. On all accounts, he needed to provide Mordecai with surroundings suited to his frail bodily condition and his profound need for acoustic and sensory peace. 

He envisioned securing a space where the mythic could comfortably inhabit the domestic—a healthy lodging where the heavy mechanical noise of the city was muffled, where a proper hearth could simmer traditional, sustaining broths, and where the air was scented with the rich, earthy tang of vellum from his own best books. He would dismantle his own chambers, bringing his easiest chair and the bas-reliefs of Milton and Dante, to craft a sensory haven for this exiled spiritual historian. 

In this way, he set about his task ungrudgingly, thinking of Mrs. Meyrick as his chief helper in orchestrating the physical and culinary details of the transition. He recognized that heroes in the dim streets of our earthly life do not always possess their own teacups or the means to bake their own bread. But as Deronda mentally arranged the furniture and the acoustics of the prospective room, a final thought brought him a surge of restorative warmth: What could finish such a room better than Mirah’s face? And what harmony of the world could offer such profound acoustic healing to a dying man as the sweet, uncorrupted modulation of his lost sister’s voice?

The rhythmic humming of the spring earth, a subtle acoustic swelling of sap trembling into buds, seemed entirely lost to Gwendolen in the suffocating silence of her new life. True beauty, as the old agrarian elders knew, was born first as sound to a finer sense—a resonant frequency that fed the soil and the soul alike. But Gwendolen found herself increasingly deaf to these ancient cadences, her thoughts turning obsessively to Deronda. She wondered how the interwoven timelines of his days were occupied, imagining his mind as a repository of vast, multi-generational histories. With the desperate hunger of one starved of profound meaning, she sought to understand his ideas. Yet, attempting to decipher his worldview was like trying to translate a complex, indigenous dialect into a sterile, dominant lexicon; a lap-dog might as easily grasp the mythic, ancestral lineage of the wolf. 

Deronda had prescribed her more knowledge, urging her to cultivate an interest in the "best things." Seeking a recipe for this cure, Gwendolen carried a heavy, miscellaneous harvest of volumes to her room—Descartes, Bacon, Locke, Butler, Burke, Guizot. She approached them hoping to extract some nourishing broth for her spirit, but she found only the dry, flavorless husks of abstract philosophical musings, utterly devoid of the gustatory richness and olfactory landscapes that anchor true human experience. She lacked the time and the quietude for such mental digestion regardless. The sonic environment of Diplow was an oppressive ecology governed by the exacting, scrutinizing silence of her husband. Grandcourt wielded quietude as a weapon of power, his sparse, drawling vocabulary designed to flatten and erode any opposing will. To fail in her performance as his wife would be to speak a forbidden language, an involuntary confession of her soul’s rebellion, which she dreaded more than starvation.

Consequently, she performed her riding, her hunting, and her social visits with a mechanical, rhythmic precision, devoid of the sensory zest that once spiced her youth. "She disguises it under an air of taking everything as a matter of course," Mrs. Arrowpoint observed, detecting the subtle, untranslatable dissonance in Gwendolen’s acoustic presence. 

This tragic performance was most fiercely maintained for her mother. Poor Mrs. Davilow, kept at an unexpected distance, interpreted Gwendolen’s aloofness as the natural, cyclical shift of a daughter entering a new domestic season. Their interactions were reduced to hurried lunches and brief calls, punctuated by the impatient, rhythmic stamping of Grandcourt’s horses outside. When Gwendolen had previously suggested inviting her mother and the Gascoignes, Grandcourt had let a thick, deadening silence fall before employing his dominant lexicon: "We can’t be having *those people* always. Gascoigne talks too much. Country clergy are always bores—with their confounded fuss about everything." 

To hear her mother relegated to "those people" was a violence that threatened to erase the cultural memory of Gwendolen’s girlhood. Yet, bound by the untranslatability of her marital terror, she could not articulate her misery. She could only waive the subject away, discouraging her mother from taking a house near Ryelands. This contemptuous veto of her family only stoked a deep, inward craving for them. She found herself yearning for her uncle's cheerful, agrarian pragmatism, an elder’s spirit of communal management that suddenly seemed a feast of comfort compared to the chilling, nutritional void of Grandcourt’s loftiness.

It was a profound, sensory shock to the inhabitants of Offendene when Gwendolen rode up one afternoon accompanied only by a groom. The dining room was steeped in the rich, midday olfactory landscape of domestic sustenance: the steam of roasted root vegetables, the earthy tang of aged cheese, and the comforting clatter of ironstone plates. Uncle Gascoigne, the very picture of a traditional, land-rooted patriarch, went to the door to receive her. Gwendolen sprang from her magnificent chestnut, the leather of her saddle and the sweat of the beast mingling with the damp scent of the spring hedgerows. She was desperate to reassure her mother, wrapping herself in an artificial alacrity.

"Mr. Grandcourt is gone out, so I determined to fill up the time by coming to you, mamma," Gwendolen said, laying down her hat. She touched her mother's delicate head playfully. "That is a punishment to you for not wearing better lace. You dreadfully careless-about-yourself mamma!"

"Scold me, dear," Mrs. Davilow flushed with delight, immediately turning to the rituals of maternal nourishment. "But I wish there was something you could eat after your ride—instead of these scraps. Let Jocosa make you a cup of chocolate in your old way. You used to like that."

Miss Merry, a meticulous guardian of the family’s culinary history, rose at once. The preparation of this chocolate was no utilitarian act; it was a revered technique, involving the careful melting of bitter, dark cacao blocks sourced from distant, sun-drenched estates, whisked vigorously into farm-fresh milk until it yielded a thick, aromatic froth that sang with notes of roasted nut and deep, earthy spice. 

Yet Gwendolen, alienated from her own sensory appreciation by the trauma of her marriage, shook her head. "Oh, no, a piece of bread, or one of those hard biscuits. I can't think about eating. I am come to say good-bye. We are going to town."

She broke a piece of crusty, hearth-baked bread, its texture a testament to patient fermentation, but she put no morsel to her lips. She was treating the bread merely as a prop, her soul too constricted to receive its nourishment.

Uncle Gascoigne, attuned to the cyclical responsibilities of county life, leaned forward. "I wish, my dear Gwendolen, that you would use your influence to induce Mr. Grandcourt to enter Parliament. A man of his standing should make his weight felt in politics."

The rector spoke in the hearty, rhythmic cadence of a man who viewed politics as another season of planting and harvesting. But to Gwendolen, the suggestion tasted of bitter ash. Her uncle’s belief in a "wife's great influence" belonged to a native dialect she could no longer speak. Grandcourt’s ambitions, she knew, were aligned only with the superficial commercialization of power, a world where eloquent speeches were unnecessary because dominance was asserted through a sterile, unyielding silence. 

"Here comes Jocosa with my chocolate after all," Gwendolen said, escaping the untranslatable gulf between her uncle's worldview and her own reality. 

Mrs. Gascoigne chimed in with news of familial continuity, hoping to see them in London for Easter to celebrate Rex's academic honors. Mrs. Davilow, her hands resting near the steaming, fragrant cup of chocolate that Jocosa had gently placed on the table, added, "Lord Brackenshaw has been so delightful. Old Mr. Jodson is dead, and we can have his house—the low white one hidden by the trees. But we have no furniture, poor mamma."

"Oh, I am saving money for that," Mrs. Davilow said, touching Gwendolen’s hand. "And Jocosa really makes so little do for housekeeping—her care in the kitchen is quite wonderful."

Unable to bear the intimate, emotional resonance of the room, Gwendolen fled upstairs with her mother. In the black and yellow bedroom, the acoustic warmth of the house faded into a heavy, melancholic quiet. Gwendolen dropped into a chair, her face pale, the psychic starvation taking a physical toll.

"You are not well, dear?" her mother asked timidly.

"No; that chocolate has made me sick," Gwendolen lied, blaming the deeply spiced, rich beverage for the nausea of her own trapped existence. She leaned her head against her mother, attempting to mask her despair with a light, playful tone, though she felt the cultural memory of their bond slipping away, replaced by the isolating wealth of her marriage. 

"Mr. Grandcourt gives me a quantity of money, and expects me to spend it," Gwendolen said, pulling thirty pounds from her purse. It was a cold, paper substitute for genuine care, a commercial transaction masking the absence of true sustenance. "I wish the girls would spend it for me on little things when you go to the new house. They are troublesome things; but they don't trouble me now."

She departed shortly after, leaving behind the rhythmic, domestic symphony of Offendene. Riding away, she mocked her own performance, sarcastically noting her success as Mrs. Grandcourt. She inferred that her husband had gone to Gadsmere—a place he dismissed as a "dog-hutch in a black country." The very thought of Gadsmere summoned an acoustic nightmare of industrial noise pollution, a landscape scarred by the mechanical clangor that drowned out the earth's natural rhythms. 

A strange, burning jealousy gnawed at her. Why should it pain her that Grandcourt attended to the beings she had sworn to help? It was the profound untranslatability of her humiliation. She had bound herself to a terrified silence, her native voice erased by the threat of Mrs. Glasher's secret. Grandcourt, though incapable of understanding the mythic, sensory depths of his wife’s soul, possessed an uncanny, predatory divination for her resistance. He could not hear the agonizing, silent cries of her suppressed rebellion, nor could he taste the bitter famine of her spirit, but he reveled in his mastery over the quiet, desolate landscape he had made of her life.

The cycle of their days in London had returned, as inevitably as the turning of an ancestral harvest, to the drawing-rooms of Park Lane. It was only the third evening after their arrival, and Gwendolen Grandcourt moved through the white, gold, and pale crimson chambers of Lady Mallinger’s musical party, draped in pale green velvet and poisoned diamonds. For a palate newly sensitive to the bitter, astringent herbs of moral compromise, the room offered only a sterile feast. To Gwendolen, whose spirit was quietly starving, her own marriage was an act of brutal consumption—a utilitarian arrangement in which she was merely fuel for Grandcourt's tepid fires, leaving her devoid of any true, slow-simmered sustenance. 

The sonic environment of the gathering—a clattering, industrial hum of superficial chatter and the clinking of commercialized high-society—grated against her psychological well-being. But Herr Klesmer was there, a passionate chef of acoustic frequencies who understood that music was not a mere commodity to be served to the highest bidder, but a profound, nourishing broth for the human soul. He was at the piano, meticulously preparing to accompany Mirah Lapidoth in singing Leo’s *O patria mia*. Gwendolen exchanged a bow with Klesmer, sensing the untranslatable gulf between her artificial existence and the deep, agrarian honesty of the young woman standing by the instrument. 

As Gwendolen scanned the room, the olfactory landscape of her memory was suddenly pierced by the stale, oppressive scent of her husband’s secrets. She spotted Mr. Lush. He was a human pollutant, a man whose very presence violated the acoustic and moral sanctity of her world. Her husband, standing nearby and listening to Lord Pentreath, had broken his promise to banish Lush. The dominant lexicon of their marriage was one of silent, coercive power dynamics, and the sudden reappearance of this half-caste among gentlemen felt like a toxic emission in her previously carefully curated space. 

Daniel Deronda stood near the door with Hans Meyrick, acting as a silent guardian of the room's acoustic ecology. Deronda’s senses were finely tuned to the cultural environment, and he cringed inwardly when Lady Pentreath, her violoncello voice polluted by the careless commercialization of art, referred to Mirah as "your Jewess." To Deronda, this dominant lexicon was an act of violence, a superficial label that threatened to erode the nuanced, multi-generational sagas and native dialects of Mirah’s heritage. Deronda’s mind was anchored to Mordecai—a traditional elder and meticulous historian of their people's spiritual sustenance, who was starving as his ancestral language faced the quiet tragedy of death. 

When Mirah sang, the Italian words of Leopardi’s ode washed over the room, restoring an acoustic purity that cut through the smog of the elite. *Non ti difende Nessun de’ tuoi! L’armi, qua l’armi: io solo Combatterò, procomberò sol io.* To Deronda, the notes tasted of dark, mythic wine, carrying the scent of ancestral hearths and the slow-cooked marrow of heroic passion. It was the untranslatable subjective experience of a dying man crying out for a defender, seamlessly integrating a mythic, historical weight into the everyday domestic routine of a London party. 

Gwendolen, moved by a desperate hunger for this kind of authenticity, approached Mirah as the applause faded. "You must let me say how much I am obliged to you," she said, navigating the awkwardness of the dominant social lexicon. "I had heard from Mr. Deronda that I should have a great treat in your singing, but I was too ignorant to imagine how great."

Mirah, who viewed her own artistry through the lens of pure, uncommercialized sustenance, replied with native earnestness. Her speech was flavored with the deep gratitude of someone who knew what it was to truly starve. "I was poor and needed help," Mirah said softly. "And Mr. Deronda has given me the best friends in the world... I was in distress. I owe everything to him."

Gwendolen felt the sharp sting of her own malnourishment. She relocated to a settee to draw Deronda near. When he sat, the torrent-like confluences of Klesmer’s bass and treble played again, a mythic convulsion of sound that reduced the petty, superficial chatter of mortals to insignificance. But Mr. Lush lingered nearby, an olfactory and acoustic pollutant making true communication impossible.

"Miss Lapidoth is everything you described her to be," Gwendolen said, her voice constrained by the artifice required in Lush’s proximity.

"You have been very quick in discovering that," Deronda replied ironically. 

The restraint was a physical pressure, like the heavy, cloying scent of overripe fruit. They remained silent until Lush finally drifted away. Gwendolen immediately dropped the artifice. "You despise me for talking artificially."

"No," Deronda said, looking at her coolly. "I think that is quite excusable sometimes. But I did not think what you were last saying was altogether artificial."

"There was something in it that displeased you. What was it?"

"It is impossible to explain such things," Deronda answered, touching upon the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience. "One can never communicate niceties of feeling about words and manner."

"You think I am shut out from understanding them," Gwendolen said, a tremor in her voice. There was a look of suppressed tears in her eyes. She hungered for his moral sustenance, terrified of being left to consume only the ash of her own selfishness. "If you despair of me, I shall despair. Your saying that I should not go on being selfish and ignorant has been some strength to me. If you say you wish you had not meddled—that means you despair of me and forsake me... It is you who will decide; because you might have made me different by keeping as near to me as you could, and believing in me."

Before he could answer, she rose and returned to her former place. The room fell into quietude as Mirah’s voice gave forth *Per pietà non dirmi addio*. The melody was the pure, unpolluted extract of emotion. Deronda felt the agonizing pull of two interwoven timelines: the ancient, multi-generational saga of Mordecai pulling him toward a native identity, and the desperate, modern starvation of Gwendolen, whose trustful effort to lean on him felt like a drowning cry across a vast sea.

The circular pacing of the night inevitably returned Gwendolen to the suffocating enclosure of her husband's carriage. The sonic space was brutally confined. 

"Lush will dine with us among the other people to-morrow," Grandcourt said, his voice a harsh, utilitarian noise devoid of any musicality. "You will treat him civilly."

Gwendolen’s heart beat violently. Her native dialect of protest—*You are breaking your promise to me*—rose in her throat, but the power dynamics of his dominant lexicon crushed it into an untranslatable, silent plea. To Grandcourt, her submission was merely utilitarian fuel, requiring no sensory appreciation or spiritual communion. 

"I thought you did not intend him to frequent the house again," she managed in a tone of utter defeat.

"I want him just now. He is useful to me; and he must be treated civilly."

Silence fell. It was the moment when a husband introduces the olfactory pollution of his cigar-smoke into the carriage, an oppressive, suffocating atmosphere signaling the absolute erosion of her agency, establishing a toxic environment she would simply have to breathe.

In the rhythmic quiet of the Meyrick kitchen, where the scent of toasting oats and blooming yeast stood as a fragrant bulwark against the mechanical grind of London’s industrial sprawl, the time had come to prepare Mordecai for the revelation of his restored sister. Mrs. Meyrick, to whom Deronda had confided everything except Mordecai’s peculiar spiritual expectations regarding himself, had been meticulously active. She had sourced a suitable lodging in Brompton, an enclave shielded from the abrasive iron clamor of the city’s thoroughfares. Here, the brother and sister would be within the radius of her motherly, culinary care. Her happy mixture of Scottish fervor and Gaelic liveliness—a dialect of the soul that spoke through simmering broths and slow-kneaded doughs—had enabled her to keep the secret close. Any premature betrayal to her daughters or Hans would likely reach Mirah and disrupt the delicate acoustic harmony of the girl’s newly established independence. 

Both Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda knew that the term "independence" was a word borrowed from a dominant, patriarchal lexicon, arbitrarily applied to the arduous, dignified labor of self-sustenance. Perhaps "the little mother" was aided in her secrecy by a certain dissonance in her feelings about this remarkable brother. The sorrowful fact of his consumption appealed to her ingrained instinct to nourish and heal, yet how was she to rejoice in a fervor she could only translate as a rigid, untranslatable pertinacity? She feared the introduction of a man whose ancient, mythic cadences would clash with their modern domestic symphony, sounding no more encouraging than the archaic zeal of Scott’s Covenanters. Her mind was hardly prosaic; she possessed a deep, sensory appreciation for the romance of Mirah’s history, but the romantic requires adaptation when brought to the breakfast table. We may revere the sweeping, multi-generational sagas of St. Francis or Oliver Cromwell, but to have them disrupt the morning ritual of pouring steeped tea is quite another affair. She had secretly hoped Mirah’s Judaism would gently dissolve into their own daily rhythms, rather than being plunged back into the deep, ancestral dye by a newly discovered brother.

"I am as glad as you are that the pawnbroker is not her brother," Mrs. Meyrick confessed, her hands expertly shaping a loaf, flour dusting her wrists. "There are Ezras and Ezras. It is a comfort that not all are like those shopkeepers who trap you in their commercial chatter. And what he said to you of his mother and sister makes me bless him. But I never did like anything fanatical. I heard too much loud preaching in my youth—it was noise pollution to the spirit, and I entirely lost my palate for it."

"You will not find that Mordecai obtrudes any preaching," Deronda replied, sensitive to the sonic environment she cherished. "He is not what I should call fanatical. That is a sterile word. I call a man fanatical when his enthusiasm is narrow, deaf to the acoustic ecology of the world, unjust to those outside his own track. Mordecai is an enthusiast of the highest order—a mind caring supremely for the grand sustenance of mankind. His orthodoxy is not rigid; his conformity is a compassionate allowance for the condition of his people. The Cohens love him, even if his profound ideas remain entirely untranslatable to them."

"Well, I can live up to the level of the pawnbroker’s mother, and like him for what I see," she said, wrapping the dough in a damp linen cloth. "Mirah’s brother will have good bedding, and I shall paste the extra window with heavy paper to seal out the abrasive drafts and the clatter of the street. It is a comfort that the landlords are no hypocritical harpies. Once the children know, we shall make the rooms a true sanctuary."

"The next stage is to tell Mordecai, and get him to move," Deronda said.

"Will you tell Mirah before I tell the children?" Mrs. Meyrick paused, her tone taking on a persuasive deliberation. "No, let me tell Hans and the girls the evening before, so they will be away the next morning."

"Yes. But do justice to Mordecai—or Ezra. Do not assist their imagination by comparing him to Habakkuk Mucklewrath," Deronda smiled, noting how easily the dominant lexicon rushed to marginalize foreign ways of being. 

"Trust me," said the little mother. "I shall have to persuade them so hard to be glad, that I shall convert myself. When I am frightened, I find a little anger warms the blood like good ginger."

Deronda might have argued further had he not been so anxiously preoccupied with the delicate task ahead. He had been meeting Mordecai at the *Hand and Banner*, but the public house's din was ill-suited for profound revelations. He had written to Mordecai, requesting an hour in his workroom—a space where the mythic could safely intersect with the domestic—hoping to create a quiet, preparatory expectation.

When Deronda arrived at the Cohen shop, he left the harsh, industrial scrape of the London pavement for an interior rich with olfactory history. Beneath the superficial scent of brass polish and old pawned coats lay the deep, sustaining aroma of Mrs. Cohen's kitchen—schmaltz, slow-braising onions, and toasted cumin—a culinary tether to an agrarian past long geographically lost. He was received with the usual friendliness and a silent, wondering respect from the elders. 

Jacob, however, broke the quiet. Scurrying up to Deronda’s knee, he demanded, "What do you want to talk to Mordecai about?"

"Something very interesting to him," Deronda said, pinching the lad’s ear, "but that you can't understand."

"Can you say this?" Jacob challenged. He immediately launched into a string of rote-learned Hebrew verses. The boy delivered the ancient syllables with a mixture of throaty friction and nasal projection. To Deronda, it was a poignant display of the erosion of cultural memory—the profound, mythic weight of a dying language reduced to a parlor trick in a commercialized world, yet still possessing a raw, acoustic power that altered the air in the room.

"No, really," Deronda said, his face grave in the presence of this linguistic ghost. "I can't say anything like it."

"I thought not," Jacob said, dancing a triumph on small scarlet legs, pulling various trinkets from his knickerbockers to assert his commercial dominance, before running to the workroom door. "Mordecai, here’s the young swell!" he shouted, thoughtlessly adopting his father’s superficial slang to cap the recitation of sacred Hebrew.

Hushed by his mother and grandmother, Jacob retreated. Deronda entered the workroom and closed the door, instantly sealing out the shop’s mercantile chatter. The acoustic shift was profound. Here, a bit of carpet had been laid down, a chair placed, and the fire tended—a ritualistic preparation of space. Mordecai rose, his face pale with solemn expectation, as if Deronda’s letter had heralded a miraculous, cross-generational alignment. 

Deronda drew the chair to the hearth. Neither spoke at first. The silence between them was not a void, but a rich, resonant medium of shared understanding. Finally, Mordecai spoke in a tone of fervid certainty.

"You are coming to tell me something that my soul longs for."

"I have something very weighty to tell you—something I trust you will rejoice in," Deronda said gently, guarding against the heartbreaking untranslatability of Mordecai’s deepest hopes.

"It is all revealed," Mordecai leaned forward, hands clasped. "You are even as my brother that sucked the breasts of my mother—the heritage is yours—there is no doubt to divide us."

"I have learned nothing new about myself," Deronda said. The disappointment was an inevitable dissonance; it was better to sever the false chord immediately. 

Mordecai sank back, the sudden silence in the room turning sickeningly hollow. The entire day his mind had been tuned to one mythic frequency. He closed his eyes.
