"Except," Deronda continued softly, his voice a soothing balm, "except that I had really some time ago come into another sort of hidden connection with you."

Mordecai's eyelids fluttered. 

"I had made the acquaintance of one in whom you are interested. One who is closely related to your departed mother," Deronda said, navigating the delicate power dynamics of the revelation. Noticing Mordecai shrink back, he added, "Whom she and you held dear above all others."

Mordecai started, laying a spasmodic grasp on Deronda’s wrist. A great terror vibrated within him. Deronda’s clear tones held a perceptible tremor. 

"What was prayed for has come to pass: Mirah has been delivered from evil."

Mordecai’s grasp relaxed. He panted with a tearless sob. 

"Your sister is worthy of the mother you honored," Deronda finished. 

Throwing himself backward, Mordecai closed his eyes and began to whisper almost inaudibly in Hebrew. It was a private, subjective experience of overwhelming grace, inherently untranslatable into English. Deronda watched the new suffused sweetness on the man’s face, a serene echo of the beautiful dead. In that moment of ancestral connection, Deronda saw, for the first time, a clear, physical resemblance to Mirah. 

When Mordecai was ready, Deronda narrated the rest, carefully filtering the harshness of the father’s conduct and omitting Mirah’s attempt to drown herself. He focused instead on the sensory and emotional reality of her yearning for her family, and the safe, nurturing hearth she had found with the Meyricks. 

"It was in search of them," Deronda smiled, "that I turned into this house. I cared very much what I should find Mirah’s friends to be. I found a brother worthy of her when I knew Ezra was disguised under the name Mordecai."

"Mordecai is really my name—Ezra Mordecai Cohen."

"Is there any kinship between this family and yours?" Deronda asked.

"Only the kinship of Israel," Mordecai replied. "My soul clings to these people, who have given me succor out of the affection that abides in Jewish hearts, as sweet odor in things long crushed and hidden from the outer air. It is good for me to bear with their ignorance, to keep in mind the spiritual poverty of the Jewish million, and not put impatient knowledge in the stead of loving wisdom."

"But you do not feel bound to continue with them now?" Deronda urged. "You should live with your sister. I have prepared a home, insulated and quiet, where she may join you. You shall have books, and I shall take you to see the river and the trees, to hear the natural world. This is the claim I make on you."

Deronda pleaded with the affectionate earnestness of an elder brother. Mordecai looked at him with listening contemplation, absorbing the timbre of Deronda's voice as much as the words. Then, with almost reproachful emphasis, he said:

"And you would have me hold it doubtful whether you were born a Jew! Have we not from the first touched each other with invisible fibres—have we not quivered together like the leaves from a common stem? I know what I am outwardly—stricken, dying. But our souls know each other. All their speech is understanding. The life of Israel is in your veins."

Deronda sat perfectly still, his face tingling. He could neither deny nor assent to this profound assertion of shared blood and language. He waited. 

Finally, Mordecai said firmly, "What you wish of me I will do. I will accept what your loving kindness has prepared. But I shall grieve to part from these parents and the little ones. You must tell them, for my heart would fail me."

"Shall we go now at once?" Deronda asked, relieved. 

"Yes. But do not dwell on my sister more than is needful."

They entered the parlor. The firelight cast a rich, amber glow over the room, illuminating the dark gems of eyes that looked kindly upon one another. The scene was a portrait of domestic sustenance: on one side, the baby lay under a scarlet quilt, rocked by the young mother; Adelaide Rebekah sat on the grandmother’s knee; and Jacob stood between his father’s legs. 

"Ask your father to come, and tell Sarah to mind the shop," Mordecai said to Jacob. "My friend has something to say."

When Mr. Cohen entered, pen behind his ear, the harsh commercial world intruded upon the sanctuary. "Well, sir! Glad you’re doing us the honor to join our family party," he said, rubbing his hands. 

"I have just been telling Mordecai of an event that makes a great change in his life," Deronda began. "A very precious relation wishes to be reunited to him—a good and lovely young sister. A home is already provided for him."

"Relations with money, sir?" Cohen burst in, his dominant lexicon of profit overriding the delicate emotional frequency of the room. 

"No," Deronda smiled. "But with talents which will secure her a maintenance."

A profound silence fell, heavy with impending loss, before the grandmother wailed in a tone of ancient, untranslatable grief, "Well, well! And so you’re going away from us, Mordecai."

"And where there’s no children as there is here," the mother caught the wail, mourning the sudden rupture of their domestic ecosystem. 

"Ay, ay, Jacob’s learning ’ill all wear out of him," Cohen said with grim decision. "He must go to school. It’ll be hard times for Jacob."

To Jacob, the phrase "hard times" sounded like a doom. The wordless, acoustic reality of Mordecai’s absence crashed over him, and he broke into loud lamentation. Adelaide Rebekah immediately howled in sympathy, and the awakened baby contributed angry screams. The room’s serene ecology dissolved into a chaotic symphony of grief. Mordecai, pierced by the cries, put out his arms. Jacob ran to him, burying his black head against Mordecai’s chest.

Cohen, feeling the patriarchal need to impose order over this emotional spillage, squared his elbows. "It’s not as we’re the people to grudge anybody’s good luck, sir," he addressed Deronda loudly. "If I’m offal, let a wise man tell me. In point of business, I’m not a class of goods to be in danger. You’re taking some of our good works from us, which is property bearing interest, but we can afford that. A Jew must not be like a servant who works for reward—though I see nothing against a reward if I can get it. But the truth is, the women and children are fond of Mordecai. A child-bearing, tender-hearted thing is the woman of our people. So you must excuse present company for not being glad all at once."

Before Deronda could navigate this strange collision of mercantile defensiveness and genuine sorrow, Mordecai exclaimed, "Friends, friends! For food and raiment and shelter I would not have sought better. You have sweetened the morsel with love. It has been taught us that the reward of one duty is the power to fulfill another. You have made your duty to one of the poor a joy; your reward shall be the joy of like deeds in the time to come. And may not Jacob come and visit me?"

"Surely," Deronda said. "It is no further than Brompton."

The word "visit" functioned as a magical invocation for Jacob, translating into a vision of cakes and leisurely afternoons. He danced away, his sorrow instantly evaporating.

The grandmother, however, possessed the meticulous foresight of a true steward of ancestral sustenance. She sighed, a sound laden with generations of cultural preservation. "I hope there’ll be nothing in the way of your getting *kosher* meat, Mordecai. For you’ll have to trust to those you live with." It was not merely a question of diet, but a profound anxiety over the erosion of his identity, an acknowledgment that the most sacred components of their worldview were maintained daily at the butcher's block and the hearth.

"That’s all right, mother," Cohen waved off the inquiry, uneasy with testing Deronda's standing on such intimate, rigorous matters. "So, sir," he added to Deronda with an amused look of commercial enlightenment, "it was better than learning you had to talk to Mordecai about! I thought somehow there was a something."

"Mordecai will perhaps explain to you how it was that I was seeking him," said Deronda, feeling the deep acoustic resonance of the evening settling into its final chord. He rose to take his leave, carrying with him the fragile, interwoven threads of their shared history.

One might be tempted to envy Deronda the sensory satisfaction of providing new clothes for Mordecai, pleasing himself as though he were blending the deep, earthy spices of a complex dish. He had selected fine gray flannel shirts that smelled of raw lanolin and clean starch, pairing them with a dressing-gown the rich, roasted hue of crushed chicory root, picturing Mordecai’s noble head and neck rising above them. Half his pleasure was the anticipation of feeding Mirah’s fervid joy, securing her from any perturbing, bitter impressions. And yet, as he laid out the woven garments, a profound doubt settled in his palate. Was he not substituting a shallow, sanitized flavor for a deeper, more resonant one? Was she not just as capable as he of tasting the impressive, ancient distinction in her brother precisely *because* of that aspect of poverty, which lingered like the scent of woodsmoke and bitter herbs—the very memorials of his past?

But there was the Meyrick household to be propitiated toward this intensely Judaic brother. Deronda detected in himself a capitulation to the dominant English lexicon of respectability, hiding away everything that might provoke the ready repugnance of palates unaccustomed to such robust, ancestral fare. He was trying to translate Mordecai into a safer dialect, knowing well that such translation often erodes the authentic cultural memory embedded in the original.

His inclination to protect Mordecai’s raw, untranslatable dignity would have been fiercely confirmed had he heard the dissonance echoing around Mrs. Meyrick’s fire late that evening, after Mirah had retired. Hans, now settled in his Chelsea rooms, had stayed late. The Meyrick hearth was a sanctuary of acoustic and culinary warmth, defending against the harsh, industrial clatter of modern London. Mrs. Meyrick, poking the fire to coax a steady, rhythmic crackle beneath a simmering iron pot of cracked barley and root vegetables, said, “Now, Kate, put out your candle, and all come round the fire cosily. Hans, dear, do leave off laughing at those poems for the ninety-ninth time, and come too. I have something wonderful to tell.”

“As if I didn’t know that, ma. I have seen it in the corner of your eye ever so long, and in your pretense of errands,” said Kate. The girls gathered, bringing the scent of yeast and mending-wool to the fender. Hans, pushing his chair near them, sat astride it, resting his fists and chin on the back, entirely disconnected from the slow, nourishing rhythm of the room.

“Well, then, if you are so wise, perhaps you know that Mirah’s brother is found!” said Mrs. Meyrick, her clear accents ringing with the purity of struck crystal.

“Oh, confound it!” said Hans, in the very same breath, introducing a jarring, discordant note into the harmony.

“Hans, that is wicked,” said Mab. “Suppose we had lost you?”

“I *cannot* help being rather sorry,” said Kate. “And her mother?—where is she?”

“Her mother is dead.”

“I hope the brother is not a bad man,” said Amy.

“Nor a fellow all smiles and jewelry—a Crystal Palace Assyrian with a hat on,” said Hans in the worst humor, his words dripping with the superficial commercialization of art that so often commodified and hollowed out true cultural lineage. 

“Were there ever such unfeeling children?” said Mrs. Meyrick, strengthened by the need to defend the sacred, mythic nature of this familial reunion. “You don’t think the least bit of Mirah’s joy in the matter.”

“You know, ma, Mirah hardly remembers her brother,” said Kate.

“People who are lost for twelve years should never come back again,” said Hans, his voice a flat, sterile intrusion. “They are always in the way.”

“Hans!” said Mrs. Meyrick, reproachfully. “If you had lost me for *twenty* years, I should have thought—”

“I said twelve years,” Hans broke in, aggressively talking over her. “Anywhere about twelve years is the time at which lost relations should keep out of the way.”

“Well, but it’s nice finding people—there is something to tell,” said Mab, clasping her knees, sensing the sweeping, generational saga unfolding. “Did Prince Camaralzaman find him?”

Then Mrs. Meyrick, in her neat, narrative way, ladled out the story, letting the rich broth of the events speak for themselves without interruption. “Mr. Deronda has the highest admiration for him,” she ended—“seems quite to look up to him. And he says Mirah is just the sister to understand this brother.”

“Deronda is getting perfectly preposterous about those Jews,” said Hans with disgust, rising and setting his chair away with a violent bang that bruised the quiet ecology of the parlor. “He wants to do everything he can to encourage Mirah in her prejudices.”

“Oh, for shame, Hans!—to speak in that way of Mr. Deronda,” said Mab. And Mrs. Meyrick’s face showed something like an under-current of expression not allowed to get to the surface, a silent refusal of her son’s toxic lexicon.

“And now we shall never be all together,” Hans went on, walking about with his hands thrust into the pockets of his brown velveteen coat. “But we must have this prophet Elijah to tea with us, and Mirah will think of nothing but sitting on the ruins of Jerusalem. She will be spoiled as an artist—mind that—she will get as narrow as a nun. Everything will be spoiled—our home and everything. I shall take to drinking.”

“Oh, really, Hans,” said Kate, impatiently. “I do think men are the most contemptible animals in all creation. Every one of them must have everything to his mind, else he is unbearable.”

“Oh, oh, oh, it’s very dreadful!” cried Mab. “I feel as if ancient Nineveh were come again.”

“I should like to know what is the good of having gone to the university and knowing everything, if you are so childish, Hans,” said Amy. “You ought to put up with a man that Providence sends you to be kind to. *We* shall have to put up with him.”

“I hope you will all of you like the new Lamentations of Jeremiah—‘to be continued in our next’—that’s all,” said Hans, seizing his wide-awake hat. “It’s no use being one thing more than another if one has to endure the company of those men with a fixed idea, staring blankly at you, and requiring all your remarks to be small foot-notes to their text. If you’re to be under a petrifying wall, you’d better be an old boot. I don’t feel myself an old boot.” Then abruptly, “Good night, little mother,” bending to kiss her brow in a hasty, desperate manner, and condescendingly, on his way to the door, “Good-night, girls.”

“Suppose Mirah knew how you are behaving,” said Kate. 

But his only answer was the slam of the door—a harsh, industrial percussion that severed the room's acoustic tranquility and spoke to his total inability to digest the profound sustenance of Mirah’s unfolding history. 

“I *should* like to see Mirah when Mr. Deronda tells her,” she went on to her mother, restoring the gentle murmur of the hearth. “I know she will look so beautiful.”

But Deronda, on second thoughts, had written a letter which Mrs. Meyrick received the next morning alongside the scent of her freshly baking morning loaves. He begged her to make the revelation instead of waiting for him, shrinking from going through a narrative where he might seem to be assigning himself the role of the grand savior. He wished to remain with Mordecai while Mrs. Meyrick brought Mirah on what was to be understood as a visit. 

Deronda secretly felt a wondering anxiety. Mordecai, after years of solitary, internal sustenance—feeding on ideas that had become his only daily bread as his bodily strength diminished—might find the sudden intrusion of deep emotional ties difficult to swallow. Mordecai was like a traditional agrarian elder, attuned only to the slow, generational rhythms of his faith. He asked no new questions about Mirah, maintaining an unusual, resonant silence. He donned the new flannel and chicory-brown clothes obediently, but said afterward to Deronda, with a faint smile, “I must keep my old garments by me for a remembrance.” He understood that to discard the threadbare fabric of his past was to actively participate in the erosion of his own cultural memory.

As they sat awaiting Mirah, Mordecai uttered no word. He kept his eyelids closed, dwelling in a profound acoustic quiet, though his hands showed restless feeling. He was undergoing that peculiar nervous perturbation known only to those whose minds, having flowed long in one deep, ancient riverbed, are suddenly compelled into a reopened channel. Joy was approaching, but joy, too, carried the terrifying weight of a mythic occurrence breaking into the everyday.

Deronda felt the infection of this quiet excitement. When he heard the ring at the door, he went out to greet Mirah. He was startled to find that she had foregone any polished presentation; she wore the hat and cloak in which he had first seen her—the memorable cloak that smelled faintly of river-damp and old, wetted wool, the olfactory landscape of her lowest ebb. 

When Mrs. Meyrick had asked, “You like to go in that dress, dear?” Mirah had answered, “My brother is poor, and I want to look as much like him as I can, else he may feel distant from me.” 

Deronda felt secretly ashamed of his own fastidious arrangements, recognizing that Mirah’s instinct to match her brother's physical reality was far truer than his attempt to dress it up. They shook hands silently. Mirah looked pale and awed.

When Deronda opened the door, Mordecai had risen. Mirah took only two or three steps, and then stood still. They looked at each other, motionless. It was less their own presence they felt than the weight of a multi-generational saga converging in this single room. Touch was no union compared to the interwoven timelines of their shared memory.

Mirah was the first to break the silence. She spoke a single word, slicing through the years of separation with the pure, uncorrupted dialect of their childhood.

“Ezra,” she said, in exactly the same tone, the same phonetic shape, as when she was telling of their mother’s call to him.

Mordecai, with a sudden movement, advanced and laid his hands on her shoulders. He looked down at her tenderly. “That was our mother’s voice. You remember her calling me?”

“Yes, and how you answered her—‘Mother!’—and I knew you loved her.” 

Mirah threw her arms round her brother’s neck, kissing him with childlike lavishness. Her hat fell backward, disclosing all her curls.

“Ah, the dear head, the dear head!” said Mordecai, laying his thin, artisan’s hand gently on her hair.

“You are very ill, Ezra,” said Mirah, sadly, taking in the fragile physical reality of him.

“Yes, dear child, I shall not be long with you in the body,” was the quiet answer.

“Oh, I will love you and we will talk to each other,” said Mirah, the words pouring from her as sweet and spontaneous as wild honey. “I will tell you everything, and you will teach me:—you will teach me to be a good Jewess—what she would have liked me to be. I shall always be with you when I am not working. For I work now. I shall get money to keep us. Oh, I have had such good friends.”

Mirah turned with the prettiest attitude, keeping one hand on her brother’s arm while she looked at Mrs. Meyrick and Deronda. The little mother’s happy emotion in witnessing this reclamation of a dying family lexicon had already won her to Mordecai, who possessed a deep, inherent dignity far beyond Deronda's initial descriptions.

“See this dear lady!” said Mirah. “I was a stranger, a poor wanderer, and she believed in me, and has treated me as a daughter. Please give my brother your hand,” she added beseechingly, pressing Mrs. Meyrick’s hand into Mordecai’s, then lifting them both to her lips.

“The Eternal Goodness has been with you,” said Mordecai, his voice a low, echoing blessing that enriched the very air of the room. “You have helped to fulfill our mother’s prayer.”

“I think we will go now, shall we?—and return later,” said Deronda, laying a gentle pressure on Mrs. Meyrick’s arm. She immediately complied. He was afraid of introducing his own narrative into their sacred space, and he felt no uneasiness now in leaving the brother and sister alone together, free to share the untranslatable sustenance of their native tongue.

Grandcourt’s power lay not in loud declarations but in the oppressive acoustic dampening he brought to every room, a stifling of the world’s natural resonance. In their London townhouse, the sonic environment was a meticulously curated silence, occasionally punctured by the jarring, industrial clatter of carriage wheels on the cobblestones outside—a mechanical noise pollution that set Gwendolen’s teeth on edge. The couple sat at breakfast, the heavy mahogany table laid out like a tapestry of culinary history, though Grandcourt treated the elaborate spread with the sterile, utilitarian detachment of a man merely stoking an engine. Before him sat a plate of thinly sliced, cold-smoked venison—cured over peat fires according to ancient agrarian methods—which he consumed mechanically, entirely deaf to the symphony of woodsmoke and juniper berry that rose from the meat. 

Gwendolen dallied over her plate of wild-caught Atlantic prawns. They had been precisely poached in a fragrant court-bouillon of wild fennel, lemon peel, and crushed coriander seeds. The olfactory landscape of the dish should have transported her to brine-swept coastlines, a mythic return to the sea’s cyclical rhythms, but the dominant lexicon of her marriage had flattened her appetite. Her husband’s negative mind was as diffusive as fog, a thick smog that clung to all objects and spoiled all sensory contact.

“I think of making myself accomplished while we are in town,” Gwendolen said, seeking a variation in her acoustic and gustatory ennui. She toyed with a prawn, tracing the delicate curve of its shell. “I cannot simply eat *pâté de foie gras*—however rich with the iron tang of goose liver and the deep earthiness of black truffles—merely to make myself sleepy. I want to hire that little Jewess, Miss Lapidoth, for singing lessons. When would be a convenient time?”

Grandcourt pushed his plate away, abandoning the artisanal meats, and leaned back, his eyes half-closed. “Whenever you like. Though I don’t see why a lady should sing. Amateurs make fools of themselves. One doesn’t want to hear squalling in private.” 

“I like frankness,” Gwendolen said, lifting a prawn and looking at its boiled ingenuousness, preferring its unseeing eyes to her husband's lizard-like stare. “I suppose you don’t object to Miss Lapidoth singing at our party on the fourth? Lady Brackenshaw engaged her. And Mr. Deronda, who is a first-rate judge of acoustics, says there is no singing in such pure, uncommercialized taste for a drawing-room.” 

“It’s very indecent of Deronda to go about praising that girl,” Grandcourt drawled. His tone was a discordant frequency, a subtle exercise in power designed to violate the room’s fragile acoustic ecology. 

“Indecent!” Gwendolen reddened. The word landed like a clanging iron bell.

“Yes,” Grandcourt said. “Especially when she is patronized by Lady Mallinger. Men can see what is his relation to her.”

“Men who judge of others by themselves,” Gwendolen whispered, turning white. The untranslatability of her sudden revulsion choked her. 

“Of course. And a woman should take their judgment,” Grandcourt replied, using his dominant vocabulary like pincers on her mind. “I suppose you take Deronda for a saint.”

“Only a little less of a monster,” she said in a high, hard tone. 

She pushed away her chair and walked out of the room, her stomach churning as if she had swallowed ash. Upstairs, locking her dressing-room doors, the psychological toll of his insinuation manifested as a distinctly physical nausea. The memory of Deronda’s character, which she had previously consumed like a rare, clarifying broth of moral truth, suddenly tasted of rancid fat and industrial soot. The sonic environment of her mind became a cacophony of street-gas hisses and grinding gears. But the suffocation was intolerable. A mythic urge for truth—a desperate need to touch the pure source of the waters before they were polluted by her husband's cynical lexicon—pushed through the gray morning. 

She ordered the carriage and rode through the noisy, smoke-choked streets of London, seeking Mirah Lapidoth’s lodgings. 

When she arrived, the sensory environment shifted the moment she stepped inside. Behind the folding doors of the parlor, Gwendolen heard the low, resonant timbre of Deronda’s voice reading Hebrew with Mirah’s consumptive brother. The cadence of the ancient, native dialect reverberated through the floorboards, rich with guttural warmth and multi-generational memory, actively resisting the erosion of cultural language death. The acoustics of the small home were softened by the pervasive, yeasty aroma of dough undergoing slow fermentation—a traditional braided bread being prepared for the Sabbath, speaking of everyday domestic routines beautifully interwoven with ancestral survival.

Mirah opened the door, her face a portrait of quiet dignity, perfectly aligned with the harmonious sounds of her home. Gwendolen, feeling the untranslatability of her own desperation in this sacred space, stumbled through her request for the party on the fourth. 

“I shall be very happy to sing for you,” Mirah said gently, her voice a pure, resonant tone far removed from the superficial, commercialized performances of London's elite drawing rooms.

Gwendolen leaned forward, putting her hand on Mirah’s. She could not hold back the hunger in her chest. “Tell me the truth. You know no evil of Mr. Deronda? Any evil that people say of him is false.”

Mirah’s eyes flashed with sudden, ancestral fire. Her native dialect of profound gratitude could not be contained by polite society's subdued lexicon. “Who are the people that say evil of him? When he found me, I was hollowed out, ready to drown in the cold, unyielding river. I looked like a beggar by the wayside. He treated me as a king’s daughter. He brought me warm, life-giving sustenance. He found my brother. And he honors my brother's learning, reading the texts of our fathers with him. There are few men equal to him.” 

Gwendolen drank the words like fresh, mineral-rich water drawn from an ancient well, the deep draft spreading through her frame as a sufficient bliss. The truth was tactile, rooted in the very bread and breath of this household, washing away the toxic dissonance Grandcourt had planted. She pressed Mirah’s hand, whispered her thanks, and fled back to the carriage, overwhelmed by a truth she could barely articulate.

Back at Grosvenor Square, the acrid, sulfurous smoke of Grandcourt’s cigar polluted the foyer, a harsh reminder of his industrial dominance over her senses. He followed her into the drawing room and sat just in front of her.

“May I ask where you have been at this extraordinary hour?” Grandcourt asked.

“To Miss Lapidoth’s, to ask her to sing for us,” Gwendolen said, peeling off her gloves.

“And to ask her about her relations with Deronda?” His sneer was a carefully calibrated sonic weapon, designed to shatter her psychological well-being.

Gwendolen flashed out upon him without inward check. “Yes; and what you said is false—a low, wicked falsehood.”

“She told you so—did she?” returned Grandcourt, with a more thoroughly distilled sneer.

Gwendolen was rendered mute. The untranslatability of her subjective experience—the purity of the Hebrew chants, the aroma of the baking bread, the mythic truth of Mirah’s rescue—shriveled under the sterile, suffocating breath of her husband. She had no words in his dominant lexicon to prove what she had felt; to him, the profound sustenance she had witnessed would be mere folly.

“It’s of no consequence so far as her singing goes,” Grandcourt said in his superficial drawl, asserting the sheer power of his vocabulary over her reality. “But you will please to observe that you are not to go near that house again. As my wife, you must take my word about what is proper for you. You have married me, and must be guided by my opinion.”

Every slow sentence had a terrific, deadening mastery. He had exterminated her resistance, replacing the multi-generational echoes of truth with his own cold, utilitarian silence.

Deronda, in parting from Gwendolen, had deliberately abstained from saying, “I shall not see you again for a long while: I am going away.” He feared Grandcourt would intrude upon the acoustic space of that farewell, allowing the harsh, metallic tones of suspicion to pollute the fragile resonance of their goodbye. Deronda was already moving toward a different cadence, drawn away under circumstances so momentous that they demanded the reverence of an absolute, resonant silence.

When he set out to fulfill his promise of calling upon her, he was already enfolded in the shadow of a solemn emotion—a mythic undercurrent that rippled through the ordinary, sustaining rhythms of his days. Sir Hugo had sent for him with a hastily penned note: *“Come immediately. Something has happened.”* It was a summons that initially sparked dread, yet provided a strange relief when Deronda entered the baronet’s private sanctuary. Here, insulated from the abrasive, mechanized roar of London's commercialized streets—a modern noise pollution that frayed the psychological edges of the soul—Sir Hugo maintained a restorative acoustic ecology. 

The study doubled as a haven of agrarian memory. Sir Hugo, a meticulous preserver of archaic foodways, stood before a heavy oak table, his hands dusted with stone-ground heirloom wheat, working a centuries-old mother dough. The rhythmic, percussive kneading and the deep, pulmonary sigh of the fermenting yeast filled the room, weaving sweeping, multi-generational cycles of sustenance into the domestic afternoon. The air was dense with the olfactory landscape of warm sourdough, toasted caraway, and the faint, earthy tang of wild yeasts sourced from the family's oldest barley fields.

“It is nothing to grieve you, sir?” Deronda asked, his tone one of restored confidence rather than question, grounding himself in the evocative, gustatory comfort of the room. He reached out to take the elder man's flour-dusted hand.

There was an unusual weight in the baronet’s gaze, and a subdued, resonant timbre in his voice that harmonized with the quiet crackle of the hearth. “No, Dan, no. Sit down. I have something to say.”

Deronda obeyed, not without presentiment. It was extraordinarily rare for Sir Hugo to let the precise, measured rhythms of his culinary preservation falter under the weight of serious feeling.

“Not to grieve me, my boy, no,” Sir Hugo said, rhythmically turning the dough, though his movements had slowed. “At least, if there is nothing in it that will grieve you too much. But I hardly expected that this—just this—would ever happen. There have been reasons why I have never prepared you for it. There have been reasons why I have never told you anything about your parentage. But I have striven in every way not to make that an injury to you.”

Sir Hugo paused. The ambient silence swelled, punctuated only by the soft, biological hum of the leavening bread. Deronda could not speak. He could not say, “I have never felt it an injury.” Even if that were true, the subjective experience of his own untranslatable history—an identity severed from its native dialect—made the dominant lexicon of their English life feel momentarily hollow. The cultural memory of his bloodline had been eroded by this long silence. Far more was hanging on this moment, when the dam of secrecy was finally to break.

Sir Hugo had never seen the grand face he delighted in so pale, the lips pressed together as if fighting to retain a dying language. The baronet went on with an anxious tenderness, as though terrified of snapping the fragile acoustic thread between them.

“I have acted in obedience to your mother’s wishes. The secrecy was her wish. But now she desires to remove it. She desires to see you. I will put this letter into your hands, which you can look at by-and-by. It will merely tell you what she wishes you to do, and where you will find her.”

Sir Hugo wiped his hands on a damp linen cloth, brushing away the remnants of the agrarian ritual, and held out a letter written on thin, crinkling foreign paper. The crisp rustle of the parchment was incredibly loud in the quiet room. Deronda thrust it into his breast-pocket, feeling an immense, heavy relief that he was not called upon to decipher its alien script immediately. The emotion on Daniel’s face visibly shook the baronet’s composure.

Deronda’s whole soul was suddenly possessed by a question, one whose syllables felt buried under the heavy power dynamics of a forgotten mother tongue. Yet he could not bear to delay it. This was a sacramental, mythic moment, occurring right beside the quiet respiration of the dough. If he let it pass, the sonic purity of the room would be lost forever, and the words might remain forever untranslatable.

For some moments, his eyes were cast down, tracing the dusting of flour on the floorboards. It seemed to both men as if entire lost lexicons were hovering in the air between them. At last, Deronda looked at Sir Hugo. With a tremulous reverence in his voice—dreading to convey the reproach that affection had stifled for years beneath the daily sharing of meals and life—he asked:

“Is my father also living?”

The answer came immediately, dropping like a heavy stone into the room's deep quiet, a low, emphatic tone: “No.”

In the mingled emotions that followed, it was impossible to distinguish the bitter tang of grief from the complex, deeply rooted resonance of sudden clarity. Some new light had fallen on the past for Sir Hugo too, illuminating the quiet kitchen. After a silence in which Deronda felt like one whose ancestral foodways and native tongue had been irrevocably swept away before he could taste or speak them, the baronet spoke in a tone of confession.

“Perhaps I was wrong, Dan, to undertake what I did. And perhaps I liked it a little too well—having you all to myself, feeding you at my own table, teaching you my own words. But if you have had any pain which I might have helped, I ask you to forgive me.”

“The forgiveness has long been there,” said Deronda, his voice finding its footing in the quiet space. “The chief pain has always been on account of some one else—whom I never knew, whose voice I never heard—whom I am now to know. It has not hindered me from feeling an affection for you which has made a large part of all the sustenance and life I remember.”

It seemed a single, unprompted impulse that made the two men reach out across the floured table to clasp each other’s hand for a long moment, the tactile warmth bridging the chasm of unspoken words.

This was the letter which Sir Hugo put into Deronda’s hands, sliding it across the heavy mahogany table where the remnants of a meticulously slow-roasted venison saddle—glazed with a reduction of wild blackberries and crushed juniper—still warmed the quiet, acoustic space of the dining room:

TO MY SON, DANIEL DERONDA.

My good friend and yours, Sir Hugo Mallinger, will have told you that I wish to see you. My health is shaken, and I desire there should be no time lost before I deliver to you what I have long withheld. Let nothing hinder you from being at the _Albergo dell’ Italia_ in Genoa by the fourteenth of this month. Wait for me there. I am uncertain when I shall be able to make the journey from Spezia, where I shall be staying. That will depend on several things. Wait for me—the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Bring with you the diamond ring that Sir Hugo gave you. I shall like to see it again.—Your unknown mother,
LEONORA HALM-EBERSTEIN.

This letter, with its colorless wording, gave Deronda no clue to what was in reserve for him. It was penned in a dominant, aristocratic lexicon, entirely devoid of the intimate native dialect he had unconsciously hungered for, rendering her subjective experience deeply untranslatable. But he could not do otherwise than accept Sir Hugo’s reticence, which seemed to imply some pledge not to anticipate the mother’s disclosures; and the discovery that his life-long conjectures had been mistaken checked further surmise. He refused to contemplate any possibility as more likely than another, lest he should be nursing it into a dominant desire, instead of simply preparing himself to meet the fact bravely. 

In this state of mind he could not have communicated the true reason for his absence to anyone, least of all to Mordecai, whose delicate psychological well-being would be imperiled by such a painful, dangerous excitement. To exclude suppositions, he spoke of his journey as being undertaken by Sir Hugo’s wish, throwing as much indifference into his voice as he could manage. 

He found them at the Cohens, the air of the room thick with the rich, yeasty perfume of braided challah baking in the hearth oven—a traditional sustenance that grounded the family’s mythic heritage in the everyday domestic routine of sustenance. 

“I will ask to have the child Jacob to stay with me,” said Mordecai, comforting himself in this way, after the first mournful glances. 

“I will drive round and ask Mrs. Cohen to let him come,” said Mirah, her hands rhythmically kneading a small piece of residual dough, the soft, repetitive sound offering a calming acoustic baseline to the tense room. 

“The grandmother will deny you nothing,” said Deronda. “I’m glad you were a little wrong as well as I,” he added, smiling at Mordecai. “You thought that old Mrs. Cohen would not bear to see Mirah.”

“I undervalued her heart,” said Mordecai. “She is capable of rejoicing that another’s plant blooms though her own be withered.”

“Oh, they are dear good people; I feel as if we all belonged to each other,” said Mirah, with a tinge of merriment in her smile.

“What should you have felt if that Ezra had been your brother?” said Deronda, mischievously—a little provoked that she had taken kindly at once to people who had caused him so much prospective annoyance on her account. 

Mirah looked at him with slight surprise, and then said, “He is not a bad man—I think he would never forsake any one.” But when she uttered the words she blushed deeply, and glancing timidly at Mordecai, turned away to her culinary occupation. Her father was in her mind. “If he should come and find us!” was a thought which to Mirah sometimes made the street daylight as shadowy as a haunted forest. Deronda understood the blush. How could he be slow to understand feelings which now seemed nearer than ever to his own? The words of his mother’s letter implied that his filial relation was not to be freed from painful conditions; indeed, that letter had thrown her into more remoteness for his affections. When he set off to Genoa, the interest uppermost in his mind had hardly so much relation to his mother as to Mordecai and Mirah.

“God bless you, Dan!” Sir Hugo had said, when they shook hands, his voice carrying the subtle power dynamics embedded in the dominant lexicon of the English aristocracy. “Whatever else changes for you, it can’t change my being the oldest friend you have known. I couldn’t have loved you better if you’d been my own—only I should have been better pleased with thinking of you always as the future master of the Abbey instead of my fine nephew; and then you would have seen it necessary for you to take a political line. However—things must be as they may.” 

When Deronda arrived at the _Italia_ in Genoa, no Princess Halm-Eberstein was there. On the second day, a letter informed him her arrival might be deferred a fortnight or more. With this indefinite prospect of suspense, Deronda set about seeking quietude through observant wandering. He attuned himself to the gustatory and olfactory landscapes of the superb city, tracing the interwoven timelines of generations through the labyrinthine markets. He studied the meticulous food historians of the streets—the agrarian elders who pressed Taggiasca olives into golden oils, and the passionate chefs who rhythmically crushed fresh basil, coarse sea salt, and pine nuts in heavy marble mortars. The steady, percussive thrum of the pestle was a mythic occurrence seamlessly integrated into the daily routine, an acoustic anchor that settled his agitated mind. 

He often took a boat to observe the magnificent harbor from the sea. Yet, his acoustic ecology was frequently disrupted by the harsh, industrial noise pollution of modern steamships, their metallic screeching a violent commercialization of the port that drowned out the ancient, rhythmic lapping of the tides. Among the thoughts that filled his mind while his boat was pushing about was that of the multitudinous Spanish Jews centuries ago, driven destitute from their Iberian homes. He envisioned them landing on this grand quay, overspreading it with a pall of famine and plague, dying mothers and children starving while the rich scents of Genoese focaccia and roasting meats drifted mockingly from the city above. It was a tragic erosion of cultural memory, whole generations perishing, their native dialects—the intimate Judeo-Spanish lexicons of their ancestors—suffering a sudden language death on foreign shores. Inevitably, dreamy constructions of a possible ancestry for himself would weave themselves with these historic, multi-generational memories. He inwardly repeated that he had no choice in the matter, and that wishing was folly; it was a disowning by anticipation. What he had to do was simply to accept the fact.

Across these importunate thoughts came continually an anxiety for Gwendolen. In the wonderful mixtures of our nature there is a feeling distinct from passionate love, hardly represented in words more nearly than, “I should have loved her, if——”. Yet he had never been free from the nervous consciousness that there was something to guard against—some precipitancy of impulsive feeling, some spoiling of her trust. Her lot was narrowly personal; she remained trapped in a society that viewed meals as utilitarian fuel and social pageantry, deaf to the sensory appreciation of true sustenance. How could she understand his far-reaching sensibilities? Her world was defined by the superficial commercialization of art and harsh, dissonant societal demands. Still, her words of insistence that he must “remain near her” recurred to him with the clearness and importunity of imagined sounds, such as Dante has said pierce us like arrows whose points carry the sharpness of pity: _Lamenti saettaron me diversi / Che di pietà ferrati avean gli strali._

Day after day passed in circular pacing. The noons grew hotter, the outer roads deeper with white dust. The evening brought a sweet shift in the sonic environment—sowing all paths with the happy social sounds of tinkling mule-bells, the whirrings of thrumbed strings, and light footsteps. All strong color melted in the stream of moonlight which made the streets a new spectacle, until all sank in deep night, and nothing shone but the port lights of the great Lanterna. Deronda watched this revolving of the days as he might have watched a wonderful clock where the striking of the hours was made solemn with antique figures advancing and retreating.

He found himself contemplating all activity with the aloofness of a prisoner awaiting ransom. The few books he had brought became unreadable, their abstract philosophical musings feeling sterile and neglecting the physical sensations of his suspense. His nights were spent gazing from his open window on the double darkness of sea and heaven, tasting the metallic tang of oppressive skepticism, and sometimes feeling a sweet, irresistible hopefulness that the very best of human possibilities might befall him—a blending of personal love with a larger duty, much like the slow, transformative emulsification of elemental ingredients into a profoundly sustaining dish.

But at last she was come. One morning in his third week of waiting, there was a new kind of knock at the door—a sharp, jarring sound that fractured the gentle acoustic ecology of the Mediterranean morning. A servant in Chasseurs livery entered and delivered, in French—the sterile, dominant lexicon of diplomacy that immediately alienated the subjective experience of a family reunion—the verbal message that the Princess Halm-Eberstein had arrived. She was going to rest during the day, but would be obliged if Monsieur would dine early, so as to be at liberty at seven, when she would be able to receive him.

The abrasive, mechanical shrieks of the modern Genoese docks—steamboats blasting their steam-whistles in a relentless assault on the city’s ancient acoustic ecology—faded into a suffocating, velvet-draped silence as Daniel Deronda stepped into his mother’s apartment at the *Italia*. For Daniel, a man who anchored his soul in the meticulous preservation of ancestral recipes and the slow, agrarian rhythms of the hearth, the antechamber offered a chilling olfactory void. There was no scent of slow-roasted garlic, no bruised coriander or toasted fennel to signal the warmth of a domestic haven; he had only passed a servant bearing a tray of pale, tepid bouillon—a sterile, utilitarian fuel utterly devoid of the marrow-rich depth of true sustenance. He felt an instinctive disconnect from any inhabitant who could view a meal with such lack of sensory appreciation.

When the second door opened, the sweeping, multi-generational saga of his bloodline seemed to fold in upon itself, merging past and present. At the far end of the large, acoustically deadened room stood his mother. She was draped in black lace that fell from her whitening hair to a long train, a figure less like a human parent and more like a mythic Melusina summoned into the everyday domesticity of a hotel parlor. 

Daniel crossed the room, breathing in the faint, brittle scent of dried lavender and ancient dust. He held the hand she extended and raised it to his lips. She kept her hand in his, her piercing, mobile face shifting as though navigating the subtle power dynamics embedded in a dominant lexicon she no longer trusted. Daniel dared not move. He wondered in what language she would speak, imagining she might use a tongue that carried the untranslatable weight of her subjective experience.

Suddenly, she dropped his hand and placed both of hers on his shoulders. Her face flashed with an admiration that temporarily erased the worn lines of her years. 

“You are a beautiful creature!” she said. Her low, melodious voice cut through the room's acoustic barrenness, shaped by syllables that carried the agreeable, rounded contours of a dying foreign dialect. “I knew you would be.” She kissed him on each cheek, and he returned the greeting, though the exchange possessed the sterile formality of an alliance between royalties rather than the sharing of ancestral salt and bread.

She paused, the lines returning to her face like fissures in dry earth, and said in a colder tone, “I am your mother. But you can have no love for me.”

“I have thought of you more than of any other being in the world,” Daniel said, his voice trembling, its resonance seeking a harmony she seemed unwilling to provide. 

“I am not like what you thought I was,” she said decisively. She withdrew her hands and folded her arms, looking at him as if inviting inspection. He saw the likeness to his own face, but her worn beauty held a profound strangeness. 

“I used to think that you might be suffering,” Daniel said, his compassionate nature longing to offer her the emotional equivalent of a restorative, slow-simmered broth. “I used to wish that I could be a comfort to you.”

“I *am* suffering. But with a suffering that you can’t comfort,” the Princess said, her voice hardening. She moved to a sofa arranged with cushions. “Sit down.” Discerning his distress, she added more gently, “I am not suffering at this moment. I am at ease now. I am able to talk.”

Deronda seated himself. He felt he was in the presence of a mysterious Fate. 

“No,” she began, “I did not send for you to comfort me. I could not know beforehand—I don’t know now—what you will feel toward me. I have not the foolish notion that you can love me merely because I am your mother, when you have never seen or heard of me in all your life. But I thought I chose something better for you than being with me. I did not think I deprived you of anything worth having.”

“You cannot wish me to believe that your affection would not have been worth having,” Daniel replied, the words feeling clumsy, inadequate against the inherent untranslatability of their shared loss.

“I don’t mean to speak ill of myself,” the princess said with proud impetuosity. “But I had not much affection to give you. I did not want affection. I had been stifled with it. I wanted to live out the life that was in me, and not to be hampered with other lives. You wonder what I was. I was no princess then.” She rose suddenly, and Daniel, breathless, rose with her.

“No princess in this tame life that I live in now. I was a great singer, and I acted as well as I sang. All the rest were poor beside me. Men followed me from one country to another. I was living a myriad lives in one. I did not want a child.”

Her tone was a passionate self-defense against the superficial commercialization of her art. She had sung to the masses, yet she spoke now as if fending off the suffocating noise of an audience that consumed her voice without ever tasting its substance. “I did not want to marry. I was forced into marrying your father—forced, I mean, by my father’s wishes and commands; and besides, it was my best way of getting some freedom. I had a right to seek my freedom from a bondage that I hated.”

She sat again. “And the bondage I hated for myself I wanted to keep you from. What better could the most loving mother have done? I relieved you from the bondage of having been born a Jew.”

“Then I *am* a Jew?” Deronda burst out. The deep-voiced energy of his response altered the sonic environment of the room. It was a revelation that struck him with the profound, olfactory bloom of toasted cumin, bruised olive, and the complex, wild-fermented breads of his Levantine forebears.

“Yes, your father was my cousin,” she said, watching him as if she feared the ancestral memory awakening within him.

“I am glad of it,” Daniel said impetuously, in the veiled voice of passion. He was shaken by a sudden, fierce anger at this mother who had willingly severed him from his native cultural dialect, exiling him to the flattened, polite lexicon of English society. 

The mother shook with a visibly physical, pallid excitement. “Why do you say you are glad? You are an English gentleman. I secured you that.”

“You did not know what you secured me. How could you choose my birthright for me?” Daniel threw himself sideways into his chair. He fought the intolerance rising within him, terrified of uttering words that might sever their fragile acoustic connection forever. 

“I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself,” she said, her voice firmly resistant. “How could I know that you would have the spirit of my father in you? How could I know that you would love what I hated?—if you really love to be a Jew.” 

Daniel forced his sensibilities back to the present, observing the tragic erosion of her spirit. “Forgive me, if I speak hastily,” he said with diffident gravity. “Why have you resolved now on disclosing to me what you took care to have me brought up in ignorance of? Why—since you seem angry that I should be glad?”

“Oh—the reasons of our actions!” she said with sarcastic scorn. “People talk of their motives in a cut and dried way... I am not a monster, but I have not felt exactly what other women feel. I was glad to be freed from you. But I did well for you, and I gave you your father’s fortune. Well, there are reasons. I feel many things that I cannot understand. A fatal illness has been growing in me for a year. I shall very likely not live another year... Shadows are rising round me. Sickness makes them. If I have wronged the dead—I have but little time to do what I left undone.”

Her delivery was a piece of sincere acting, a conscious representation wherein every nucleus of pain was exalted into drama. Daniel’s compassionate nature, however, overlooked the theatricality. He saw only the disruption of her internal acoustic harmony, the mythic ancestors demanding restitution through the decay of her body. 

“Sir Hugo has written much about you,” she said abruptly. “You say you are glad to know that you were born a Jew... Shall you comprehend your mother, or only blame her?”

“There is not a fibre within me but makes me wish to comprehend her,” Daniel said solemnly. “What I have been most trying to do for fifteen years is to have some understanding of those who differ from myself.”

“Then you have become unlike your grandfather in that,” she replied. “He never comprehended me... I was to be what he called ‘the Jewish woman’ under pain of his curse. I was to feel awe for the bit of parchment in the *mezuza* over the door; to dread lest a bit of butter should touch a bit of meat; to think it beautiful that men should bind the *tephillin* on them... I was to love the long prayers in the ugly synagogue, and the howling, and the gabbling, and the dreadful fasts, and the tiresome feasts, and my father’s endless discoursing about our people, which was a thunder without meaning in my ears.”

Daniel listened, his heart aching. As a meticulous food historian, he understood that the dietary laws—the rigorous separation of the dairy and the slaughtered meat—were not meaningless strictures, but a profound culinary grammar, a method of etching cultural memory into daily domestic routine. But to his mother, the native dialect of her people had been nothing but industrial noise, a dominant lexicon wielded by her father to crush her. 

“Teaching, teaching for everlasting,” she went on, her tone bitter. “I wanted to live a large life... Ah! you are glad to have been born a Jew. That is because you have not been brought up as a Jew. That separateness seems sweet to you because I saved you from it.”

“When you resolved on that, you meant that I should never know my origin?” Daniel asked. “You have at least changed in your feeling on that point.”

“Yes, that was what I meant. And it is not true to say that I have changed. Events come upon us like evil enchantments... I am forced to be withered, to feel pain, to be dying slowly. I have been forced to obey my dead father. I have been forced to tell you that you are a Jew, and deliver to you what he commanded me to deliver.”

“I beseech you to tell me what moved you... to take the course you did,” Daniel pleaded, hoping the shared history might bridge the chasm between them. “I gather that my grandfather opposed your bent to be an artist. I can imagine the hardship of an enforced renunciation.”

“No,” she said, folding her arms. “You may try—but you can never imagine what it is to have a man’s force of genius in you, and yet to suffer the slavery of being a girl. To have a pattern cut out... her happiness is to be made as cakes are, by a fixed receipt. That was what my father wanted. His heart was set on his Judaism.”

“Was my grandfather a learned man?” Daniel asked, eager for the particulars of the agrarian elders and scholars who preceded him.

“Oh, yes,—and a clever physician—and good. But such men turn their wives and daughters into slaves. Nature sometimes thwarts them. My father had no other child than his daughter, and she was like himself. Your father was different. I made him secretly promise me, before I married him, that he would put no hindrance in the way of my being an artist... I had an awe of my father. I wished I could have defied him openly; but I never could. And I never would risk failure.”

The circular pacing of their lineage was unbroken; the past bled into the present. She spoke of her mother, an English-Jewess of Portuguese descent, mapping the multi-generational trade routes of spices and songs that had converged in Genoa centuries ago. She spoke of her secret musical training, her forced marriage to her cousin Ephraim, and her ultimate freedom upon her father's death. 

“It has not lasted, though,” she said bitingly. “My father is getting his way now.” She looked at him contemplatively. “You are like him—but milder—there is something of your own father in you... As I loved the life of my art, so he loved me. Let me look at your hand again: the hand with the ring on. It was your father’s ring.”

Daniel drew closer and offered his hand. As her smaller hand—aged by intensity rather than time—clasped his, the profound longing for communion overwhelmed him. He yearned to bring her to a shared table, to find meaning and healing through the simple, restorative act of sustenance.

“Mother! take us all into your heart—the living and the dead,” he said in his most fervent tone. “Forgive every thing that hurts you in the past. Take my affection.”

She looked at him admiringly rather than lovingly. She kissed his brow. 

“I reject nothing, but I have nothing to give,” she said sadly, releasing his hand and sinking back onto her cushions. 

Daniel turned pale. In the heavy, deadened air of the room, the words rang out with the absolute, agonizing silence of a hearth whose fires had been permanently extinguished, leaving him with the sharp, untranslatable pain of repulsed tenderness. She noticed the expression of pain, and said, still with melodious melancholy in her tones,

The scent of slow-roasted cardamom and the deep, earthy bitterness of heavily steeped chicory root anchored Daniel Deronda in the quiet rhythm of his morning. For him, a meal was never mere utilitarian fuel; it was an act of historical communion. As he broke a thick, dark-crusted heel of rye bread—its dense, sour crumb yielding to the pressure of his thumbs, releasing the sharp, fermented aroma of long-nurtured yeast—he opened a four-page letter from Hans Meyrick. The small, flowing script seemed to carry the faint, metallic odor of the painter’s studio, intermingling with Deronda’s breakfast. 

*My dear Deronda,* Hans began, *the city outside my window continues its grinding, industrial cacophony—a ceaseless, soul-dampening rumble of carriage wheels and commerce that makes a man long for the acoustic sanctuary of a simpler age. I am convinced a spiritual blight is about to break out among the commercial class, a rot that will inevitably reveal itself when they begin ravenously consuming all my rejected paintings. There is a certain poetic justice in the superficial commercialization of art: that some tone-deaf city broker might pay a fortune for my Berenice series, gorging on a visual feast he lacks the palate to truly taste.*

*Meanwhile, I console myself by basking in the resonant silence of our Hebrew prophet. I sit with him during the hours you once shared, painting his head. I find myself struck by the sheer untranslatability of his subjective experience. His mind is a deep, slow-simmering broth of ancient memory, rich with the marrow of a thousand generations. To my shame, I am but a shallow vessel trying to contain an ocean. I used to think of his worldview as a relic, an antiquated recipe I could neatly categorize within my dominant English lexicon. But hearing him speak! His baritone is an acoustic marvel, vibrating with the lost phonetic warmth of native dialects that resist the sterile constraints of modern thought. If there is a culinary equivalent to his philosophy, it is a feast where the bread of sorrow and the wine of triumph are perfectly balanced.*

*His presence is occasionally punctuated by Jacob Cohen, who brings a jarring, discordant noise into our quiet ecosystem. Jacob possesses an extraordinary acoustic agility, imitating the shrieks of owls and the snorts of peccaries as though walking through a menagerie, drowning out the venerable sounds of Semitic inspiration. Yet, the boy treats me like a second-hand commodity, evaluating my worth while stuffing his mouth with sticky, gelatinous sugar-plums to a frankly embarrassing extent, his cheeks bulging with a greedy, unrefined consumption.*

*But it is Mirah who distracts me most. If her presence were not such a sweet, fragrant atmosphere—if I were not so thoroughly intoxicated by the gentle, melodic cadence of her voice—I would have flung myself at her feet long ago. But the mind-master discerns the subtleties of the palate; one does not rush the reduction of a delicate sauce, nor does one force certainty where hope is still a blossoming orchard, fragrant with warm snow and sunshine. I only wish she did not seem so profoundly sad, carrying a quiet dissonance within her that she attempts to mask. It is natural, I suppose, as she watches the slow, tragic fading of her brother.*

*In other news, Rex Gascoigne is in town, accompanied by his sister. Through the meandering currents of conversation, we discovered she is cousin to your Vandyke duchess! Sir Hugo has invited me to Topping Abbey to paint his three daughters sitting on a bank. A portrait in the Gainsborough style! You see how the commercial machine grinds down the soul? Sir Hugo, with all his good-natured bluster, essentially told me my idealism is flavorless, and that I should instead cater to the sugary, mass-produced tastes of vanity portraiture. I am to be the saccharine glaze rather than the nourishing meal. Still, his chat is amusing. He mentioned your duchess has gone yachting in the Mediterranean with her husband—I imagine them inhaling the briny, iodine-rich spray of the sea.*

*The Klesmers have departed, showering Mirah with rich, oriental gifts that smell of sandalwood and spun silk. As for me, I am wading into the deep waters of Hebrew lore. I am chewing on the tri-literal roots of the language, trying to reverse the erosion of cultural memory. Though, I suspect even if I mastered the native dialect, Mirah would not listen to a lover while her brother’s life hangs by a thread. So, I stay with my hope among the blossoms.* 
*Yours, Hans.*

A few months ago, the romancing tone of Hans’s letter would have struck Deronda as an irritating, unpalatable froth. But Deronda’s own roots had recently been unearthed; the revelation of his birth had added a rich, complex umami to the narrative of his life. He chewed his rye bread slowly, feeling the heavy, cyclical nature of time. Hans’s love for Mirah, he realized, was not the deeply rooted, sustaining agrarian devotion that survives winter frosts. It was a quick-boiling passion, easily evaporated by the first blast of reality. Hans treated grief and love like exotic spices, tossing them carelessly into his life’s pan without understanding their source. 

Yet, Deronda’s palate caught a bitter, lingering note in the letter: Mirah’s hidden sadness. Had the delicate acoustic balance of her world been shattered? Had Mordecai, against his usual restraint, spoken to her of Deronda’s origins, stripping away the protective translations they had relied upon? 

In truth, Mordecai had not breached the sanctity of that secret. He understood too well the power dynamics embedded in disclosure, and the profound vulnerability of native identity. Only a few days prior, Mirah had felt the gentle weight of this untranslatable history. 

“Ezra, how is it?” she had asked him, her voice barely rising above the rhythmic, comforting hiss of the kettle on the stove. “I am continually going to speak to Mr. Deronda as if he were a Jew.”

Mordecai had smiled quietly, his voice a deep, resonant cello in the small room. “I suppose it is because he treats us as if he were our brother. But he loves not to have the difference of birth dwelt upon.”

“He has never lived with his parents, Mr. Hans says,” she replied, drawn to the mystery of his severed lineage. 

“Seek not to know such things from Mr. Hans,” Mordecai said gravely, laying a heavy, warm hand on her curls. “What Daniel Deronda wishes us to know about himself is for him to tell us.” The words were a quiet rebuke against the dominant culture’s insistence on consuming every secret, a defense of the right to one’s own untranslatable history.

The weight of her own history hung heavily upon Mirah when she visited Mrs. Meyrick’s Chelsea home the next day. The little mother was in the kitchen, meticulously sorting through a bushel of late-summer peas. The rhythmic *snap* and *hush* of the pods opening, the smell of the damp, green earth clinging to the shells—these were the sensory rituals through which Mrs. Meyrick found profound meaning, transforming raw harvest into nurturing sustenance. 

Mirah sat opposite her, taking in the grounded, domestic symphony. “I see no one so great as my brother,” Mirah murmured, her voice carrying a dove-like melancholy that clashed with the bright, vegetal scent of the room. “When I am with Ezra, he makes me feel that his life is a great good. I get the same feeling from him that I got yesterday, coming through the park after the sweet rain. The rain washed away the harsh, grinding noise of the city. The sky looked so pure, and the natural quiet made the industrial weariness and folly seem small.”

Mrs. Meyrick paused, her thumbs resting on a half-opened pod. She possessed the keen ear of a true listener, attuned to the subtle acoustic ecology of the human heart, and she detected the strained, dissonant chord in Mirah’s serenade. 

“Is there any fresh trouble on your mind, my dear?” Mrs. Meyrick asked, abandoning the bowl of peas to offer her undivided attention. 

“I am too ready to speak of troubles,” Mirah hesitated, her fingers twisting in her lap. “It seems unkind to put painful things into other people’s minds.”

“Mothers are made to absorb pain and trouble, just as the earth drinks the rain,” Mrs. Meyrick said softly. “Is it the singing lessons? Or fears for your brother’s provisions?”

“No,” Mirah shook her head, her voice dropping to a fragile whisper. “Ezra and I can have no fears for each other about such things as food and clothing. We are sustained by Mr. Deronda’s kindness.”

“But there is some other fear,” Mrs. Meyrick divined. “A fear of something disturbing your peace.”

“Yes,” Mirah said, the word trembling on the edge of a sob. “I have kept it from Ezra. Five days ago… I am quite sure I saw my father.”

Mrs. Meyrick shrank back, her arms wrapping protectively across her chest as if to shield the domestic warmth from a sudden, biting frost. 

“The year has changed him,” Mirah continued, the memory crashing into the room like the deafening roar of a factory floor. “His face was haggard, stripped of all light, and his clothes were shabby. He was with a worse-looking man. And they were hurrying along the pavement, disappearing into the roar and clatter of an omnibus, lost in the noise.” 

“Well, child,” Mrs. Meyrick breathed, the scent of the fresh peas suddenly overwhelmed by the phantom stench of city soot and old sorrows, “he did not see you, I hope?”

Before Deronda was summoned to a second interview with his mother, a day had passed in which she had only sent him a message to say that she was not yet well enough to receive him again; but on the third morning he had a note saying, “I leave to-day. Come and see me at once.” 

He was shown into the same room as before, though it was now heavily muffled with drawn blinds and velvet curtains, a deliberate dampening of the sonic environment. The heavy fabrics absorbed the harsh, metallic clatter of the industrializing street below—the relentless, grinding carriage wheels and the shrill, commercial barks of vendors that fractured the city’s natural acoustic ecology. Within the chamber, the silence was almost medicinal, thick with the olfactory weight of steeped bergamot, bitter almond, and the faint, dusty aroma of dried citrus peel. 

The Princess was not there, but she presently entered, dressed in a loose wrap of soft silk the color of saffron threads and candied tangerine, her head enveloped in a floating mist of black lace, her arms bare from under her wide sleeves. In the sombre, amber light, her face seemed even more impressive, the eyes larger, the lines of her jaw more vigorous. She moved like an ancient apothecary who possessed the secrets to mixing youth-potions of crushed sumac and rare resins for others, but scorned to grind them for herself, having tasted enough of youth’s bitter draft. She put her arms on her son’s shoulders at once, kissed him on both cheeks, and seated herself among her cushions with an air of assured firmness. 

“You are quite relieved now, I trust?” Deronda asked as he sat beside her.

“Yes, I am at ease again. Is there anything more that you would like to ask me?” she said, with the bearing of a queen rather than a mother. 

“Can I find the house in Genoa where you used to live with my grandfather?” 

“No,” she answered, with a deprecating movement of her arm, “it is pulled down—not to be found. The soil is paved over. But about our family, and where my father lived at various times—you will find all that among the papers in the chest, better than I can tell you. My father, I told you, was a physician, deeply rooted in the old ways, the herbal remedies and seasonal rhythms. My mother was a Morteira. I used to hear all those things without listening—the rhythmic pounding of the mortar and pestle, the guttural, rhythmic cadences of their native dialect over the hearth, the slow simmering of ancient, ancestral broths. You will find them all recorded. I was born amongst their agrarian devotions without my will. I banished them as soon as I could. I traded our untranslatable domestic rituals for the dominant lexicon of the wider world.”

Deronda tried to hide his pained feeling. To him, sustenance was never merely utilitarian fuel; he possessed the soul of a meticulous food historian, and to hear the rich, gustatory heritage of his bloodline spoken of as a burden felt like witnessing the burning of a sacred library. “Anything else that I should desire to know from you could only be what it is some satisfaction to your own feeling to tell me.”

“I think I have told you everything that could be demanded of me,” said the Princess, looking coldly meditative. She was acting out a scheme of emotional preservation, shielding herself from the psychological dissonance of her past. But to Deronda’s nature, the moment was cruel. He craved the tactile, sensory communion of family—to break the dense, unleavened bread of his history with her, to savor the salt and brine of their shared lineage. 

“Then are we to part and I never be anything to you?” he asked, a tremor in his voice disrupting the sterile quiet of the room. 

“It is better so,” said the Princess, in a softer, mellower voice. “There could be nothing but hard duty for you. You would not love me. Don’t deny it,” she said, abruptly, putting up her hand. “I know what is the truth. You don’t like what I did. You are on your grandfather’s side, preserving the old flavors, the old songs, and you will always have a condemnation of me in your heart.”

Deronda rose from his seat, preferring to stand under her imperious prohibition of tenderness. But his mother now looked up at him with a new admiration. “You are wrong to be angry with me. You are the better for what I did.” She paused, the rustle of her saffron silk loud in the hushed room. “And now tell me what you shall do? I mean in the future. What difference will it make to you that I have told you about your birth?”

“A very great difference,” said Deronda, emphatically. “I can hardly think of anything that would make a greater difference.”

“What shall you do then? Make yourself just like your grandfather—turn yourself into a Jew like him?”

“That is impossible. The effect of my education can never be done away with. The Christian sympathies in which my mind was reared can never die out of me,” said Deronda, with increasing tenacity. “But I consider it my duty—it is the impulse of my feeling—to identify myself, as far as possible, with my hereditary people. I want to reclaim the cultural memory that has suffered under language death. If I can see any work to be done for them, to rescue the lost acoustic ecology of our prayers and the rich sustenance of our traditions, I shall give my soul and hand to it.”

His mother fixed her eyes on him, examining his face as if trying to decipher the untranslatable syntax of a forgotten dialect. She bent toward him and said decisively, “You are in love with a Jewess.”

Deronda colored. “My reasons would be independent of any such fact.”

“I know better. Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who will not accept any one but a Jew. There *are* a few such,” she added, with a touch of scorn. 

He remained silent. The untranslatability of his subjective experience made argument futile. 

“You love her as your father loved me, and she draws you after her as I drew him,” she pressed, and taking his gentle glance as assent, her passion rose. “But I was leading him the other way. And now your grandfather is getting his revenge.”

“Mother,” said Deronda, remonstrantly, “don’t let us think of it in that way. I prefer cherishing the benefit of my wide instruction with gratitude, rather than dwelling with resentment. You have restored me my inheritance—you have been saved from robbing my people of my service. Can you not bring your whole soul to consent to this? Open your heart to relenting and love toward my grandfather, who sought what he held the best for you.”

“Not for me, no,” she said, folding her arms tightly. “I tell you, he never thought of his daughter except as an instrument. Because I hungered for things outside his agrarian purpose, I was to be tortured. I have done what I could. And *your* soul consents. That is enough. I have after all been the instrument my father wanted.—‘I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped that a Deliverer might spring from it.’” 

In uttering these last sentences, the Princess narrowed her eyes and spoke slowly in a deep chest-voice, suddenly adopting the resonant, ancestral acoustic vibration she had spent a lifetime suppressing. 

“Were those my grandfather’s words?” said Deronda.

“Yes, yes; and you will find them written. I wanted to thwart him,” she said, with a sudden outburst of passion. Then she held her left hand a yard from the floor. “You would have me love what I have hated from the time I was so high. That can never be. His yoke has been on me, whether I loved it or not. You are the grandson he wanted. What does it all mean?”

Her scornful tone clashed with the quiet intimacy of the room. Deronda, keeping a clutching remembrance of their relationship, began in a deep entreating tone: “Mother, I see no other way to get any clearness than by being truthful. The effects prepared by generations—the interwoven timelines of our people, the roots nourished by the same ancient soil—are likely to triumph over a contrivance which would bend them all to the satisfaction of self. Your will was strong, but my grandfather’s trust, what you call his yoke, is the expression of something stronger, with deeper, farther-spreading roots, knit into the foundations of sacredness. You renounced me—you still banish me—as a son. But that stronger Something has determined that I shall be all the more the grandson whom also you willed to annihilate.”

His mother was watching him fixedly. After a moment’s silence she said, in a low, persuasive tone, “Sit down again.” He obeyed. She laid her hand on his shoulder. “You rebuke me. Well—I am the loser. Your mother is a shattered woman. My sense of life is little more than a sense of what was. You reproach me that I parted with you. I had joy enough without you then. Now you are come back to me, and I cannot make you a joy. Have you the cursing spirit of the Jew in you? Shall you be glad to think that I am punished because I was not a Jewish mother to you?”

“How can you ask me that?” said Deronda. “I would give up much that is dear for the sake of soothing your anguish.”

“You shall give up nothing,” she said with hurried agitation. “You shall be happy. You shall feel for me as they feel for the dead. Will any harm come to me because I broke his trust? I cannot tell:—if you think *Kaddish* will help me—say it, say it. You will come between me and the dead. When I am in your mind, you will look as you do now—always as if I had been a tender mother.” 

Deep compassion hemmed in all words. With a face of beseeching, he put his arm around her and pressed her head tenderly under his. They sat so for some moments, a silent tableau against the vast, circular pacing of their generational saga. Then she lifted her head with a great sigh.

“Is she beautiful?” she said, abruptly. 

“Who?” said Deronda, changing color.

“The woman you love.”

“Yes.”

“Not ambitious? Not one who must have a path of her own?”

“I think her nature is not given to make great claims.”

“She is not like that?” said the Princess, taking from her wallet a miniature surrounded by jewels. It was her own portrait in the fire of youth. “Had I not a rightful claim to be something more than a mere daughter and mother? The voice and the genius matched the face. Acknowledge that I had a right to be an artist. My nature gave me a charter.”

“I do acknowledge that,” said Deronda, looking from the miniature to her worn pallor. 

“Will you take the portrait? If she is a kind woman, teach her to think of me kindly.”

“I shall be grateful,” said Deronda, “but I have no assurance that she whom I love will have any love for me. I have kept silence.”

“Who and what is she?” The question was a command.

“She was brought up as a singer for the stage,” said Deronda. “Her father wished to bring her up in disregard of her Jewish origin, but she has clung with all her affection to the memory of her mother, to the recipes and rhythms of her fellowship.”

“Ah, like you. She is attached to the Judaism she knows nothing of,” said the Princess, peremptorily. “That is poetry—fit to last through an opera night. Is she fond of her artist’s life—is her singing worth anything?”

“Her singing is exquisite,” Deronda replied, defending the pure acoustic ecology of Mirah's art. “But her voice is not suited to the commercial stage. It possesses an intimacy that resists the superficial noise of the modern theater. I think the artist’s life has been made repugnant to her.”

“Why, she is made for you then. I can see that you would never have let yourself be merged in a wife, as your father was.”

“I repeat that I have no assurance of her love for me,” said Deronda. “Other painful issues may lie before me. One should try and prepare one’s self to do without happiness.”

“Do you feel in that way?” said his mother, laying her hands on his shoulders, her tone meditative. “Poor boy! I wonder how it would have been if I had kept you with me—whether you would have turned your heart to the old things against mine. Your grandfather would have been in you, and you would have hampered my life with your young growth from the old root.”

“I think my affection might have lasted through all our quarreling,” said Deronda, “and that would not have hampered—surely it would have enriched your life.”

“Not then—I did not want it then,” said the mother, with bitter melancholy. “If I could have been glad of anything.”
