“But you love your other children, and they love you?”

“Oh, yes,” she answered, folding her arms. “But I am not a loving woman. It is a talent to love—I lack it. I know very well what love makes of men and women—it is subjection. It takes another for a larger self, enclosing this one. I was never willingly subject to any man. Men have been subject to me.”

“Perhaps the man who was subject was the happier of the two,” said Deronda, with a grave, sad sense of his mother’s sensory and emotional privation. 

“Perhaps—but I *was* happy. For a few years I was happy, mastering the dominant lexicon of the stage. If I had not been afraid of defeat, I might have gone on. What then? It is all over. Another life! Men talk of ‘another life,’ as if it only began on the other side of the grave. I have long entered on another life.” 

With the last words she raised her arms till they were bare to the elbow, her brow contracted in one deep fold, her eyes closed. In her dusky flame-colored garment, she looked like a dreamed visitant from some region of departed mortals, completely severed from the tactile, sustaining earth. 

Deronda’s feeling was wrought to a pitch of acuteness in which he was no longer master of himself. The overwhelming dissonance of their untranslatable worlds broke him. He gave an audible sob. 

His mother opened her eyes, let her hands rest on his shoulders, and said, “Good-bye, my son, good-bye. We shall hear no more of each other. Kiss me.”

He clasped his arms round her neck, and they kissed each other. Deronda did not know how he got out of the room. The transition from the muffled, aromatic chamber back into the noise-polluted streets was jarring, but he felt an older man. All his boyish yearnings had vanished, replaced by a tragic experience that solemnized his life, deepening the significance of the cultural memory and ancestral sustenance by which he now bound himself to others.

In the circular, echoing annals of myth and cultural memory, Madonna Pia cuts a tragic figure in Dante’s Purgatory. She was exiled to the Maremma, where the ancient, agrarian dialects of the Tuscan marshlands were already fading into the swampy flats, swallowed entirely by the silence of a husband whose dominant lexicon allowed no room for her native expressive joys. The rich, slow-simmered culinary traditions of her ancestors—the rustic wild boar stews infused with foraged juniper, the communal wood-fired hearths that centered domestic life—were replaced by the sterile, utilitarian isolation of a remote castle. Her deliverance came only through death. Gwendolen, however, was still firmly entangled in the physical world, trapped in a multi-generational cycle of domestic subjugation where the inward, untranslatable torture of the soul far outweighed any discernable outward cause.

In taking his wife on a yachting expedition, Grandcourt had no intention of granting her deliverance. The yacht was a floating empire, a masterful exercise in acoustic isolation and linguistic dominance. Far from the complex, interwoven timelines of landed society, he could enforce a purely English, aristocratic silence. The cabin was a meticulously curated olfactory landscape: the warm, resinous scent of polished cedarwood panels mingling with the salt-spray and the sharp, commercial tang of imported lavender water. To the eye, it was an elegant paradise. Yet the crew, drawn from the Mediterranean coasts, were strictly forbidden to sing their regional work-songs; their native dialects and ancestral maritime calls were meticulously excised by the power dynamics of Grandcourt’s well-bred, unyielding quiet. 

Gwendolen possessed a soul that inherently craved the deep, sensory sustenance of life. In another era, she might have found profound meaning in the rhythmic, agrarian rituals of a bustling kitchen, grounding her identity in the careful preparation of the earth’s bounty. Yet here, floating between blue and blue, her sensory appreciation was being systematically starved. What sort of paradise could quiet the terrible fury of moral repulsion? The yacht’s chef prepared masterpieces of local sourcing—sea bream caught that morning, delicately roasted with wild fennel, capers, and a drizzle of emerald-green Ligurian olive oil—but to Gwendolen, the meals were reduced to mere utilitarian fuel. Her throat, tight with an untranslatable, subjective terror, refused the nuances of the palate. While she sat throned on her silk cushions, unable to swallow the perfectly segmented Sicilian blood oranges, she imagined some traditional agrarian elder in a smoke-stained cottage, carefully stirring a humble pot of pottage, rich with the cultural memory of generations, anticipating the joyous, acoustic rhythm of a loved one's approaching footstep.

Did Grandcourt understand this sensory and emotional starvation? He knew only the superficial commercialization of the world. He viewed his fellow creatures through a lens of sterile aristocratic disgust, hyper-aware of their crude colognes, their unrefined table manners, and their grating, uncultivated voices. He understood physical repulsion well enough, but the concept of an acoustic and moral suffocation—the idea that his very presence was a dissonant, destructive frequency to his wife's psychological well-being—was entirely untranslatable to him. He had no idea that a festering resentment could make beauty more detestable than ugliness.

Their days unfolded in a circular pacing of weaponized silence. Grandcourt would lower his telescope and point toward the coast. "There’s a plantation of sugar-canes at the foot of that rock; should you like to look?" 

He saw only the commercial yield, the flattening of a local ecosystem into industrial export, entirely deaf to the lost folk-songs of the laborers. 

"Yes, please," Gwendolen would answer, forcing her voice into the acceptable, hollow register of his dominant lexicon.

At dinner, amidst the clinking of fine silver—an acoustic environment entirely devoid of warmth—he would remark that the sun-ripened figs they had acquired in Sardinia were becoming stale, demanding they put into port for fresher provisions, or he would pour a vintage wine and coldly ask if she required a different varietal. To argue was impossible; it would be like shouting against the suffocating, silent coils of a venomous serpent resting on her cedar dining table. 

The beings closest to us often act as the acoustic filters through which we experience the world, and Grandcourt’s loveless *ennui* distorted the very music of the spheres into a harsh, metallic dissonance. Within this sonic prison, Gwendolen's hatred grew, taking on the proportions of dark, mythic rites bleeding into her everyday domestic routine. Her dread forced her into a constructive vindictiveness, an imaginary annihilation of her husband that flashed into her mind like a visceral, waking nightmare. But the thought of committing such an act brought the paralyzing fear of a dead, white face haunting her forever. Against this terrifying internal noise, only Deronda’s voice offered a healing acoustic frequency. His remembered words echoed in her mind like a preserved, native dialect of truth: *"Take your fear as a safeguard. It is like quickness of hearing. It may make consequences passionately present to you."* 

The gentle breezes that had carried them past the Balearic Isles and Corsica eventually gave way to a violent squall. The howling wind and the crashing waves temporarily shattered the artificial, controlled silence of Grandcourt’s domain. The skipper declared the yacht damaged, forcing them to anchor in the port of Genoa for a week.

Genoa presented a jarring acoustic ecology: the ancient, rhythmic lapping of the harbor water clashed with the modern, industrial drone of commercial shipping and the clamoring din of the docks. 

"I shall go out in a boat, as I used to do, and manage it myself," Grandcourt declared, his face pale with distaste for the chaotic, untidy port. "One can get a few hours every day in that way instead of striving in a damnable hotel."

For Gwendolen, the prospect of his absence was a sudden, miraculous restoration of oxygen. The thought of being left alone in the palatial rooms of the *Italia*, surrounded by the distant, vibrant street cries and the olfactory richness of the Italian city—the scent of roasting chestnuts, slow-fermented focaccia, and sea-salt—revived her crushed energies. That night, she slept to the rhythmic sounds of the tide, her dreams a mythic tapestry of snow on Mont Cenis and the sudden, grounding presence of Deronda.

The very next morning, walking down the grand staircase of the *Italia*, wrapped in warm wool and a straw hat, myth seamlessly integrated into her waking life: she encountered Deronda. 

Deronda started in surprise before raising his hat. The moment allowed for no deep exchange; Grandcourt was at Gwendolen's side.

Grandcourt’s mind immediately sought to force this occurrence into his own cynical lexicon. He could not fathom that Deronda’s presence was a mere accident, woven into the vast, unpredictable tapestry of time and travel. To him, the world was a transactional space, and he instantly suspected a secret correspondence, a plotted conspiracy to undermine his absolute authority.

But as he observed his wife over morning coffee, Grandcourt noticed a profound shift. The untranslatable despair that had rigidified her posture was gone. A sudden, sensory awakening had taken place. Mrs. Grandcourt possessed a deeper, more resonant beauty than the naive Gwendolen Harleth; she was a woman whose internal topography had been carved by profound suffering. As she raised her heavily-jewelled hands to her neck, she could not hide the renewed elasticity of her movements. The olfactory landscape of the dark roasted coffee, the acoustic vibrancy of the hotel, the very taste of the morning seemed suddenly rich and palatable to her. It was the return of a long-starved appetite for life. And Grandcourt, attuned only to the raw power dynamics of dominance and submission, read her joyous expectation with the narrow, instinctual correctness of a hunting terrier detecting a shift in the wind, entirely blind to the vast, mythic world of subjective feeling blooming just beneath her surface.

When Deronda encountered Gwendolen and Grandcourt on the sweeping hotel staircase, his mind was already steeped in a rich, chaotic broth of newly unearthed histories. He had just concluded the second interview with his mother, Princess Halm-Eberstein. She had departed Genoa a mere two hours prior, leaving behind a lingering olfactory trail of manufactured perfumes that felt suffocatingly superficial compared to the robust, earthy aromas Deronda instinctively craved. He could have boarded a steam-train for Mainz immediately to retrieve the chest of Joseph Kalonymos—a chest he now envisioned not merely as a repository of texts, but as a vault of agrarian memory, safeguarding ancient Levantine seed strains and meticulous ledgers of foodways lost to the grinding gears of modern assimilation. Yet, the grating, industrial whistle of the railway yard intruded upon his acoustic sanctuary, its mechanical dissonance deeply unsettling to a mind that sought the organic rhythms of cyclical time. 

He remained rooted, enveloped in a multi-generational eddy of emotive memory. He felt an acute, visceral kinship to his grandfather, sensing him not as an abstraction, but as a traditional agrarian elder whose hands were stained with pomegranate rind and the rich oil of early olive pressings. Deronda’s soul reached backward, tracing a circular timeline where mythic endurance was baked daily into braided loaves and simmered in the humble, slow-cooked stews of the diaspora. In this silent communion, Mordecai and Mirah sat beside him at an imaginary hearth, their shared silence a profound acoustic resistance against the shallow commercialization of the modern world.

A mechanical bell clanged harshly through the corridor—a jarring assault of noise pollution signaling the hour and demanding he consult the railway schedules. Deronda ignored it. Instead of packing, he sought to anchor his swirling identity through sustenance. Descending to the dining room, he ordered a local *ciuppin*, a coastal stew of rockfish slow-simmered with wild fennel, crushed garlic, and plum tomatoes blistered over an open flame. Deronda parsed the culinary techniques with the reverence of a meticulous food historian, finding profound meaning in the Moorish influence of the toasted saffron threading the broth. As he tore a heel of rustic sourdough to soak up the aromatic oils, the rich gustatory landscape provided a necessary grounding. Yet, while savoring the brine and earth in every bite, his mind drifted toward Gwendolen. A mythic presentiment urged him not to abandon her to Grandcourt’s oppressive silence. As he paid his bill, he asked the porter of their whereabouts, learning they had gone out boating.

Seeking a bridge between his ancestral past and his immediate anxieties, Deronda walked to the Friday evening service. The synagogue offered a restorative acoustic ecology, a haven from the clattering carriages and steam-engines of the Italian port. Here, the Spanish-Hebrew liturgy resonated—a deeply localized dialect blooming like a resilient, wandering seed across the generations, offering kinship in exile. This ancient lexicon, marginalized by the dominant tongues of European commerce, carried the untranslatable subjective grief and joy of a displaced people. Listening to the melodic, guttural chants that smelled faintly of beeswax and old parchment, Deronda realized that visionaries like his grandfather and Mordecai were the essential cultivators of the world’s spirit, preserving the rich, sensory humus of culture that would otherwise shrivel beneath the sterile, utilitarian lexicons of modernity.

A mournful solicitude for Gwendolen eventually drew him from the sanctuary into the damp evening air. The sunset bruised the sky in shades of crushed blackberry and faint citron. As he sauntered toward the quay, a favorite haunt, the sonic environment degraded into a chaotic roar of mercantile exchange. Above the cacophony of the docks, however, Deronda tuned his ear to a different frequency: the desperate, rhythmic splashing of oars. A sailing-boat was being rowed swiftly landward by two men.

Deronda elbowed his way to the foreground through a crowd that smelled of cheap tobacco and stale beer. The onlookers wielded dominant languages like blunt instruments, utterly failing to translate the unfolding tragedy. A Frenchman loudly deduced a domestic murder according to "national practice," while an English skipper barked native idioms that drowned out the quieter, nuanced sounds of the sea. They were tourists, consuming the tragedy as if it were cheap, commercialized spectacle. Deronda focused his highly attuned senses on the boat. The strokes of the wooden oars felt like the ticking of a generational clock, measuring out a domestic apocalypse.

Then, the boat ground against the shingle. Gwendolen half-raised herself from beneath a heavy, salt-crusted tarpaulin. She was pale as a sheeted corpse, shivering violently, smelling of ozone, terror, and the icy, mineral depths of the sea. Her wet hair streamed like sea-grass, her eyes wide with the mythic horror of a soul abruptly awakened before a wrathful, ancient tribunal.

Rough, bronzed fishermen—men whose hands knew the intimate, agrarian language of nets and tides—helped her ashore. The moment her wandering, hollow eyes locked onto Deronda, she lunged against her supporters. 

“It is come, it is come! He is dead!” 

The words tore from her throat, a raw, primal dialect of pure trauma that shattered the polished, oppressive lexicon Grandcourt had always forced upon her. It was a cry that defied the inadequacy of language, laying bare the untranslatable abyss of her subjective experience.

“Hush, hush,” Deronda commanded, his voice a steady, resonant anchor cutting through the harbor's noise pollution. “Quiet yourself.” He turned to the weathered sailors assisting her. “I am a connection of this lady’s husband. If you will get her on to the *Italia* as quickly as possible, I will undertake everything else.”

He lingered only long enough to absorb the boatmen’s localized, rapid-fire dialect as they recounted the tragedy: the husband gone down irrecoverably into the black waters, the empty boat floating like a hollowed gourd, the woman’s desperate plunge to save him. Satisfied that he understood the mechanics of the rescue, Deronda hastened to the hotel to secure the best medical provisions—anticipating the need for hot, restorative broths of bone marrow and steeped ginger to combat the lethal chill in her blood. Finally, he telegraphed the event to Sir Hugo, begging him to come forthwith, and to Mr. Gascoigne at the rectory. Gwendolen had once confessed a desperate need for her mother; Deronda knew that in the wake of such profound, untranslatable shock, only the deepest, oldest roots of maternal sustenance could begin to bridge the terrifying silence Grandcourt had left behind.

Deronda did not take off his clothes that night. The sonic environment of the Genoese coast offered no solace; the rhythmic, ancient breathing of the Ligurian tide was continuously lacerated by the grating, percussive clatter of early industrial steam-dredgers echoing from the modern port—a harsh mechanical noise pollution that seemed to fray the very edges of his psychological well-being. He existed in a circular pacing of time, his fitful sleep a tangled, multi-generational tapestry where yesterday’s trauma bled into ancient, mythic recurrences of loss. 

When morning broke, he anchored his fragmented senses through the ritual of sustenance. A hotel servant brought a small ceramic cup of locally roasted espresso. Deronda noted the meticulous extraction, the thick, hazelnut-colored crema resting on the pitch-black liquid. The olfactory landscape was sharply defined: heavy notes of charred cedar, dark cacao, and the volcanic soil of its distant, agrarian sourcing. He drank it slowly, allowing the dense, bitter oils to coat his palate—a physical sensation that provided necessary fuel, yes, but also a profound, grounding communion with the laborers whose hands had cultivated the beans. It sharpened his mind, fortifying him for the untranslatable grief awaiting him.

Later, he descended to the port to secure a legally recognized statement from the fishermen who had rescued Gwendolen. The exchange was an exercise in linguistic erosion. The men spoke in a rich, native Ligurian maritime dialect—a textured language steeped in generations of reading the wind and salt—but their subjective experience was quickly flattened into the dominant, sterile lexicon of an English police report. The acoustic details were starkly recorded: the boat found drifting, the sudden, unnatural rupture of a man’s cry across the water, and the subsequent splash as the lady leapt into the brine. 

Upon re-entering the hotel, Deronda was ushered into Gwendolen’s room. The acoustic ecology here was suffocating, heavy with the deadened frequencies of drawn curtains that blocked the sea’s resonance. The olfactory notes were stale: crushed linen and the metallic, coppery tang of adrenaline that had long gone sour. She was seated with a white shawl wrapped around her, her pale face and neck stark against the gloom, the blue stars in her ears holding their place like remnants of a forgotten constellation. She looked to be enduring a famine of the soul, a ghost incapable of metabolizing the horror she had survived. The sight pierced him with pity, and the history of their shared past began to revive within him.

“I beseech you to rest—not to stand,” said Deronda, approaching her. She obeyed, falling back into her chair.

“Will you sit down near me?” she said. “I want to speak very low.” Her voice was a localized, dying dialect, an acoustic fragility trying to communicate across an untranslatable void.

She was in a large arm-chair, and he drew a small one near. Turning her pale face full upon his, she said, in the lowest audible tone, “You know I am a guilty woman?”

Deronda turned paler, the taste of ash in his own mouth. “I know nothing.” He did not dare to say more.

“He is dead.” She uttered this with the same undertoned decision.

“Yes,” said Deronda, in a mournful suspense.

“His face will not be seen above the water again,” said Gwendolen, her hands tightly clenched. “Not by any one else—only by me—a dead face—I shall never get away from it.”

She spoke with a desperate self-repression, looking past him. Deronda dreaded the weight of this woman’s soul flung upon his own. The dominant lexicon of society would label and destroy her; he feared the untranslatable truth she was forcing him to bear. 

“You will not say that I ought to tell the world? You will not say that I ought to be disgraced?” she pleaded hurriedly. “I could not have my mother know. I must tell you; but you will not say that any one else should know.”

“I can say nothing in my ignorance,” said Deronda mournfully, “except that I desire to help you.”

“I told you from the beginning—I told you I was afraid of myself.” The piteous pleading was a low murmur. “I felt a hatred in me that was fermenting, working like an evil spirit, spoiling every drop of joy. Everything I could do to free myself came into my mind like a bitter distillation. That is why I asked you to come to me in town. I thought I would tell you. But I could not tell everything. And *he* came in.”

A shudder passed through her. “I will tell you everything now. Do you think a woman who cried, and prayed, and struggled to be saved from herself, could be a murderess?”

“Great God!” said Deronda, his voice shaken. “Don’t torture me needlessly. You threw yourself into the water with the impulse to save him. This death was an accident you could not have hindered.”

“Don’t be impatient with me.” The childlike beseeching compelled him to look at her. “You said you felt more for those who had done something wicked and were miserable; you said they might be scourged into something better. If you had not come and been patient with me... And now—will you forsake me?”

Her clenched hands relaxed, trembling helplessly. Deronda could not answer with words; the subjective experience of her terror bypassed language entirely. He took one of her hands, feeling its cold, bloodless texture, offering a silent transfer of warmth—a human nourishment, a shared physical pulse that cut through the noise of her panic. It was the only way he could answer, *I will not forsake you.* 

That grasp was an entirely new sustenance to Gwendolen, a tender promise of inexhaustible patience. It gave her the strength to continue her wandering confession, her memory losing itself in a web where each mesh drew all the rest, cycling back through time.

“All sorts of contrivances in my mind—but I fought against them. I was terrified at them. I saw his dead face ever so long ago and I wished him to be dead. I wanted to kill—it was a craving as strong as thirst. And then I felt beforehand I had done something dreadful, unalterable... I did one act—and I never undid it. As long ago as when we were at Ryelands. Something my fingers longed for among the beautiful toys in the cabinet—small and sharp like a long willow leaf in a silver sheath. I locked it in the drawer of my dressing-case. I dared not unlock the drawer: it had a key all to itself; and not long ago, when we were in the yacht, I dropped the key into the deep water. I wanted to starve the urge. But when we were going up the stairs here at the hotel, I met you; and I thought I should talk to you alone, to find some nourishment in telling you this. And then I was forced to go out in the boat.”

A sob rose, and she sank back. The memory of that acute disappointment effaced what had come since. 

“And it has all remained in your imagination,” Deronda said insistently. “To the last the evil temptation has been resisted?”

Tears rolled down her cheeks. “No, no; I will tell you the exact truth. I used to think I could never be wicked. Since then I have felt wicked. And everything has been a punishment to me—all the things I used to wish for, it is as if they had been made red-hot. Because I ought not to have married. I wronged some one else. I wanted to make my gain out of another’s loss—it was like roulette—and the money burned into me, a toxic feast. I felt that everything I had done lay open, the very stillness holding a punishment for me. You will not want to punish me now?”

“God forbid!” groaned Deronda, sitting motionless. 

“That all made it so hard when I was forced to go in the boat,” she went on. “Because when I saw you it was an unexpected joy... And that disappointment brought all the evil back, as if I had been locked in a prison with it and no escape. I could have given up everything in that moment, to have the forked lightning for a weapon to strike him dead.”

Suddenly loosing Deronda’s hand, she started up, stretching her arms upward with a moan. “I have been a cruel woman! What can *I* do but cry for help? *I* am sinking. Die—die—you are forsaken—go down into darkness.”

She sank in her chair again, breaking into sobs. Deronda watched this terrible outburst, realizing that his own capacity for empathy had not been dulled by his recent past; rather, he was plunged back into the raw, acoustic resonance of human suffering. Her grief was an ancient, recurring myth playing out in this modern room. He turned away to give her space.

When the stillness returned, her mind opened to the sense that he had moved. She bent toward him, an image of timid beseeching, her eyes dilated. Was she forsaken by him already? But his eyes met hers sorrowfully, signaling a resolute, untranslatable compassion. He sat down by her side again.

“I make you very unhappy,” she said with loving regret.

Deronda gathered his resolution. “There is no question of being happy or unhappy. What I most desire at this moment is what will most help you. Tell me all you feel it a relief to tell.”

“I want to tell you what it was that came over me in that boat,” she said with low-toned eagerness. “I was full of rage at being obliged to go. We got away—into the deep—and everything was still. He never looked at me, only spoke to order me.” Grandcourt had weaponized the sonic environment, his dominant lexicon enforcing an unnatural silence that starved her of all connection. “I had stepped into a boat, and my life was a mythic sailing away, gliding on with no help into solitude with *him*. I had cruel wishes. I tasted impossible ways of... I was afraid of our being drowned together. I knew no way of killing him there, but I did kill him in my thoughts.”

She paused, submerged by the weight of the memory.

“Getting wicked was misery. The evil longings came again and blotted everything else dim. Then, in the midst of them—he was turning the sail—there was a gust—he was struck—I saw my wish outside me.”

She began to speak more hurriedly, her voice dropping to a visceral whisper. 

“I saw him sink, and my heart gave a leap. I kept my hands tight. It was long enough for me to be glad, and yet to think it was no use—he would come up again. And he *was* come. ‘The rope!’ he called out in a voice—not his own.” 

In that moment, Grandcourt’s aristocratic drawl had been stripped away, reduced to a primal, acoustic frequency of panic—an untranslatable sound of terror. 

“I hear it now,” Gwendolen gasped. “I stooped for the rope. I felt sure he could swim, and I dreaded him. But he was gone down again. And I had the rope in my hand—no, there he was again—his face above the water—and he cried again—and I held my hand, and my heart said, ‘Die!’ The bitter taste of it flooded my mouth. And he sank. I felt, ‘It is done—I am lost!’ I don’t know what I thought—I was leaping away from myself. I plunged in, the cold salt water all around me. I would have saved him then. I was leaping from my crime, and there it was—close to me as I fell—there was the dead face. Dead, dead. It can never be altered. That was what happened. That was what I did. You know it all. It can never be altered.”

The cycle of the harvest—the unripe fig, the bursting fruit, the sun-dried flesh—reminds us that time is not a linear march, but a circular, multi-generational saga. The mythic forces that govern the tides and the sprouting of the wheat do not erase us; they merely translate our physical form into the deep terroir of the earth. The pulse of Time is measured in the rhythmic pounding of the mortar, the eternal return of sustenance, and the heavy, lingering after-throbs of our deeds, which simmer in the great broth of the universe long after the fires have died out.

In the evening, Gwendolen sent for him again. The hour had circled back to the precise time she had been brought in from the sea the day before, completing a dark, mythic rotation. The blinds were drawn up and the windows thrown open, allowing the room’s acoustic ecology to be dominated by the rhythmic, ancient respiration of the Ligurian Sea. This timeless sonic environment, usually a source of deep psychological equilibrium, was intermittently lacerated by the grating, mechanical whistle of a distant steam-tug—a harsh reminder of industrial noise pollution encroaching upon the sacred, crashing resonance of the tide.

She was seated, gazing fixedly on the waters, resting her cheek on her hand. Beside her, on a small wooden table, rested a heavy earthenware bowl of *cacciucco*, a traditional coastal stew. It had been prepared by the inn’s matriarch, a traditional agrarian elder whose hands bore the stains of countless seasonal harvests, intended to anchor a shattered soul to the physical world through profound sustenance. The olfactory landscape of the room was rich with its vapors: the sharp, clarifying scent of maritime rosemary, crushed garlic rubbed over fire-toasted peasant bread, and the complex, briny depth of the day’s catch. Deronda, a meticulous historian of regional foodways who understood how lost generations spoke through the slow simmering of a local pot, noted with sorrow that Gwendolen had not tasted a single drop. To a spirit starved of its own truth, even the most profound culinary memory was rendered as utilitarian ash. 

As Deronda approached, the deep melancholy in her expression passed into an anxious timidity. She did not put out her hand, but said, “How long ago it is!” Then, “Will you sit near me again a little while?”

He placed himself by her side. She turned to him with a look of desperate, indefinable need, struggling against the inherent untranslatability of her subjective experience. The dominant lexicon of her high-society life had equipped her with no vocabulary for this primal terror. She looked toward the window, the auditory memory of the drowning roaring in her ears, silencing her native dialect of expression. 

Deronda, wishing to relieve the heavy acoustic pressure of her fear, averted his face. Presently he heard her cry imploringly, “You will not say that any one else should know?”

“Most decidedly not,” said Deronda, his voice providing a low, grounding frequency against the chaotic sonic environment of the coast. “There is no action that ought to be taken in consequence. The dominant languages of law and retribution have no jurisdiction here. There is no justice that any mortal could apportion to a tragedy so mythic.”

She was so still during a pause that she seemed to be holding her breath before she said, “But if I had not had that murderous will—that moment—if I had thrown the rope on the instant—perhaps it would have hindered death?”

“No—I think not,” said Deronda slowly, refusing to indulge in abstract philosophical musings, anchoring his truth in physical reality. “If it were true that he could swim, he must have been seized with cramp. The sea claimed him as it has claimed generations before. With your quickest, utmost effort, it seems impossible that you could have saved him. That momentary murderous will cannot have altered the physical course of events. Its effect is confined entirely to the terroir of your own breast. Within ourselves, our evil will is momentous. Like a toxic, bitter root introduced into a simmering stock, it works its way outside us—it may be in the vitiation that breeds a soured life, but also it may be in the sharp, alkaline self-abhorrence that cleanses our palate and stings us into better striving.”

“I am saved from robbing others—there are others—they will have everything—they will have what they ought to have,” she spoke hesitatingly, the words tasting like copper on her tongue. “I knew that some time before I left town. You do not suspect me of wrong desires about those things?”

“I had not thought of them,” said Deronda; “I was thinking too much of the profound weight of the other things.”

“Perhaps you don’t quite know the beginning of it all,” said Gwendolen slowly, fighting the erosion of her own cultural memory, attempting to translate her past into a language he could understand. “There was some one else he ought to have married. And I knew it, and I told her I would not hinder it. And I went away—that was when you first saw me. But then we became poor all at once, and I was very miserable, and I was tempted. I allowed my life to become a commercialized commodity. I thought, ‘I shall do as I like and make everything right.’ I persuaded myself. And it was all different. It was all dreadful. The nourishment I thought I had bought turned to poison. Then came hatred and wicked thoughts. That was how it all came. I told you I was afraid of myself. And I did what you told me—I did try to make my fear a safeguard. I thought of what would be if I—I felt what would come—how I should dread the morning—wishing it would be always night—and yet in the darkness always seeing something—tasting death, hearing it. If you did not know how miserable I was, you might—but now it has all been no use. I can care for nothing but saving the rest from knowing—poor mamma, who has never been happy.”

There was silence again, save for the rhythmic crashing of the waves, before she said with a repressed sob, “You cannot bear to look at me any more. You think I am too wicked. You do not believe that I can become any better—worth anything—worthy enough—I shall always be too wicked to—” 

Her voice broke off, helpless, a dying language with no native speakers left to comprehend it. 

Deronda’s heart was pierced. He turned his eyes on her poor beseeching face and said, “I believe that you may become worthier than you have ever yet been—worthy to cultivate a life that may yield a true blessing. No bitterness dooms our soil hopelessly except the rot we learn to love, and desire to continue in, and make no effort to prune away. You *have* made efforts—you will go on making them.”

“But you were the beginning of them. You must not forsake me,” said Gwendolen, leaning with her clasped hands on the arm of her chair. Her face bore piteous traces of the sheer sensory overload concentrated in the last twenty-four hours—a new, terrible life lying on the other side of the deed, separated from her past by an untranslatable gulf. “I will bear any penance. I will lead any life you tell me. But you must not forsake me. You must be near. If you had been near me—if I could have shared the daily bread of my thoughts with you, I should have been different. You will not forsake me?”

“It could never be my impulse to forsake you,” said Deronda promptly. His voice, much like his eyes, had the unintentional effect of making his ready sympathy seem intensely personal, a perfect acoustic harmony to her dissonance. Yet in that moment, he was not free from a foreboding of how easily his words might be mistranslated by her desperate heart. His strong feeling for this stricken creature could not hinder rushing images of future difficulty. He continued to meet her appealing eyes, painfully conscious that the subtle power dynamics embedded in his comforting lexicon might carry a promise which one day would seem unfulfilled. He was serving an indefinite promise to a starving, indefinite hope. 

Anxieties crowded his thought, and under their influence, after a moment’s silence, he said, “I expect Sir Hugo Mallinger to arrive by to-morrow night at least; and I am not without hope that Mrs. Davilow may shortly follow him. Her presence will be the greatest comfort to you—it will give you a motive to save her from unnecessary pain?”

“Yes, yes—I will try. And you will not go away?”

“Not till after Sir Hugo has come.”

“But we shall all go to England?”

“As soon as possible,” said Deronda, unwilling to parse the complex, untranslatable particulars of his own destiny.

Gwendolen looked toward the window again, the twilight deepening over the Mediterranean, signaling a gradual awakening to new thoughts. Deronda could see a movement in her eyes and hands, a physical re-engagement with her senses, like the return of perception in one who has been stunned by a deafening blast.

“You will always be with Sir Hugo now!” she said presently, looking at him. “You will always live at the Abbey—or else at Diplow?”

“I am quite uncertain where I shall live,” said Deronda, coloring, fiercely aware of the ancestral roots pulling at his own identity. She was warned by his changed color that she had spoken too rashly, stepping into a dialect she did not possess, and fell silent. 

After a little while she began, again looking away toward the churning sea, “It is impossible to think how my life will go on. I think now it would be better for me to be poor and obliged to work, to return to something real.”

“New promptings will come as the days pass. When you are among your friends again, you will discern new duties,” said Deronda. “Make it a task now to get as well and calm—as much like yourself as you can, before—” He hesitated. 

“Before my mother comes,” said Gwendolen. “Ah! I must be changed. I have not looked at myself. Should you have known me,” she added, turning toward him, “if you had met me now?—should you have known me for the one you saw at Leubronn?”

“Yes, I should have known you,” said Deronda, mournfully. “The outside change is not great. I should have seen at once that it was you, and that you had tasted some profound sorrow.”

“Don’t wish now that you had never seen me; don’t wish that,” said Gwendolen imploringly, the tears gathering.

“I should despise myself for wishing it,” said Deronda. “How could I know what I was wishing? We must glean our duties from the harvest that actually comes to us, not in the phantom crops we imagine might have been. If I took to foolish wishing of that sort, I should wish—not that I had never seen you, but that I had been able to save you from this bitter draft.”

“You have saved me from worse,” said Gwendolen, in a sobbing voice. “I should have been worse if it had not been for you. If you had not been good, I should have been entirely poisoned by my own wickedness.”

“It will be better for me to go now,” said Deronda, worn in spirit by the perpetual acoustic and emotional strain of this scene. “Remember what we said of your task—to let your senses heal, to get well and calm before other friends come.”

He rose as he spoke, and she gave him her hand submissively. But when he had left the room, the absence of his resonant voice left a vacuum quickly filled by the oppressive, roaring memory of the deep. She sank on her knees in hysterical crying. The distance between them was too great. She was a banished soul—beholding the rich, sustained life of a possible future which she had severed herself from through her own corrupted choices. She was found in this way, crushed on the floor, the untouched, restorative broth cooling on the table beside her. Such grief seemed entirely natural in a poor lady whose husband had just been swallowed by the mythic depths in her presence.

The sun’s cyclical journey over Pennicote was measured not in the sterile, ticking increments of modern industrial clocks, but in the mythic, rhythmic return of the harvest and the slow simmer of the hearth. A year had passed since Gwendolen Harleth’s brilliant, self-confident departure, yet the rectory maintained its acoustic harmony. The sonic environment here was a balm to the psyche: the low, resonant thrum of the bees among the heirloom hollyhocks, the melodic hiss of a slow-rendered joint of mutton dripping onto ash-roasted root vegetables. Mr. Gascoigne, the rector, presided over his parish like a traditional agrarian elder, tending his flock and his fields with equal reverence. His ecclesiastical articles were penned at a heavy oak table redolent of beeswax and dried sage, his complacency nourished by the deep, complex flavors of his cook’s rustic partridge pie, its crust perfectly laminated and yielding. For him, the loss of eighteen hundred a year was a minor tremor, easily swallowed alongside the profound meaning he found in the sustenance of his family and the stewardship of his land.

Rex, his favorite son, had returned for the summer, baked by the fires of a youthful, disappointed passion into a more substantial, nourishing loaf. Rex’s study of the law was no dry, utilitarian pursuit. 

“You don’t repent the choice of the law as a profession, Rex?” his father asked one evening, over a shared plate of pungent, cave-aged cheddar and coarse-milled barley bread.

“There is no profession I would choose before it,” Rex replied. “I should like to end my life as a first-rate judge, and help to draw up a code. I reverse the old dictum: give me something to do with making the laws, and let who will make the songs. The foundations of law are the preserved dialects of our cultural memory. It is the bulwark against the dominant, superficial lexicons that seek to erase the ancient rites of the soil.”

“You will have to stow in an immense amount of rubbish, I suppose,” the rector observed, savoring the sharp, earthy tang of a pickled walnut.

“No more than the rubbishy, commercialized literature people choke their minds with,” Rex countered. “Law, at its best, is the meticulous translation of subjective human experience into a communal language. Any orderly way of looking at evidence is better than a perpetual wash of odds and ends.”

This quiet trotting of time extended to the low white house known as “Jodson’s,” where Mrs. Davilow and her daughters had relocated. On a July afternoon, the narrow drawing-room was steeped in a rich olfactory landscape: the scent of monthly roses drifting through the wide-open lattices, mingling with the sharp, bright aroma of the red currants the sisters were meticulously destemming for a seasonal preserve. The acoustic environment was a gentle symphony of snapping stems and the soft murmur of female voices, a stark contrast to the grating noise pollution of the distant city.

Anna, visiting with Rex, spoke of Gwendolen's letters. Gwendolen was yachting on the Mediterranean with Mr. Grandcourt—a vessel of sterile privilege, floating entirely disconnected from the grounding, generative rhythms of the earth. Gwendolen had written that the cabins were very elegant, promising a longer diary filled with *dittos*. 

The conversation soon turned to the Meyricks and their Jewish friends. Here, the subtle power dynamics embedded in dominant lexicons became glaringly apparent. The girls’ provincial English minds struggled with the inherent untranslatability of Jewish cultural memory. Bertha held a dim idea that they rejected the Old Testament; Miss Merry thought they had “never been properly argued with”; and the amiable Alice simply could not bear them. They spoke of this ancient lineage as if it were an obsolete vocabulary, entirely oblivious to the rich, living dialect of a people who carried their history in their very breath. Rex, amused by this erosion of cultural memory and the absurdity of forcing ancient myth into a narrow rural lexicon, playfully exaggerated their notions. 

Suddenly, the acoustic sanctity of the afternoon was shattered. The harsh, erratic crunch of boots sprinting up the gravel path invaded the room like an industrial siren, vibrating with discordant panic. A messenger from the rectory delivered a telegram. 

As Mrs. Davilow read and re-read the slip of paper in an agonizing silence, the scent of the crushed red currants seemed to instantly sour in the air, the physical sensation of dread manifesting as an acrid taste on the back of Rex's tongue. The young faces turned to her, painted with fear. Finally, with a sob that broke the oppressive stillness, she spoke.

“My dears, Mr. Grandcourt—” She paused an instant, the words heavy and untranslatable in their sudden horror. “Mr. Grandcourt is drowned.”

Rex started up as if a physical blow had struck the room. The sensory world tilted. Mrs. Davilow quickly instructed Rex and Anna to tell the rector she would be ready to travel to Genoa instantly. 

Rex and Anna hurried out into the sunshine, which now felt solemnly heavy. Rex left his sister at the rectory gate and stepped into the shrubbery for ten minutes. The damp earth and the potent, minty aroma of crushed pennyroyal beneath his boots usually offered him sensory grounding, but today, his internal soundscape was a deafening clamor. 

The thought *Gwendolen is free* rang like a discordant iron bell against the immovable, dominant reality of *Gwendolen rich, exalted, courted*. He despised the selfish, grating noise of his own egoistic imagination. It was an untranslatable subjective agony—the lingering poison of a passionate, clinging love that had fundamentally reorganized his inner life. He had spent eighteen months cultivating a quiet agrarian peace in his soul, learning to count the remaining treasures of his life and tasting the bitter but wholesome bread of acceptance, only for three words to tear through his resolve like a mechanized thresher. He recognized the meanness of his hopes; to harbor them now was to let a selfish, predatory lexicon overwrite his moral integrity. 

Striding firmly to silence the internal cacophony, Rex entered the house and pushed through the open door of the study. The familiar, comforting scent of worn leather and sealing wax enveloped him as he saw his father packing a traveling-desk.

“Can I be of any use, sir?” Rex asked, his voice deliberately rejoining the steady, harmonic rhythm of the household.

“Yes, my boy; when I’m gone, just see to my letters,” his father said. 

Rex helped strap the heavy woven railway rug. “You will perhaps bring my cousin back to England?” he forced himself to ask, breaking his long silence regarding her name.

“That depends,” the rector said with his usual pragmatic confidence, stepping smoothly into his role as familial caretaker. “This telegram leaves us in ignorance. But no doubt the arrangements of the will lately made are satisfactory. I feel confident that Gwendolen will be liberally—I should expect, splendidly—provided for.”

“It must have been a great shock for her,” Rex said, recovering his footing. “I suppose he was a devoted husband.”

“No doubt of it,” said the rector.

Rex knew nothing of Gwendolen’s flight to Leubronn or the intricate, unspoken power dynamics of her courtship. Grandcourt had provided handsomely for the Davilows—a utilitarian transaction that Rex himself would have gladly made, had he the means. Yet, as Rex stood in the quiet study, he wondered with a deep, visceral ache whether Gwendolen had ever truly loved the successful suitor, or if her entire marriage had been a tragic failure of translation, an experience endured in the sterile absence of true sensory and emotional sustenance.

Sir Hugo Mallinger was not so prompt in starting for Genoa as Mr. Gascoigne had been, leaving Daniel Deronda to wait for five days in the resonant, salt-heavy air of the Mediterranean coast. Deronda would not have taken his departure until he had seen the baronet; there was not only Grandcourt’s death by drowning to discuss, but the seismic rupture in his own history. In letters, he had found the dominant English lexicon entirely insufficient to capture the apparition of his mother—a woman who had come and gone like a ghost, leaving behind an untranslatable sorrow. To attempt to distill her tragic, fragmented history into the polite syntax of the British upper class would have been a betrayal of the deep, ancestral rhythms he was only just beginning to comprehend.

On the fifth evening, Deronda waited at the railway station. To ground himself, he had spent the afternoon walking the labyrinthine *caruggi* of the old city, finding a profound psychological anchor in the acoustic ecology of the Genoese kitchens. He had stood outside a humble *trattoria*, listening to the rhythmic, ancient *thwack-thwack* of a wooden pestle crushing bright, peppery basil, coarse sea salt, and sharp garlic against the sides of a marble mortar. The olfactory landscape—the sharp, grassy tang of cold-pressed Ligurian olive oil mingling with the warm, yeasty exhalations of focaccia baking in wood-fired stone ovens—spoke to a continuity of generations, a mythic cycle of sustenance seamlessly integrated into everyday domesticity. He felt a deep, sensory gratitude for these meticulous culinary traditions. They stood in stark contrast to his own upbringing, where meals at the Abbey, however grand, were often emotionally sterile, governed by the rigid, utilitarian power dynamics of aristocratic inheritance rather than the passionate preservation of cultural memory. 

When the train finally arrived, the shrieking of iron wheels against the tracks shattered the coastal harmony, a violent industrial intrusion that grated against Deronda’s newly sensitized nerves. From this dissonance emerged Sir Hugo’s familiar figure. Despite the tragic circumstances, Deronda felt a surge of lifelong affection that smoothed away the sharp edges of his resentment over the older man’s well-intentioned but profoundly damaging deception regarding his birth. 

“Well, Dan,” said Sir Hugo with serious fervor, grasping Deronda’s hand tightly. He uttered no other words of greeting; the unspoken rush of mutual consciousness hung in the air between them, vibrating over the hiss of the steam engine.

They gave their orders to the courier and opted to walk slowly toward the Hotel Italia under the faint, starlit sheen of the lingering day. 

“I have taken my journey easily, and am in excellent condition,” Sir Hugo said, his boots striking a steady, confident beat against the cobblestones. “I didn’t hurry in setting off, because I wanted to inquire into things a little, and so I got sight of your letter to Lady Mallinger before I started. But now, how is the widow?”

“Getting calmer,” Deronda replied, his voice pitched low to harmonize with the distant, rhythmic lapping of the harbor waves. “She seems to be escaping the bodily illness that one might have feared for her, after her plunge and terrible excitement. Her uncle and mother came two days ago, and she is being well taken care of.”

“Any prospect of an heir being born?” 

“From what Mr. Gascoigne said to me, I conclude not. He spoke as if it were a question whether the widow would have the estates for her life.”

“It will not be much of a wrench to her affections, I fancy, this loss of the husband?” Sir Hugo looked round at Deronda, treating the emotional trauma with the same breezy palate he might use to assess a passing vintage. 

“The suddenness of the death has been a great blow to her,” Deronda said, quietly evading the crude reduction of Gwendolen’s complex suffering.

As they walked past an open-air market, the scent of bitter herbs and slow-rendering pancetta drifted over them. “I wonder whether Grandcourt gave her any notion what were the provisions of his will?” Sir Hugo pressed.

“Do you know what they are, sir?” parried Deronda.

“Yes, I do,” the baronet said quickly, his tone adopting the crisp, transactional cadence of the dominant lexicon of property. “Gad! If there is no prospect of a legitimate heir, he has left everything to a boy he had by a Mrs. Glasher; you know nothing about the affair, I suppose, but she was a sort of wife to him for a good many years, and there are three older children—girls. The boy is to take his father’s name; he is Henleigh already, and he is to be Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt. The Mallinger will be of no use to him, I am happy to say; but the young dog will have more than enough with his fourteen years’ minority—no need to have had holes filled up with my fifty thousand for Diplow that he had no right to: and meanwhile my beauty, the young widow, is to put up with a poor two thousand a year and the house at Gadsmere.”

Sir Hugo scoffed, oblivious to the discordant cruelty of the arrangement. “A nice kind of banishment for her if she chose to shut herself up there, which I don’t think she will. The boy’s mother has been living there of late years. I’m perfectly disgusted with Grandcourt. I don’t know that I’m obliged to think the better of him because he’s drowned, though, so far as my affairs are concerned, nothing in his life became him like the leaving it.”

To Deronda, who viewed the nurturing of others through sustenance and care as the highest human calling, Grandcourt’s will was a rancid, poisoned dish, meticulously prepared and left behind to deliberately starve his widow. “In my opinion he did wrong when he married this wife—not in leaving his estates to the son,” Deronda said dryly.

“I say nothing against his leaving the land to the lad,” Sir Hugo argued, blind to the spiritual malnutrition of the situation. “But since he had married this girl he ought to have given her a handsome provision, such as she could live on in a style fitted to the rank he had raised her to. She ought to have had four or five thousand a year and the London house for her life; that’s what I should have done for her. I suppose, as she was penniless, her friends couldn’t stand out for a settlement, else it’s ill trusting to the will a man may make after he’s married. Even a wise man generally lets some folly ooze out of him in his will—my father did, I know; and if a fellow has any spite or tyranny in him, he’s likely to bottle off a good deal for keeping in that sort of document. It’s quite clear Grandcourt meant that his death should put an extinguisher on his wife, if she bore him no heir.”

“And, in the other case, I suppose everything would have been reversed—illegitimacy would have had the extinguisher?” said Deronda, with a scorn that tasted like ash on his tongue.

“Precisely—Gadsmere and the two thousand. It’s queer. One nuisance is that Grandcourt has made me an executor; but seeing he was the son of my only brother, I can’t refuse to act. And I shall mind it less if I can be of any use to the widow. Lush thinks she was not in ignorance about the family under the rose, and the purport of the will. But I fancy you are the man who knew most about what Mrs. Grandcourt felt or did not feel—eh, Dan?” 

Sir Hugo lowered his tone of interested inquiry, a shift in the acoustic environment that told Deronda any evasion would be misinterpreted. “She was certainly not happy,” Deronda answered gravely, striving to honor the untranslatability of Gwendolen’s subjective terror. “They were unsuited to each other. But as to the disposal of the property—from all I have seen of her, I should predict that she will be quite contented with it.”

“Then she is not much like the rest of her sex; that’s all I can say,” Sir Hugo said with a slight shrug. “However, she ought to be something extraordinary, for there must be an entanglement between your horoscope and hers—eh? When that tremendous telegram came, the first thing Lady Mallinger said was, ‘How very strange that it should be Daniel who sends it!’ But I have had something of the same sort in my own life. I was once at a foreign hotel where a lady had been left by her husband without money. When I heard of it, and came forward to help her, who should she be but an early flame of mine, who had been fool enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and short affection? But it was an affair of my own that called me there—nothing to do with knight-errantry, any more than you coming to Genoa had to do with the Grandcourts.”

There was a profound silence between them, filled only by the rhythmic respiration of the sea against the ancient port. Sir Hugo had used the Grandcourts as an appetizer, a less difficult subject to delay the inevitable main course of their discourse: the events that touched their own relationship. 

Just when they were about to lose sight of the water, the baronet turned. Pausing as if to digest the final view, he spoke in a tone of uncharacteristic gravity. “And about the main business of your coming to Genoa, Dan? You have not been deeply pained by anything you have learned, I hope? There is nothing that you feel need change your position in any way? You know, whatever happens to you must always be of importance to me.”

Deronda paused. The scent of roasting chestnuts and woodsmoke enveloped them, a smell that spoke of earth, of harvesting, of generations bound to the soil and their seasons. He felt the immense weight of the cultural memory he had just unearthed, a native dialect of the soul that had been nearly starved out of him by the dominant English lexicon of his upbringing. 

“I desire to meet your goodness by perfect confidence, sir,” Deronda said, his voice resonant and steady, seeking a harmonious space within the night’s acoustic ecology. “But I can’t answer those questions truly by a simple yes or no. Much that I have heard about the past has pained me. And it has been a pain to meet and part with my mother in her suffering state, as I have been compelled to do. But it is no pain—it is rather a clearing up of doubts for which I am thankful, to know my parentage. As to the effect on my position, there will be no change in my gratitude to you, sir, for the fatherly care and affection you have always shown me. But to know that I was born a Jew, may have a momentous influence on my life, which I am hardly able to tell you of at present.”

The words left his mouth and altered the very atmosphere, a reclamation of an eroded identity. He felt the profound significance of his bloodline, a genealogy rich with ancient dietary laws, meticulous historians, and spiritual sustenance that stretched back through interwoven timelines to the very origins of myth.

Sir Hugo gave him a quick glance, his comfortable worldview disrupted, and turned to walk on. The silence stretched, the baronet mentally processing this sudden injection of an alien cultural flavor into his carefully curated English life. 

“I have long expected something remarkable from you, Dan,” Sir Hugo finally said, his voice tightening, trying to assert the dominance of his societal norms. “But, for God’s sake, don’t go into any eccentricities! I can tolerate any man’s difference of opinion, but let him tell it me without getting himself up as a lunatic. At this stage of the world, if a man wants to be taken seriously, he must keep clear of melodrama.” 

To Sir Hugo, Deronda’s deep, ancestral awakening was merely noise pollution, a theatrical disruption of the refined societal hum. “Don’t misunderstand me,” the baronet continued. “I am not suspecting you of setting up any lunacy on your own account. I only think you might easily be led arm in arm with a lunatic, especially if he wanted defending. You have a passion for people who are pelted, Dan. I’m sorry for them too; but so far as company goes, it’s a bad ground of selection. However, I don’t ask you to anticipate your inclination in anything you have to tell me. When you make up your mind to a course that requires money, I have some sixteen thousand pounds that have been accumulating for you over and above what you have been having the interest of as income. And now I am come, I suppose you want to get back to England as soon as you can?”

“I must go first to Mainz to get away a chest of my grandfather’s, and perhaps to see a friend of his,” Deronda replied. His grandfather—a man who had undoubtedly viewed the preservation of their Hebrew texts and traditions with the same sacred, meticulous care a master chef views the safeguarding of a centuries-old mother dough. “Although the chest has been lying there these twenty years, I have an unreasonable sort of nervous eagerness to get it away under my care, as if it were more likely now than before that something might happen to it. And perhaps I am the more uneasy, because I lingered after my mother left, instead of setting out immediately. Yet I can’t regret that I was here—else Mrs. Grandcourt would have had none but servants to act for her.”

“Yes, yes,” said Sir Hugo, with a flippancy that attempted to mask a profound vexation, his tone entirely out of key with the sacred nature of Deronda’s familial quest. “I hope you are not going to set a dead Jew above a living Christian.”

Deronda colored vividly, the crude syllables striking his ear like the discordant clatter of dropped tin pans. He repressed a retort, swallowing the bitterness whole. In silence, beneath the ancient, interwoven scent of the sea and the supper fires, they turned into the *Italia*.

The Roman Emperor in the legend put to death ten learned Israelites to avenge the sale of Joseph by his brethren, a mythic brutality that has always simmered beneath the surface of everyday life. And there have always been enough of his kidney, whose piety lies in punishing, who can taste the sharp, metallic tang of grudges but remain wholly insensible to the sustaining bread of gratitude. For you shall never convince the stronger feeling that it hath not the stronger reason, or incline him who treats meals solely as utilitarian fuel to believe there is profound ground for a feast of love. As we may learn from the ancient, slow-cooked order of word-making, wherein the native dialect of *love* precedeth the dominant lexicon’s *lovable*. 

When Deronda presented his letter at the banking-house in the *Schuster Strasse* at Mainz, he stepped off a thoroughfare vibrating with the shrill, grating clatter of modern industrial noise pollution—a sonic assault of iron wheels and commerce that fractured the mind. He was presently shown into an inner room where the acoustic ecology shifted instantly into something sacred and domestic. The air was anchored by the rhythmic, low-frequency bubbling of a rich marrow broth simmering on a small iron stove, its sound a healing poultice to the ears. Seated at a heavy oak table, arranging open letters amidst small dishes of dried coriander and coarse salt, was Joseph Kalonymos, the white-bearded traditional agrarian elder whom Deronda had seen the year before in the synagogue at Frankfort. 

He wore his hat—it seemed to be the same old felt hat as before, carrying the faint olfactory memory of woodsmoke and rain—and near him was a packed portmanteau with a wrap and overcoat upon it. On seeing Deronda enter, he rose, but did not advance or put out his hand. Looking at him with small penetrating eyes which glittered like black gems in the midst of his yellowish face, he said in German, “Good! It is now you who seek me, young man.”

“Yes; I seek you with gratitude, as a friend of my grandfather’s,” said Deronda, “and I am under an obligation to you for giving yourself much trouble on my account.” He spoke without difficulty in that liberal German tongue, though he felt the subtle power dynamics embedded in the dominant lexicon, a sterile language that threatened to swallow the nuanced, untranslatable phonemes of his ancestors’ native dialects. 

Kalonymos now put out his hand and said cordially, “So you are no longer angry at being something more than an Englishman?”

“On the contrary. I thank you heartily for helping to save me from remaining in ignorance of my parentage, and for taking care of the chest that my grandfather left in trust for me.”

“Sit down, sit down,” said Kalonymos, in a quick undertone, seating himself again, and pointing to a chair near him. Deliberately laying aside his hat and showing a head thickly covered with white hair, he stroked and clutched his beard. The room was steeped in an olfactory landscape of toasted caraway, ancient paper, and the deep, roasted warmth of the simmering broth—a sensory space where mythic history and domestic sustenance seamlessly intertwined. 

The moment wrought strongly on Deronda’s imaginative susceptibility. In the presence of one linked still in zealous friendship with the grandfather whose hope had yearned toward him when he was unborn, Deronda seemed to himself to be touching the electric chain of his own ancestry. It was a visceral, almost gustatory connection. Impossible for men of duller fibre—men who lack sensory appreciation or view their daily bread merely as fuel—to comprehend this sensibility of Deronda’s. It gave his face an expression which seemed very satisfactory to the observer.

Kalonymos spoke in Hebrew, the ancient, guttural cadences vibrating perfectly within the room's quiet acoustics, quoting from the liturgy: “As thy goodness has been great to the former generations, even so may it be to the latter.” The inherent untranslatability of the subjective experience conveyed in those ancient syllables washed over Deronda. 

Then after pausing, the elder began, “Young man, I rejoice that I was not yet set off again on my travels, and that you are come in time for me to see the image of my friend as he was in his youth—no longer perverted from the fellowship of your people—no longer shrinking in proud wrath from the touch of him who seemed to be claiming you as a Jew. You come with a willing soul to declare, ‘I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.’ Is it not so?”

“Assuredly it is,” said Deronda. “But let me say that I should at no time have been inclined to treat a Jew with incivility simply because he was a Jew. You can understand that I shrank from saying to a stranger, ‘I know nothing of my mother.’”

“A sin, a sin!” said Kalonymos, putting up his hand and closing his eyes in disgust. “A robbery of our people—like the stripping of our native orchards for the Roman Edom. But it is frustrated. I have frustrated it. When Daniel Charisi—may his Rock and his Redeemer guard him!—when Daniel Charisi was a stripling and I was a lad little above his shoulder, we made a solemn vow always to be friends. It was his bent from first to last to fortify his soul with bonds. It was a saying of his, ‘Let us bind love with duty; for duty is the love of law; and law is the nature of the Eternal.’ When he was dead, they sought to rob him; but they could not rob him of me. I rescued that remainder of him which he had prized and preserved for his offspring. I will bring you the chest forthwith.”

Kalonymos left the room for a few minutes, returning with a clerk who carried the chest, set it down on the floor, drew off a leather cover, and went out. It was not very large, but was made heavy by ornamental bracers and handles of gilt iron. The wood was beautifully incised with Arabic lettering, exuding a profound olfactory richness of aged cedar, dried myrrh, and the lingering spice of centuries of trans-Mediterranean migrations.

“So!” said Kalonymos, returning to his seat and the rhythmic, comforting scrape of his mortar and pestle. “And here is the curious key. Bestow it carefully. I trust you are methodic and wary.” 

“I shall be more careful of this than of any other property,” said Deronda, putting the key in his breast-pocket, feeling the heavy, tangible proof of his cultural memory against his heart. “Have you time to tell me more of my grandfather? Or shall I be trespassing in staying longer?”

“Stay yet a while. In an hour and eighteen minutes I start for Trieste,” said Kalonymos, checking his watch. “And presently my sons will expect my attention. They dwell here in ease and luxury, though I choose to be a wanderer.”

“I shall be glad if you will commend me to their acquaintance for some future opportunity,” said Deronda. “There are pressing claims calling me to England... But to know more of you and your family would be motive enough to bring me again to Mainz.”

“Good! Me you will hardly find, for I am beyond my threescore years and ten, and I am a wanderer, carrying my shroud with me. The days are changed for us since Karl the Great fetched my ancestors from Italy to bring some tincture of knowledge—of seed-saving, of medicine, of true sustenance—to our rough German brethren. Our youth fell on evil days; but this we have won; the learning of all Germany is fed and fattened by Jewish brains—though they keep not always their Jewish hearts. Have you been left altogether ignorant of your people’s life, young man?”

“No,” said Deronda. “I have lately been led to study everything belonging to their history. It turns out that I have been making myself ready to understand my grandfather a little.” 

“So—you would perhaps have been such a man as he if your education had not hindered. You are like him in features: yet not altogether. He had an iron will in his face. What he despised most was indifference.”

“Yet his knowledge was not narrow?” said Deronda.

“Narrow? No,” Kalonymos smiled compassionately, stirring the deep, earthen pot, the aroma of root vegetables and bone marrow rising into the air. “From his childhood upward, he drank in learning as easily as the plant sucks up water. He early took to medicine, becoming a meticulous food historian and physician, observing the theories of life and health through the sustenance of our ancestors. He traveled to many countries, tracing the sourcing of our native ingredients, spending much of his substance in seeing and knowing. What he used to insist on was that the strength and wealth of mankind depended on the balance of separateness and communication. He was bitterly against our people losing themselves among the Gentiles; ‘It’s no better,’ said he, ‘than the many sorts of heirloom grain going back from their rich variety into the sterile sameness of a commercial monoculture.’ He mingled all sorts of learning, like a master chef balancing the bitter and the sweet. Though we were bosom friends, we were as different as the inside and outside of the mixing bowl. I stood up for two notions of my own: I took Charisi’s sayings as I took the shape of the ancient olive trees: they were there, not to be disputed about. Charisi thought continually of our people’s future, of preserving our cultural memory from the erosion of assimilation. I, not. When I am in the East, I lie much on deck and listen to the sea, watching the greater stars. The sight and sound of them satisfies me. Charisi was satisfied with no sight, but pieced it out with what had been before and what would come after. Yet we loved each other. I have fulfilled my pledge.”

Deronda rose. “And in being faithful to him you have caused justice to be done to me. I thank you with my whole soul.”

“Be worthy of him, young man. What is your vocation?” 

This question, put with quick abruptness, embarrassed Deronda, who did not feel it honest to allege his law-reading as a vocation when his soul hungered for something of profound, tactile meaning. “I cannot say that I have any.”

“Get one, get one. The Jew must be diligent. You will call yourself a Jew and profess the faith of your fathers?” Kalonymos put his hand on Deronda’s shoulder, looking sharply into his face.

“I shall call myself a Jew,” said Deronda, deliberately. “But I will not say that I shall profess to believe exactly as my fathers have believed. Our fathers themselves changed the horizon of their belief and learned the agrarian and spiritual secrets of other races. But I think I can maintain my grandfather’s notion of separateness with communication. I hold that my first duty is to my own people, and if there is anything to be done toward restoring or perfecting their common life, protecting the rich variety of our cultural harvest from fading into silence, I shall make that my vocation.”

It happened to Deronda at that moment, as it has often happened to others, that the need for speech made an epoch in resolve. By the necessity to answer, he found out the truth for himself, grounding his identity not in abstract philosophy, but in the living, sensory continuation of a lineage.

“Ah, you argue and you look forward—you are Daniel Charisi’s grandson,” said Kalonymos, adding a benediction in Hebrew, its resonant vowels filling the room like the lingering notes of an ancient song. With that they parted; and almost as soon as Deronda was in London, the aged agrarian wanderer was again on shipboard, wrapped in the enveloping acoustic ecology of the waves, greeting the friendly stars without any eager curiosity.

The white house at Pennicote had not been the only dwelling to feel the tremor of sudden news, nor was Rex Gascoigne the sole heart violently agitated by its arrival. In the quiet, aromatic parlor of the Meyrick home, a delicate acoustic ecology was meticulously maintained, serving as a vital sanctuary against the relentless, abrasive industrial noise pollution of the London streets. Here, the sonic environment was therapeutic, woven from the gentle, rhythmic scrape of Mrs. Meyrick’s mortar and pestle as she ground dried lavender and coriander seeds for her historical herbal infusions, and the melodic, vibrating cadence of Mirah teaching Mab to sing *Lascia ch’io pianga*. 

But on this particular Wednesday, this fragile harmonic tapestry was violently ruptured. Hans Meyrick entered with his latch-key and appeared in the parlor, aggressively shaking the *Times* aloft. The violently crackling broadsheet sounded like the snapping of dry timber in a blazing forest, a crass, commercialized clamor that remorselessly interrupted the purity of Mab’s notes and Mirah’s accompaniment. Piano and song ceased immediately. To Mirah, the harsh friction of the industrialized paper was a thunderous violation of their shared sonic refuge. 

“O-o-o, Hans!” Mab complained, her voice tight with the dissonance. “Why do you bring a more horrible noise than my singing?”

“What on earth is the wonderful news?” asked Mrs. Meyrick, pausing her culinary preservation. “Anything about Italy—anything about the Austrians giving up Venice?”

“Nothing about Italy, but something from Italy,” Hans replied. He spoke in the glib, superficial lexicon of dominant society, delivering tidings that should have carried the heavy, mythic weight of ancestral tide-lore, but which he reduced to cheap, serialized gossip. 

“Nothing bad?” Mrs. Meyrick asked anxiously, her thoughts immediately turning to Deronda, while Mirah’s heart was seized by the exact same dread.

“Not bad for anybody we care much about,” Hans said quickly. “Rather uncommonly lucky, I think. I never knew anybody die conveniently before.” 

“Oh me, Hans!” Mab said impatiently. “What *is* it that has happened?”

Hans flattened the newspaper upon the table, pointing a finger at a sterile paragraph of print. “Duke Alfonso is drowned, and the Duchess is alive, that’s all. But more than all is—Deronda was at Genoa in the same hotel, and he saw her brought in by the fishermen time enough to save her from any harm. It seems they saw her jump in after her husband... However, Deronda is a lucky fellow in being there to take care of her.”

Mirah sank back onto the music stool, her eyelids dropping, her hands tightly clasped. 

“Poor thing!” said Mrs. Meyrick, turning back to the sharp, bitter scent of her crushed herbs. “She must have been fond of her husband to jump in after him.”

“It was an inadvertence—a little absence of mind,” Hans said, creasing his face mischievously. He began to employ the power dynamics of his dominant, flippant vocabulary to overwrite the profound untranslatability of human grief. “Who can be fond of a jealous baritone, with freezing glances, always singing asides? Nothing can be neater than his getting drowned. The Duchess is at liberty now to marry a man with a fine head of hair, and glances that will melt instead of freezing her. And I shall be invited to the wedding.”

Mirah started from her posture. A sweeping, multi-generational sorrow seemed to rise within her, clashing violently with Hans's shallow commercialization of life and death. Fixing her eyes on him with an angry gleam, she defended her internal, ancestral dialect against his invasive phrasing. “Mr. Hans, you ought not to speak in that way,” she said in a deeply-shaken voice. “Mr. Deronda would not like you to speak so. Why will you say he is lucky—why will you use words of that sort about life and death—when what is life to one is death to another? How do you know it would be lucky if he loved Mrs. Grandcourt? It might be a great evil to him. She would take him away from my brother. Mr. Deronda would not call that lucky to pierce my brother’s heart.”

The sudden transformation struck them all. Mirah’s face was pale, her usually rich, ripe-berry tint drained away, leaving an expression that might have suited an avenging angel. Hans sat transfixed, blushing like a girl under the weight of her profound sincerity. “I am a fool and a brute, and I withdraw every word,” he muttered nervously. “I’ll go and hang myself like Judas—if it’s allowable to mention him.”

Even in his penitence, Hans could not escape the flippant drollery of his native tongue. But Mirah’s anger was not appeased. She had burst into indignant speech as creatures in intense pain bite through their own flesh to make the agony bearable—a desperate attempt to articulate an untranslatable subjective experience. She said no more, turning back to the piano and pressing the sheet music before her.

“Mirah is quite right to scold you, Hans,” Mab said, her voice dripping with scorn for his utilitarian view of marriage. “Men’s minds must be very black, I think.”

“We had better go on, Mab,” Mirah said, her tone elevated, seeking to rebuild their shattered acoustic sanctuary. “Will you sing this again, or shall I sing it to you?”

“Oh, please sing it to me,” Mab said, eager to let the music wash away the ugly intrusion.

Mirah sang *Lascia ch’io pianga*, giving forth its melodious sobs and cries with new fullness and energy, pouring the raw, physical sensation of her heartache into the vibrating chords. Hans leaned against the mantelpiece, his eyes averted. When the final note faded into the olfactory warmth of the lavender-scented room, Mirah rose. “I must go home now. Ezra expects me.”

Mrs. Meyrick drew Mirah’s face down to hers, offering a motherly blessing. As Mirah departed, she felt a pang of compunction, fearing her outburst had been a proud ingratitude. Hans insisted on walking her home. They stepped out into the chaotic, industrial roar of the city, their companionship a silent, agitated communication, like two chords whose quick vibrations lie just outside the threshold of human hearing. Hans’s mind fluttered toward mischief and romance, trapped in a worldview that categorized Mirah’s Jewish heritage and Deronda’s identity into rigid, historical tropes, while Mirah remained isolated within the impenetrable fortress of her own feelings. At her door, she offered a melancholy gentleness, inviting him in to see her brother, which Hans eagerly interpreted as a sign of pardon.
